Yahoo becomes first licensee of ‘Spotify-like’ collective AI content licensing model

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

AI content licensing has largely comprised of one-off deals between publishers and tech companies. RSL, which is being renamed The Internet Licensing Collective (ILC), is pursuing a collective model instead, with Yahoo as its first licensee.

The deal means publishers of all sizes can license their content for use in Yahoo’s AI-powered search engine Scout and get paid based on how that content is used.

Digiday spoke with ILC co-founder Doug Leeds about how this model works and how publishers can earn revenue.

A licensee like Yahoo pays a blanket licensing fee for access to content in the collective. Leeds declined to say how much Yahoo was paying, but the fee is tied to the “demand-side revenue.”  Yahoo declined to be interviewed for this story.

As a licensee’s business scales, the amount available for publisher compensation can grow as well.

“It allows businesses that are making a lot of money off of the content to keep that content in place and sustainable. But it also allows for entrants,” Leeds said. “As the revenue scales, the percentage scales. So more money comes out. But that also means that entrants can come in, and there can be new use cases and new players and new products, and all of that’s important to the evolution of what we’re doing to the market.”

A new report out today from publishing analytics firm Mather Economics estimated that collective licensing for AI could generate publisher compensation equal to 15% to 35% of AI company revenue, a potential market that in three years could surpass the U.S. display advertising market. The analysis was based on the current annual revenue from the two biggest frontier models, OpenAI and Anthropic, and an economic model created by Mather Economics incorporating AI training-data scaling laws.

Leeds likened the licensing collective to a Spotify-like model.

“It’s kind of like Spotify, but also a song is a song is a song, and content is very different. And so we had to come up with these rules that made sense specifically for digital content,” Leeds said.

Leeds said ILC worked closely with its publisher collective to figure out those rules. The first step was to define a “segmented royalty pool.” This separates licensing revenue by AI company, rather than combining payments into a single fund shared by all participating publishers. That means publishers only receive a share of the revenue generated by AI products that actually use their content.

More AI and tech companies are testing pay-per-value licensing models. Digiday has previously reported that Google and Microsoft are testing and developing these models, which compensate publishers when their content meaningfully contributes to AI-generated responses, rather than paying based on how frequently their content is used. It’s a shift from flat-fee licensing deals to more dynamic pricing, based on the value publishers’ content delivers to AI companies.

When an AI company signs a licensing agreement with ILC, that money goes into its own royalty pool. Rather than combining all these payments into one enormous fund and distributing them across participating publishers, ILC keeps the royalty pools separate.

This protects specialized publishers from having their revenue diluted, Leeds explained. A general-purpose AI search engine like Yahoo Scout might use large quantities of content from hundreds or thousands of publishers, but a specialized healthcare AI product might use a much smaller amount of content from a handful of medical-focused publishers, and that specialized content could be considerably more valuable to the healthcare company, he said.

ILC wanted to ensure that a publisher’s compensation reflects the value of the AI product using its content, not simply how much content it contributes to the overall licensing ecosystem.

“An AI vertical in health might be very low volume, but much higher specific value. So if your content gets used in that, you should split that high value, not get diluted by all the content that got used by Yahoo,” Leeds said.

Over 3,000 publishers are now part of the ILC, he noted.

The payments are determined by what Leeds called “allocation rules.” These take into account how much content is used, like grounding, retrieval or displayed in an AI response, and whether the publisher is credited or cited as a source.

For example, an AI product that uses a publisher’s content to check a fact might receive a lower allocation than one that displays detailed election results and explicitly credits the Associated Press, Leeds said. He did not disclose the exact formula used to calculate each publisher’s share.

The allocation rules are intended to discourage AI companies from choosing content simply because it’s cheaper, and instead incentivize them to use what they consider the highest-quality sources, he said.

Publishers can also decide who they want to license to. Right now, their only option is Yahoo. But as more licensees sign up, they can go into their ILC portal and select which AI companies they do and don’t want to license their content to. For example, if a publisher signs a separate content licensing deal with an AI company, they can take their content out of the collective. In the portal, they can also see what their content is being used for, such as RAG or training.

RSL, which stood for the Really Simple Licensing (RSL), was two things: a group of publishers working on a collective licensing agreement, and the organization behind the RSL Protocol, a standard that publishers can use in robots.txt to describe in machine-readable language what their terms of service are. Publishers can ask AI companies for a pay-per-crawl or pay-per-inference fee through the RSL Protocol, for example. “Thousands” are currently using this standard, Leeds said.

But Leeds wanted a new name for the non-profit organization that reflected the publisher collective, to make it clear that this is a licensing model that any publisher can join, he said.

Leeds said ILC is currently in talks with “some” of the major AI companies interested in the licensing collective. He declined to name which ones.

“You can do a deal with a one-off, and that’s fantastic. But that doesn’t invest in the infrastructure of content in general,” he said.

ROOM’s Designers on Design Series Returns with A Story of Hospitality and Human Connection

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Who understands the struggles and successes of a designer better than another designer? That’s the core concept behind ROOM’s Designers on Design video series, which pairs leading designers together for open conversation on the future of architecture, design, and human connection.

“When we launched ROOM’s Designers on Design series, the idea was simple: create a space for designers to have the kinds of conversations that don’t always happen in the day-to-day,” says Elyse Heckman, SVP, Brand & Communications at ROOM. “No interviewer, no moderator, no prescribed talking points. Just two creative minds coming together to exchange ideas, challenge perspectives, and explore what moves the industry forward.”

Hospitality legend Anda Andrei of Anda Andrei Design and award-winning designer Matthew Goodrich of Goodrich design studio sit down for a discussion in the most recent installation. Unlike earlier episodes of this series, Andrei and Goodrich know each other outside of the studio—and have for years. Goodrich attributes his love of hospitality design to Andrei’s hotels, while Andrei has had a front-row seat to the evolution of Goodrich’s career. (Plus, their work shares a certain symbiosis: Andrei’s recent renovation of the Musée Yves Saint Laurent Paris aligns with Goodrich’s past in museum exhibition design.)

“For this episode, we wanted to take a slightly different approach to our previous pairings,” says Heckman. “Rather than bringing together designers who may be meeting for the first time, we were drawn to the idea of connecting two individuals who already share a history and familiarity with each other’s work. We loved the opportunity to explore how those relationships shape our perspectives, influence our creative instincts and ultimately help us grow as designers.”

Photography by Eric Vitale, courtesy of ROOM.

Tapping into the spirit of authentic exchange that ROOM set out to foster, the duo’s conversation begins with their first (almost) meeting. In 2003, Goodrich was helping his partner, Barnes Foundation Executive Director Thomas Collins, open Zaha Hadid’s Contemporary Arts Center in Cincinnati when he heard that Andrei would be there in 20 minutes. “I looked to Thom, and I said, ‘You’ve got to go home, take a shower, and put on a suit. This is a big deal,’” Goodrich shares.

However, the pair didn’t officially meet until years later—after Andrei had left the Ian Schrager Company, starting her own firm at 59 years old. “You came very highly recommended, and I wanted to hire you,” she says. “But then I asked you a crucial question: ‘Have you ever opened a hotel before?’” “And I had to say no,” Goodrich adds.

Designing a hotel is no easy feat, which Goodrich learned when opening his first hotel, Arlo SoHo, in 2016. He shared how the final month before opening was chaos. With nearly 40 years shaping hospitality projects, Andrei knows this reality well—but emphasizes that it’s even more painful when operations start changing after the design is finished. Like living organisms, hospitality projects evolve with the staff, users, and guests over the years.

Still, the pair agree that all these issues and pain points provide valuable lessons. “Understanding how operations affect the success of restaurants and of hotels is foundational for making great designs,” Goodrich says.

Photography by Eric Vitale, courtesy of ROOM.

On the topic of design inspiration, Andrei believe the best hotels have a story. Much like an elevator pitch, she believes projects should have a one-sentence design concept. For example, at the Delano Miami Beach Hotel, which she created in collaboration with Philippe Starck, the one-liner was: “a Greek fisherman that finds a diamond in the sea.” Goodrich agrees with the sentiment of this design tool: “If you have that short phrase, if you can go back to the essence of what your concept is, things fall into place.”

Throughout the episode, there is a sense of mutual respect and deep appreciation for the other’s work. Goodrich calls Andrei a superhero collaborator, while Andrei compliments Goodrich’s work (and name). Both value collaboration, imagination, and challenging work. Heckman sees the pair’s connection as proof of the series’ larger purpose: “It’s a wonderful example of how seemingly different creative paths can intersect, and how the work we encounter early in our careers can continue to influence us in unexpected ways.”

“When you started your company, we had some empty desks in my office and we shared offices. And who knew that nine and a half years later, we would be sitting here and talking about how amazing you did,” Andrei says with a laugh.

This is what ROOM’s Designers on Design series does so well: It brings design to the human level. From the ideas and experiences that shape the work to the friends, colleagues, and mentors who leave a lasting impact, each episode looks at the people behind the portfolio.

“What makes this episode especially compelling is that the conversation extends beyond design itself,” shares Heckman. “It gets to the heart of how we become the designers we are — the people who influence us, the experiences that shape our creative instincts, and the ways inspiration gets passed from one generation to the next. In that sense, it embodies exactly what we hoped the series would become: a platform for authentic exchange, unexpected perspectives and the stories behind the work.”

And who knows? The series might even inspire new work of its own. “I want to do a project together,” Andrei says. Goodrich answers: “I know, me too. That’s my dream.”

Exclusive: Menlo IRIS extension gives security teams control over AI use without a new browser

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Browser security company Menlo Security Inc. today launched the Menlo IRIS Browser Extension, a new offering that adds its enterprise browser security controls to the browsers employees already use with no need to switch them to separate secure ones.

Delivered as a centrally managed extension, Menlo IRIS is said to give security teams visibility into and control over generative artificial intelligence use, data movement, files and third-party extensions. Installation is claimed to take only hours, as no additional hardware is needed.

Chief Executive Bill Robbins, who took over the top job from co-founder Amir Ben-Efraim in February, said security teams have known for some time that the browser is a blind spot. Many have nonetheless held off on fixing it to avoid replacing an existing security stack, which typically means a big migration project, he added.

“Menlo IRIS removes those obstacles,” Robbins said. “Even a small team can get essential security running quickly on the browsers employees already use.”

To make its case, Menlo pointed to Verizon Communications Inc.’s 2026 Data Breach Investigations Report, released in May, which found regular AI use on corporate devices tripled from 15% to 45% of employees in a single year. Some 67% of people accessing AI services on corporate devices were found to be using noncorporate accounts, with more than 15% of users at the average company also running unauthorized AI browser extensions.

Among the headline capabilities is an audit of third-party browser extensions. Menlo says the audit covers every extension installed across a company, with an enterprise-wide inventory, real-time risk profiling and management controls all included. Few security teams have a count of the extensions their users have installed, according to Menlo. The company also warned that an extension’s risk profile can change at any time without notice.

Data-loss controls cover the clipboard, printing, uploads and downloads, with watermarking, read-only pages, restrictions on developer tools and screenshot deterrence also included. Web filtering steers users toward sanctioned tools, including generative AI services, with limits on what data can be entered into them offered as an alternative to banning the tools outright.

File security is part of the package as well, with inline inspection for data loss prevention violations, viruses and malware. For customers that want it, content disarm and reconstruction can be switched on to strip malicious content from files so they remain usable. Also available as an add-on is HEAT Shield AI for protection against zero-day phishing, social engineering and malvertising at the time of the click.

Deployment is through the Chrome Web Store, Microsoft Intune, Jamf or other device management tools, with support for Chrome, Edge, Safari, Firefox and Chromium-based AI browsers. Since nobody has to switch browsers, security patches from each browser’s vendor are said to keep reaching users as normal.

Most controls are enforced inside the browser session itself, which the company says removes the need for a proxy to route traffic. Menlo IRIS is intended to sit alongside existing network and endpoint tools.

The Menlo IRIS Browser Extension is available now.

Menlo Security has raised $250 million in funding, including a $100 million round in November 2020. Investors in the company include Vista Equity Partners Management, JPMorgan Chase & Co., American Express Ventures and General Catalyst Group Management.

Clipto enables ChatGPT and Claude to search and analyze years of people’s meetings and video calls

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Artificial intelligence assistants such as ChatGPT and Claude have become essential tools for millions of workers worldwide, helping them compose emails in seconds, generate complex code and draft reports by looking at whatever data that person has access to.

But there’s one thing that’s missing from those chatbots’ knowledge. Ask them what was discussed in the previous week’s team meeting or on yesterday’s call with a client, and it will be stumped.

It’s this shortcoming that a startup called Clipto Inc. is trying to solve with the launch of a new AI chatbot memory platform called Clipto Studio, which enables AI assistants to listen in on calls, presentations and meetings and search through them or analyze them upon request.

Clipto argues that this is going to be an important capability as AI becomes more pervasive in daily work. Though AI chatbots can sift through email inboxes and corporate databases in seconds, that’s not where many companies’ most valuable knowledge is actually stored. Rather, Clipto says the most important business insights are trapped in raw video and audio files.

Most of the big decisions are made during Zoom calls, customer interviews and team meetings, but the details of those discussions are stored in heavy, unstructured data formats that are incredibly expensive for AI to process. But AI can potentially provide much more meaningful help to workers if it has a way to access this knowledge.

That’s where Clipto Studio comes in. It’s a native skill set that integrates directly with ChatGPT, Claude and other AI assistants using the Model Context Protocol, powered by proprietary models that run locally on user’s Mac and Windows computers.

The software works by pre-indexing speech, individual speaker’s voices, facial expressions and other on-screen visuals before transforming the raw footage into a structured and easily searchable dataset that’s optimized for AI chatbots. Then, when the user asks a question about one of their recent conversations, or even one from two years ago, it can quickly find the answers it needs by searching through the metadata of those recordings.

Clipto founder and Chief Executive Henry Kang said AI knows basically everything that has been posted online, but has barely scratched the surface of the terabytes of audio and video data that’s sitting in people’s hard drives.

“The next generation of AI will need to understand the conversations, experiences and knowledge that never makes it into a text-based document,” he said. “That’s the future we’re building, giving AI the memory it needs to move beyond answering questions from the internet and start getting real world done with what we ourselves have seen and heard.”

Clipto Studio can be thought of as a kind of instant-replay service. For instance, a management consultant could ask ChatGPT to pinpoint the exact time when a project’s scope changed by looking at more than six month’s worth of calls and meetings, and it will surface the exact moment when that occurred. Alternatively, a researcher might prompt Claude to identify the most common problems with a particular product or service by looking at thousands of customer calls, and pull out direct quotes that illustrate the depth of those issues.

There are other use cases too. For instance, users could ask Clipto Studio to look at dozens of hours of event presentations they’ve attended and create a highlight reel that surfaces the most interesting or relevant moments from them.

Clipto Studio’s official launch is a big moment for the startup, which recently signaled its ambitions with the hiring of former Google LLC research scientist Dr. Mei Han as its new chief scientist. Han, who holds a doctorate in robotics from Carnegie Mellon University, has been tasked with leading the development of Clipto’s lightweight multimodal AI architecture.

Han said she believes in Clipto’s mission because it’s trying to help AI perceive the world in the same way as humans do, by seeing and listening to what’s going on around them.

“The challenge isn’t just recognizing speech or identifying what’s on screen,” she said. “It’s connecting that information across thousands of recordings so AI can understand the context and use it. We’re building the technology to make video and audio as accessible and as useful to AI as text, opening up an entirely new generation of applications.”

Neri&Hu Reinterprets Cape Town’s Maritime Heritage for Edition

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Located on Quay 7 at the V&A Waterfront in Cape Town, South Africa, the Cape Town Edition Hotel turns heritage into a destination that feels contemporary yet unmistakably local. Designed by Neri&Hu in partnership with local architects and professional firms, the hotel draws inspiration from the site’s industrial maritime character while embracing the city’s evolving cultural landscape. Although the original warehouse was too unstable to preserve, its footprint and construction logic informed the new building, which references the long, narrow forms and pitched roofs of the surrounding industrial structures.

Part of a global portfolio of hotels, The Cape Town Edition revolves around The Drum—a soaring cylindrical space open to the elements. The harbor, Table Mountain, the Atlantic, and the waterfront below are all visible from the 360-degree outdoor viewing platform. Guests, visitors, and locals alike can ascend the spiral staircase to discover Cape Town from a bird’s-eye view.

For Lyndon Neri, co-founder of Neri&Hu, The Drum began with a conversation about how to connect and distinguish the hotel’s hospitality and residential spaces. “The route is both architectural and civic,” he says. “It turns circulation into an experience of the landscape and gives the project a public dimension beyond accommodation.”

Grounded in its landscape, the Cape Town Edition is crafted with a deep appreciation for materiality. Local sourcing was central to the hotel’s sustainability strategy, informing not only the selection of building materials but also the curation of the furniture, art, and objects throughout the space.

Metalwork and joinery were supplied by local manufacturers, while the joinery draws inspiration from 19th-century travel trunks. Custom furnishings manufactured locally and rugs woven by KwaZulu-Natal-based Shuttleworth Weaving layer exceptional craftsmanship into the interiors, where warm finishes are complemented by expansive window frames that capture sweeping views.

More than 6 million bricks, supplied by a family-owned Western Cape manufacturer and laid by hand, create shifting patterns across the façade, giving the building texture and rhythm. Their warm sand tones complement the natural hues of the surrounding landscape, providing a striking backdrop against the cooler blues of the sea beyond.

“Our interest was never in an architecture that competes with this landscape, nor one that simply defers to it,” Neri says. “Instead, we asked how the building might register those forces: how brick, light, and the deliberate framing of views could create a sequence of spaces at once bound to the Waterfront and unmistakably of Cape Town.”

Throughout the hotel, art serves a purpose beyond decoration, reflecting Africa’s creative landscape and connecting guests to the people and traditions that shape the destination. Design Network Africa sourced objects from makers across the continent, including Zulu ukhamba vessels and mouth-blown recycled glass from Eswatini. Cape Town gallery Southern Guild curated the art collection, which includes black-and-white portraits by Zanele Muholi, Usha Seejarim’s artwork Fading Dish Cloth, and smoke-fired ceramics by Chuma Maweni, which lend a sculptural presence to the lobby and dining spaces.

A 240-square-meter Penthouse and a 112-square-meter Harbor Suite crown the hotel, each offering ample living spaces and panoramic views. The Cape Town Edition comprises 142 guestrooms and suites that balance elevated comfort with residential appeal. Below sea level, the spa features a sauna, steam room, infrared beds, and aromatherapy. A daily program of sound baths, yoga classes, and movement sessions enhances the experience, with additional opportunities to unwind beside the heated rooftop pool and sundeck.

The Punch Room draws on the centuries-old tradition of communal punch drinking, a ritual carried along trade routes that once passed through the Cape. Velvet banquettes, wood paneling, and atmospheric lighting create an opulent retreat that offers a modern interpretation of the region’s rich cultural and culinary heritage.

The end goal? For guests and visitors to leave with a lasting sense of Cape Town, shaped by the connections between the hotel’s interiors and the surrounding landscape. For Neri, the intention was “for the building to reflect not only the visual character of Cape Town, but also its material culture and craftsmanship.”

Malia Agostinelli is a writer and editor blending design and the built environment with the art of storytelling. Rarely found without a pen in hand, she also enjoys exploring new countries, taking yoga classes, and gathering inspiration for her Substack, Collected Elsewhere.

Industrial architecture, locally sourced materials, and South African craftsmanship create a contemporary hotel rooted in the city’s waterfront history

Designers Elena and Giulia Sella pair restored frescoes, bespoke furnishings, and Florentine craftsmanship to give a centuries-old palazzo a contemporary identity

Studio LOST draws on the history of an ancient coastal quarry through sculptural stone, bespoke timber joinery, and traditional Rhodian mosaics

Architect Rie Azuma transforms a Meiji-era prison into 48 luxury suites, preserving its brickwork and radial layout while opening former cells into generous living spaces

Andrew Skurka on Vapor Barrier Liners for Winter Camping

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

This article is adapted from Andrew Skurka’s first-person video for The Trek’s YouTube channel, part of a weekly series. Edited for clarity and readability.

On a multi-day winter backpacking trip, your own sweat can become one of your biggest problems. Moisture builds up in your gloves, boots, insulated jackets, and sleeping bag. When temperatures stay below freezing, drying everything out can be nearly impossible.

That’s where vapor barrier liners (VBLs) come in. These intentionally non-breathable layers keep perspiration from reaching your insulation. They can feel clammy and take some getting used to, but in the right conditions they can make a remarkable difference.

I learned this the hard way. I was the first person to complete the Sea-to-Sea Route, and during that trip I spent the winter on the trail, snowshoeing 1,400 miles through Michigan, Wisconsin, and Minnesota. In January 2007, a few months before the Great Western Loop, I hiked about 380 miles along Minnesota’s North Shore from Duluth to Ely, a trip I called Ultralight in the Nation’s Icebox. And I started the Alaska-Yukon Expedition in mid-March in Kotzebue, north of the Arctic Circle. I still remember that for the first three days, the temperature never got above 15 degrees below zero.

The Sea-to-Sea Route was my first legitimate winter backpacking experience, and I wore a pretty traditional layering system. It was basically an extension of what I’d wear in three-season conditions:

When I say cold, I mean daytime temperatures that never get above freezing, ideally no warmer than the 20s, and nighttime lows in the single digits or teens. The same problem applies at anything colder.

You’re active during the day. In the heat of the day, when it’s 20 degrees out and you’re really moving on snowshoes or skis, you might wear just a midweight base layer, or maybe you need a fleece. Either way, you’re perspiring from the activity. And at night, your body is still losing moisture through insensible perspiration.

If you’re wearing all of your layers at night inside a sleeping bag, that moisture moves out through your layering system. It usually gets trapped in my active insulated jacket, or on the inside of my waterproof-breathable shell, or, if I’m in my sleeping bag, inside my big arctic puffy. As the moisture moves away from your body, it gets colder and colder until it hits the dew point, and that point is often inside your layering system or inside your sleeping bag.

Even if you’ve never backpacked in winter, this has probably happened to you. After a day of alpine skiing at a resort, you notice the inside of your gloves is pretty wet. So is the inside of your plastic ski boots.

That’s not a problem if you’re out for the day, because you get home or into a hut and dry everything out. On a backpacking trip, you can’t. You can’t dry out your sleeping bag, your boots, your gloves, or your big belay parka, so the moisture stays trapped. All your stuff gets wet and stays wet, and when it’s wet, it’s also cold.

That was my failing on the Sea-to-Sea Route back in 2004 and 2005. I could go out for a night or two, but by nights three, four, and five, moisture was accumulating in my layers and my sleeping bag. After a couple of days out, I needed to get inside somewhere warm and dry so I could reset all of my systems and safely go out again.

A vapor barrier liner is a non-breathable layer worn just outside a thin or midweight base layer and underneath your insulating layers. The fluorescent orange jacket I’m wearing in the video is an example. Instead of allowing perspiration to move into your fleece, parka, or sleeping bag, the barrier keeps that moisture closer to your skin.

There’s a tradeoff: the sweat doesn’t magically disappear. It stays inside the barrier, where your base layer may become damp or clammy. That’s why I pay close attention to how hard I’m working, and I vent or adjust layers before I start sweating heavily.

If you’re considering multi-day backpacking or thru-hiking in winter with daytime temperatures consistently around 20°F or colder, I think vapor barrier liners are well worth looking into. They can significantly reduce the moisture buildup I just described, although they don’t replace good temperature management or protection from outside moisture.

With vapor barrier liners, your layering system looks like this:

For more on building a clothing system from a small set of versatile pieces, see The Only 12 Backpacking Clothes You Really Need.

The first benefit is that a vapor barrier liner protects your exterior layers. It keeps them warm by keeping them dry.

With the VBL on, I’m still perspiring a little, but much of that body moisture is stopped by the barrier instead of moving into my fleece or insulated parkas. At night, I can wear my insulating layers inside my sleeping bag while greatly reducing how much perspiration reaches the bag’s insulation. It won’t make my sleeping bag waterproof or eliminate every source of condensation, but it helps protect against moisture coming from my body.

If you’re wearing what’s almost a trash bag next to your skin, you’re going to be very aware of how much you’re perspiring, because it starts to feel clammy. When you feel that clamminess, it’s your sign to moderate your effort, so maybe you go slower, or to start taking layers off.

Sometimes you’re in between. You don’t want to take a layer off because you’re not that warm, but you definitely need to expel some heat and moisture. That’s when you ventilate. RBH Designs makes a fantastic vapor barrier outer layer, like the one I’m wearing, with all sorts of venting features: a full front zip, pit zips, and zippers on the arms. You can keep wearing the layer but really open it up so the heat and moisture escape.

In very cold weather, you don’t want a lot of ways to lose heat, which is why you carry lots of puffy layers and multiple layers. Without a vapor barrier liner, moisture moves through your layers, and as it gets close to the outside, it evaporates. That evaporation steals heat away from you. A VBL does an excellent job of keeping your heat next to you and preventing that evaporative heat loss.

Here’s a classic scenario that even three-season backpackers will relate to. You hike up a mountain without moderating your effort much. As you climb, it gets cooler and cooler, maybe you enter the clouds and lose the sun, and when you finally reach the top, your back is drenched and your hair is wet with sweat. Then, as you’re signing the summit register, you start getting cold, because all that sweat is evaporating off your body. A vapor barrier liner keeps that moisture inside. You can think of it as its own microclimate, so you don’t get that evaporative heat loss.

If you want to wander into vapor barrier liners, start with gloves. My layering system for really cold weather starts with the DeFeet Duraglove wool glove. It’s definitely a liner glove, but it has more heft than most. Over that, I go straight to the RBH Designs mitts.

When I notice my hands starting to get clammy from sweat, it’s easy. I take the mitt off and loop it onto a carabiner on my backpack’s shoulder strap. Then I can ski or snowshoe for a while without it, and when my hand starts to get cold again, I put the mitt back on.

Again, the idea with vapor barrier liners is that you become sensitive to your rate of perspiration, and you either moderate your effort or take off or adjust layers so that you sweat less.

With daytime temperatures no warmer than the teens, I would move toward vapor barrier liner socks. Here’s how the system works, from the inside out:

Then all of that goes into my boot. For those curious, mine are old-school leather three-pin telemark boots, where the telemark binding clamps down on the duckbill at the front.

Vapor barrier liner socks become important because otherwise the sweat from your feet gets into the outer layers of the boot, or gets trapped inside it, and then it’s tough to get the boot dry.

The very last thing you’d add to your vapor barrier liner system is a pair of vapor barrier liner pants. It has to be pretty cold to warrant them, with daytime highs in the low to mid teens and ideally in the single digits. Your legs generate so much heat that it has to be really cold before you’d want running tights with vapor barrier liner pants on top.

As with the top, you want the pants to be really well ventilated, maybe with full or three-quarter zips on the legs. That way you can easily open them up to let the heat and moisture out, and keep moving without having to take the pants off completely because you’re overheating and sweating too much.

With all the temperature ranges I’ve given you, keep in mind that they depend on the other conditions: how much sun there is, how much wind there is, and whether you’re walking on dirt, which absorbs a lot more solar radiation, or on ice or snow, which reflects the sun back at you. You might adjust these recommendations up or down for when to start using vapor barrier liners.

Vapor barrier clothing is a niche category, so you won’t find many options in mainstream backpacking stores. Rab makes vapor barrier socks, and Western Mountaineering offers a vapor barrier liner for sleeping bags.

For purpose-built VBL clothing, I recommend looking at RBH Designs. I’ve had excellent experiences with its equipment, including vapor barrier mitts, socks, and ventilated jackets. As with any specialized winter gear, check current availability and sizing before purchasing.

In addition to jackets, pants, socks, and mitts, there’s another commercially available option: a vapor barrier liner for your sleeping bag. You wear your base layer shirt, pants, and socks, then sleep inside the VBL, which goes inside your sleeping bag.

That’s an effective way to reduce perspiration getting into your sleeping bag’s down or synthetic insulation, because most of the body moisture stays inside the liner’s microclimate. It won’t, however, protect your sleeping bag from moisture coming from outside the liner.

My big hang-up with a vapor barrier sleeping bag liner is that you can’t use it during the day. You still end up with the same situation: you have on your liner gloves and a normal mountaineering mitt, your hands are sweating, and that sweat is making its way into the insulation of the mitt. That’s not going to help you on day two. I think it’s better to have vapor barrier liner technology not only at night, to keep your sleeping bag from getting wet, but also during the day, to keep your outer layers from getting wet.

What’s the minimum trip length where I’d want vapor barrier liners? My answer is anything of one night or more.

Suppose it’s a two-day, one-night trip, and it’s two full days. If you follow the conventional wisdom of using breathable layers, you have your liner gloves and a standard overmitt without a vapor barrier liner. At the end of the first day, your mitt is very wet and very cold. When you wake up in the morning and put it back on, it’s not going to be as nice to wear as it was on the first day.

With boots, you’re perspiring through your feet all day long, and that moisture gets trapped inside your boot. When you wake up in the morning, your boot is going to be frozen. I’ve done a lot of very uncomfortable things in the outdoors, but one of the most uncomfortable is putting your foot inside a frozen boot.

If you ever end up with wet shoes, either from your sweat or from external water like a creek crossing, the key is what you do when you pull into camp at night. Open up the shoe, really extend the tongue, and loosen the laces as much as you possibly can. Then just let it freeze overnight. In the morning, jam your feet inside, and the warmth of your feet will sort of thaw it out. If it freezes in that open position, you can at least get your feet into the shoe.

One additional advantage of using vapor barrier liners is that I can rely more confidently on down insulation during long winter trips. You don’t need VBLs to use down in winter, but limiting the body moisture that reaches it helps preserve its warmth over consecutive days.

You’ve probably heard the usual pros and cons of down versus synthetic insulation. Down generally offers more warmth for its weight. And while synthetic insulation tends to retain more warmth than down when damp, the idea that it stays fully warm when wet is misleading. Wet synthetic insulation still loses insulating performance, just usually not as much as wet down.

With a vapor barrier protecting my outer layers from perspiration, I can use a down sleeping bag, a down belay parka, and even a down active parka with less concern about that particular source of moisture. Those pieces are generally excellent for warmth-to-weight, and good down insulation can last for many years. My GoLite down belay parka dates back to 2009. A VBL doesn’t protect any of this gear from snow, rain, or other outside moisture, though, so keeping it dry still matters.

I’ve been talking about vapor barrier liners for a long time. I used them extremely successfully on Ultralight in the Nation’s Icebox in 2007 and the Alaska-Yukon Expedition in 2010. Yet in the last 15 or 20 years, the market hasn’t changed very much. There are a few good products out there, but the selection is still small.

That’s fine. I think there’s a very small market for vapor barrier liners because, let’s be honest, not many people go out in the wintertime on multi-day or multi-night trips. The days are short, the nights are long, it’s very cold, and it can be kind of lonely.

But winter can also be a beautiful time to be in the backcountry. It extends your season if you want to do more than backpack between June and September. It’s a way to expand your skill set, and you may learn things you can apply to your three-season trips. Getting out at all times of the year, not just when the weather’s nice, will definitely make you a better backpacker.

There’s not a lot of information out there about vapor barrier liners. I wrote a blog post about them years ago that now has more than 200 comments. If you have questions about vapor barrier liners, ask them in the comments on the video, and I’ll try to get to them.

On multi-day winter trips, body moisture can build up in your layers and sleeping bag night after night, and drying everything out may be impossible. A vapor barrier liner worn just outside your base layer reduces how much perspiration reaches your insulation, makes you more aware of when you’re sweating, and helps limit evaporative heat loss. I consider VBLs especially worthwhile when daytime temperatures stay around 20°F or colder: start with mitts, consider socks when highs are in the teens, and save pants for particularly cold conditions. Even for a single overnight trip in those conditions, they’re worth a serious look. You still need to manage your sweat and keep outside moisture off your gear.

Andrew Skurka has backpacked more than 30,000 miles and has been named Adventurer of the Year by both Outside and National Geographic. He is the author of The Ultimate Hiker’s Gear Guide and founded his guided backpacking program in 2011.

Skurka no longer guides winter trips, but his team leads guided backpacking trips in three-season conditions in southern Utah, the High Sierra, Alaska, and Yellowstone. The 2027 schedule will be posted by November 6, 2026, and applications open December 7, 2026.

ServiceNow launches AI-powered engine to accelerate migrations from CRM rivals

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

ServiceNow Inc. is bidding to steal business from other customer relationship management vendors with an artificial intelligence-powered engine that it says can reduce enterprise CRM implementations from months to weeks.

The new Autonomous CRM Engine uses AI to analyze existing CRM environments, recommend business process improvements and automate deployment of redesigned workflows on ServiceNow’s platform.

The offering represents a competitive challenge to established CRM providers such as Salesforce Inc. and Microsoft Corp. by potentially lowering the switching costs that help vendors retain customers. The initial rollout targets only Salesforce installations, and others are planned.

The announcement comes amid growing concern about the costs and limitations of aging CRM systems. Gartner Inc. last year warned that legacy CRM platforms can create hidden maintenance expenses, integration dependencies and security risks that impede modernization. The researcher predicts that by 2028, most enterprises will identify outdated CRM systems as an obstacle to effectively using AI.

Gartner recommends that organizations evaluate technical debt, integration dependencies, data quality and the full cost of maintaining legacy CRM systems before deciding whether to modernize or replace them.

The discovery process is one of the most time-consuming aspects of CRM modernization, said Terence Chesire, group vice president of CRM and industry workflows at ServiceNow. Organizations typically must interview employees across departments and geographies to understand how sales and service processes operate.

“We’re able to collapse that into literally days where we show them how all their sales processes occur by product line,” he said.

Autonomous CRM Engine uses metadata application programming interfaces to examine business objects, configuration settings, custom scripts and workflows. It organizes that information into a graph database and applies AI to reconstruct how activities such as sales approvals, customer escalations and service requests actually function.

ServiceNow claims the analysis can expose discrepancies between documented procedures and everyday practices, including approval steps employees routinely bypass.

Beyond transferring existing processes to a new platform, ServiceNow uses the discovery information to recommend redesigned workflows based on industry practices. Business leaders can examine proposed changes in a visual workspace and modify processes to accommodate unique business characteristics. Once approved, changes can be deployed directly into ServiceNow’s CRM environment.

Chesire acknowledged that automation doesn’t eliminate difficult organizational decisions or integration challenges. “The parts that potentially get extended are all those linkages to other systems because we always land inside a heterogeneous environment,” he said.

ServiceNow addresses those dependencies through its Workflow Data Fabric, which provides access to external information without the need to move or copy it. Its integration capabilities also extend to older applications through robotic process automation.

Gartner cautions that CRM modernization projects must account for hidden application dependencies, regulatory data-retention requirements and business processes that may rely on retired systems. It also recommends establishing clear data ownership and governance policies before making changes.

The research firm suggests measuring modernization success through operational improvements rather than retired applications. It suggests performance targets such as annual operating cost reductions of at least 20%, retention of 95% of historical data in searchable archives and a 35% reduction in duplicate or inconsistent records.

Chesire acknowledged that ServiceNow hasn’t conducted comparative productivity studies against competing CRM platforms, although the company demonstrates its technology to prospects using their own business processes and data. The firm cited several customer examples to demonstrate the potential benefits of consolidating customer operations.

Germany soccer league Bundesliga-Gruppe GmbH consolidated fan services from seven digital channels onto ServiceNow in about a month and now resolves 89% of fan inquiries on first contact, according to ServiceNow. Bell Canada connected 26 applications and 8,800 data silos to one platform to support 12,000 field employees, while Everpure Inc. eliminated 13 legacy systems and now resolves 72% of customer issues before they’re reported.

Chesire said AI adoption is increasing pressure to modernize systems whose business logic is buried in custom code. ServiceNow plans to extend Autonomous CRM Engine’s capabilities to continuously monitor configurations and eventually improve CRM operations autonomously.

The engine is available now, with additional capabilities planned for 2027.

ServiceNow said its CRM and Industry Workflows business has surpassed $2 billion in annual contract value. Its CRM net new annual contract value tripled both sequentially and year-over-year in the third quarter.

Square takes Squaremates AI agent out of beta and opens up to sellers’ own AI software

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Block Inc.-owned commerce platform Square today made its Squaremates artificial intelligence business agent generally available to sellers in the U.S., after the agent entered open beta in April under the name Managerbot.

For sellers who would rather stay in the AI software they already use, Square is also offering a new command-line tool and an updated Model Context Protocol server.

Running a business is “an endless string of judgment calls that are all on the owner,” said Willem Avé, global head of intelligence at Square. “We built Squaremates to help share that load,” he said.

Each seller’s agent now runs in its own secure workspace with access to cloud compute. Square said that gives it room for longer jobs. A seller could ask it to find customers who’ve lapsed over a given stretch of sales, for instance, and have it draft a discount to win them back.

A beta feature lets Squaremates build restocking orders directly on a supplier’s website, and the seller places them from there. Email access for its daily briefing is coming soon.

Fraser Thompson, who leads strategy and operations at Los Angeles-based Burgers Never Say Die LLC, put Squaremates to work on the restaurant’s house-made cheese sauce. “I dip my fries in it, I dunk my burger in it,” he said. “So I’m thinking, how are we not selling more of this stuff?”

Digging into attachment rates and customer segments showed that “our OGs, the regulars who’ve been with us since day one, love it.” The restaurant started promoting it on social media with what Thompson called “silly posts,” and he said that a few months later “we’d 12x’d our cheese sauce.”

“A lot of sellers already have an AI tool they like working in,” Avé said. The updated server, Square MCP, lets a third-party assistant answer questions about the business and make changes once the account is connected. Square CLI is aimed at sellers and developers who want to script or build on top of their accounts, and it gives their own tools and agents access to Square’s application programming interfaces.

Squaremates Apps are custom applications a seller writes with an AI coding tool and the CLI, which Square then hosts with a live connection to the seller’s business. Splitting cash tips by hours worked is one of the uses Square suggests.

Donnie McClanahan, who has four cafes around Knoxville, Tennessee, built a labor board app with the CLI and an AI coding agent. It pulls schedules and timecards from every location onto one screen. A lot of his mornings used to go to “playing catch-up just figuring out who’s where,” he said.

Sellers can try the tools at the Squaremates Tour, a run of in-person events Square kicked off recently. Apart from the features still in beta or coming soon, everything announced today is available to U.S. sellers at no additional cost. Squaremates itself is not yet available to sellers on Square’s Multi-Account Suite.

Apple HomeView review: An adorable smart display that earns its place

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Apple’s HomeView smart display is here. It combines a 7-inch display with Siri AI and an all-new software experience that actually feels really familiar.

I’ve spent the last ten days living with HomeView in my kitchen. Here’s everything you need to know.

HomeView is an adorable little thing. That’s the best way to describe it. A 7-inch square display sits atop a rounded aluminum speaker base. A small, polished metal arm connects it and lets you tilt the display forward and backward.

HomeView comes in four colors: Starlight, Rose Pink, Silver, and Space Gray. My review unit is Starlight, and it’s very subtle. I had to double-check the box to make sure it was Starlight, not Silver.

The decision to make the display square is interesting. Between that design and the software experience centered around faces and widgets, HomeView really does feel like an oversized Apple Watch for your home.

I really appreciate the square display. It makes HomeView look less like an iPad propped up on a stand and more like a dedicated, purpose-driven device.

In terms of specs, the 7-inch display has a 1088 × 1088 resolution, equating to a pixel density of 220 pixels per inch. Max brightness is 500 nits. It can also dim all the way to 0.3 nits, which means it can go in a bedroom without being a nuisance. It also has True Tone to match your room’s lighting temperature.

For the past ten days, my partner and I have been living with HomeView on our kitchen counter. The square display and aluminum base look great in that setting. I don’t think an iPad-style display would look nearly as intentional. We also don’t have much spare counter space. If HomeView were any bigger or wider, I’m not sure we’d have room for it.

In the lead-up to HomeView’s launch, I was skeptical of how usable a 7-inch display would be. In practice, I’ve found this to be (mostly) a non-issue. Apple has done a great job optimizing the interface for the display. Text is readable at a glance from a few feet away, timers are nice and large, and the interface adapts to show more information when you’re closer.

Sometimes I wish it were bigger. Viewing a recipe requires a bit more scrolling than I would like. It’d be nice to fit a couple more widgets on the Modular face. But those trade-offs are easier to accept when I consider how much I appreciate HomeView’s compact footprint.

Apple touts that the HomeView has a custom-engineered acoustic system. There are two custom full-range drivers in the display, paired with a down-firing woofer and two force-canceling passive radiators in the base.

In practice, HomeView’s sound quality falls somewhere between the HomePod mini and the full-size second-generation HomePod. Its sound quality won’t blow you away, but it’s respectable for music and podcast playback. I replaced a full-size original HomePod in my kitchen with it, and while it doesn’t sound quite as good, it makes up for it in other ways … like by having a screen.

HomeView can serve as an AirPlay target, too, so you can beam content from your iPhone or other Apple device right to it. You can also AirPlay from HomeView to other AirPlay targets in your house.

HomeView doesn’t have any buttons, which is a design decision I’ve found very frustrating on multiple occasions. I wish it had volume buttons, so I could quickly adjust the volume without yelling at Siri or using the touchscreen.

HomeView’s software borrows heavily from iOS and watchOS. The interface is built around a system of faces with widgets, very similar to the Apple Watch’s complications system. The process of configuring a face will feel very familiar to Apple Watch and iPhone users.

You can customize faces by long-pressing on them, which reveals a familiar “Customize” screen. Here, you can change the style, color, widgets, and background. You can also add widgets from your nearby iPhone. Apple says that widgets are visible to everyone, but widget content is only visible while your primary device is in the home.

I’ve become particularly fond of the Modular face, which lets you place four equally sized square widgets. I think it provides a nice amount of glanceable information without feeling too dense. The Photos face is also a delight, essentially turning the HomeView into a digital picture frame. Support for Shared Albums is especially nice.

I think Apple made the right decision by building the HomeView experience around faces rather than a traditional app-focused interface. Most of the time, HomeView is just sitting on your counter or table, and you’re not actively interacting with it. That’s why I’ve gravitated towards the Modular face.

The faces system also means that HomeView doesn’t constantly demand your attention. Just like the physical design, it’s there when you need it, but otherwise does a good job blending into the background.

Swiping down from the left two-thirds of the display activates Siri AI, while swiping down from the top right takes you to Control Center. Swiping up from the bottom takes you to the Home Screen, where you’ll find a grid of apps and spots to add even more widgets.

There is no App Store on HomeView. Instead, there’s a collection of built-in Apple apps paired with audio and video apps through its CarPlay-based app system. Here are the Apple apps that are available on HomeView:

I’m hopeful that Apple builds on the app story for HomeView sooner rather than later. I don’t think HomeView needs its own App Store, but I do think it needs more apps. This includes more Apple apps and additional third-party apps.

I desperately want Apple to add the Notes app to HomeView. My wife and I keep recipes and dinner ideas in Notes, so it would be great to have that experience available on HomeView. It feels like an odd omission right now.

I think the idea of basing the system on CarPlay makes sense. There are a lot of similarities in UI between how an app should work in the car and on a smart display. The Audible app, for example, translates really well from CarPlay to HomeView, as does Spotify.

However, the list of supported categories for CarPlay is a lot broader than the audio and video category Apple is allowing on HomeView right now. There are signs this could expand very soon, and I’m hopeful that’s the case.

One of the cleverest aspects of HomeView is its recipe experience. When you open a recipe in Safari, HomeView gives you the option to convert it into a dedicated cooking interface. You can browse for recipes directly on HomeView, or find one on your iPhone and simply hold it in front of HomeView to hand off the webpage.

HomeView then intelligently detects the actual recipe information and puts it into a streamlined interface. You can view the full ingredient list and measurements, and check off items as you go. The recipe itself is broken down into individual steps. It also intelligently identifies when you need to set a timer and gives you the option to start one directly from the recipe.

This is the exact type of feature that’s perfect for HomeView, and perfect for a dedicated screen sitting on the kitchen counter. You don’t have to keep reaching for your phone, unlocking it, and finding your place in the recipe.

The feature works incredibly well. I tried it with recipes of varying lengths from multiple different websites without any major problems.

There are some limitations, though. For example, it does struggle if a website splits the recipe into multiple pages. Also, as I mentioned earlier, the 7-inch display means there’s more scrolling involved than I’d like. It doesn’t solve my frustration with the lack of a dedicated Notes app. Still, it’s a great example of where this design excels.

Ultimately, the HomeView’s software interface is really well done. Delightful animations appear throughout the experience, like when notifications or the Siri blob slide in from the top bezel. Everything feels very smooth. The software feels familiar because of its similarities to Apple Watch and iPhone, but it’s also clearly purpose-built for the smart display form factor.

Since HomeView is meant for everyone in your home, Apple had to engineer a multi-user system. HomeView supports up to six people, each with their own personalized Home Screen, widgets, and access to personal information.

HomeView identifies the different people in your house in two ways: facial recognition and voice recognition. The goal is to recognize who’s interacting with it and customize the experience for that person. So, when I approach HomeView, it can recognize my face and display my calendar, reminders, and messages. When my wife approaches, it can recognize her instead and show her information.

The facial recognition implementation is particularly interesting. HomeView doesn’t have Face ID. Instead, it uses its built-in camera for facial recognition. During setup, you’re asked to turn your head left, right, up, and down so HomeView can capture your face from different angles.

In practice, HomeView’s facial recognition works surprisingly well. I was skeptical, but it’s done a good job of identifying both my wife and me. If I approach it and try to open the Messages app while it’s on my wife’s profile, I get a message saying it can’t verify my identity. If I repeatedly try, HomeView disables facial recognition, requiring my wife to authenticate using her iPhone before she can access her personal information again.

In addition to protecting personal data, HomeView does a decent job of automatically switching between profiles. There’s occasionally a delay of a few seconds, so I wish it were slightly faster.

HomeView is powered by Apple’s A18 chip, which enables support for Siri AI. However, Siri AI works a bit differently on HomeView than on other Apple devices.

HomeView can answer world knowledge questions on its own, without relying on a paired iPhone or iPad. This is basically the trivia questions we’ve all grown accustomed to shouting at voice assistants over the years. The difference, of course, is that Siri AI answers these questions well, unlike old Siri. On HomeView, the experience is even better because you can visually see the response on the display, often accompanied by images or other visual information.

The second aspect of Siri AI is personal context. To use Siri AI’s personal context on HomeView, you need an Apple Intelligence-enabled iPhone or iPad in your house.

Apple says that the device must have been recently unlocked and be connected to the same Wi-Fi network as HomeView.

In my testing over the last 10 days, I’ve never run into a situation where HomeView was unable to answer a Siri AI query because my iPhone was unavailable.

I’ve found Siri AI to be quite impressive and useful since it debuted in September. It’s a huge upgrade over old Siri. Broadly speaking, that experience extends to HomeView.

Many of the same personal context questions I ask on my iPhone can now be answered by HomeView. And because of HomeView’s form factor, it’s more useful when multitasking. Instead of reaching for my iPhone or hoping it’s within earshot, I can just ask HomeView.

Here are a few questions HomeView has answered without a hiccup:

In terms of speed, I’ve found HomeView to be slightly slower to answer personal context questions than my iPhone Duo. It’s not a huge difference, but it’s a few seconds each time. I assume this is because HomeView is basically handing off the request to my iPhone, adding an additional step that requests directly on the iPhone don’t have to do.

Siri AI is not without its faults. There are times on HomeView where it just hangs and never responds, or responds with a generic “Sorry, try again later” message of some sort. HomeView’s primary interaction method is your voice, which makes these types of Siri AI failures even more frustrating.

HomeView can also serve as the command center for your smart home. It has a dedicated Home app, as well as multiple different Home-focused face options. You can also, of course, use your voice to control HomeKit accessories and scenes through Siri.

There’s ultimately nothing particularly remarkable about HomeView’s support for smart home control. It offers much of the same functionality as the Home app on your iPhone, but in one central location that everyone in your house can use.

There is one limitation: you currently can’t use Siri AI on HomeView to control secure devices like locks and garage doors, even if the device recognizes you. If they are part of a scene, you can tell Siri AI to set the scene and it works. But you can’t individually control them.

Apple tells Joanna Stern that a software update coming next month will add security accessory support to HomeView’s Siri AI.

I wish Apple would expand what HomeView can do for the smart home. It has a built-in camera, so why can’t I use that camera for motion sensing or as a HomeKit Secure Video camera?

I’ve found the screen most useful for quickly checking the status of accessories, like seeing whether a door is locked or glancing at the doorbell camera. For actually controlling accessories, I still find Siri and automations to be the easiest approach. HomeView hasn’t really changed that, although I do appreciate being able to adjust the thermostat right from the kitchen counter.

HomeView costs $299, which is about $100 less than I expected based on rumors. That’s a surprisingly reasonable price, I think. The key, of course, is whether you’ll actually use it day to day.

When you get a new iPhone, you already have a pretty good idea of what you’ll use it for and where it fits into your life. New features might change how you use it, but the core functionality stays the same.

HomeView is very different. At first, it sits on your counter and is nice to look at. It’s a very, very cute little thing. But it takes time to discover how and why it fits into your life.

At first, I had no idea whether I’d actually use HomeView. But in the ten days it’s been sitting on my counter, it’s grown on me quite a bit. It’s gone from being a nice-to-have to something I use and rely on for multiple tasks each day.

It’s also been interesting watching my partner interact with it. Her first impression was essentially, “It’s cute, but I don’t know what I’ll use it for.” Over the last week, however, she’s remarked multiple times how much she likes it.

Whether it’s personal context-focused Siri AI interactions, simply being able to glance and see how long is left on a timer, watching the animated album art as a song plays, or the joy of the Photos face, HomeView has earned its spot.

In fact, I moved HomeView from our kitchen to my office for a day to test a few features and settings in more depth. While cooking dinner that night, we both found ourselves shouting Siri commands into the air and glancing at the empty spot on the counter, only to remember it wasn’t there.

I think that’s the best illustration of what makes HomeView special. You don’t have to be a HomeKit enthusiast or spend hours tinkering with smart home automations to appreciate it. It’s a device everyone in the house can find their own uses for, and it becomes more useful the longer it’s part of your routine.

Hundreds of streaming services and nothing to watch

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Most viewers wish there was a TV Guide for streaming.

Bruce Springsteen famously said those words during a concert and later in a song in the 1990s about the number of TV channels seemingly growing disproportionately to the availability of enjoyable content.

Within a few years, there would be hundreds of TV channels and still, some would argue in hyperbolic frustration, “nothing to watch.”

The advent of streaming services was poised as a potential solution to this problem. It granted viewers easy, on-demand access to thousands of movies and shows, which, theoretically, could ensure that viewers could always find something to watch.

According to a survey from Nielsen’s Gracenote, though, that’s not exactly the case today.

Gracenote, a Nielsen-owned content data business, surveyed 3,000 video streaming users in the US, Brazil, France, Germany, Mexico, and the UK who were at least 18 years old for its “2026 State of Play: Amid fragmentation frustrations, UX is the future of streaming” report. It found that many struggle to find something to stream. However, this is not necessarily because there’s nothing good on, but rather because they find it difficult to home in on good content on streaming services.

For example, over 51 percent of respondents said that “the abundance of content and [streaming] services is making it harder to find something to watch.”

Further, 19.2 percent of respondents said that, on average, they either “always” or “often” don’t know what to watch, can’t find anything, and then decide to do something else. And 27.4 percent of respondents said that, on average, they either always or often try to watch a specific program but end up not watching it because they couldn’t find it on streaming.

“Through the lens of catalogue size and streaming usage, connecting the 83.4 percent who don’t always know what they want to watch should be fairly straightforward, especially as companies increasingly use customer data to create personalized experiences that foster long-term engagement and loyalty. Audiences, however, aren’t finding what they’re looking for, and content discovery challenges are mounting,” the report reads.

Forty-seven percent of participants said they spend more time looking for something to watch than they did a year ago. The average amount of time spent is 12 minutes.

In recent years, numerous streaming services have paid large sums to air live events, including sports, to grow their advertising businesses. However, “nearly” 52 percent of streaming users that Gracenote surveyed said they “frequently don’t know which channel or service a specific sports event is on.”

“Sports events that are behind subscription paywalls are often hidden from traditional search, which inhibits discovery,” the report notes.

And in an example of streaming users yearning for experiences most closely connected to traditional, broadcast television, Gracenote reported that almost 35 percent of respondents spend more time watching free ad-supported streaming television services, such as Tubi or The Roku Channel, because subscription-based alternatives, such as Netflix and Disney+, have “become overwhelming.”

The on-demand nature of streaming services has degraded the need for live/virtual and print-out TV listings, such as a TV Guide. But challenges in finding content on streaming services are apparently leading many streaming viewers to yearn for that classic form of guidance.

In Gracenote’s survey, 72.3 percent of respondents said they want “a consolidated guide to the programming available across all of the streaming services that they use.”

Streaming service providers and analysts I’ve spoken with have pointed to content discovery as a streaming challenge for years. Some, like Netflix and Amazon Prime Video, have turned to AI to help people search for content by using casual terms or to analyze viewing habits to make new recommendations. Others, such as Tubi, have turned to user-generated content playlists and recommendations.

However, there is apparently still a deficit when it comes to good and accessible streaming recommendations, as 31 percent of survey respondents said they have yet to find a “good source of recommendations for their personal taste and preferences.”

Despite there being hundreds of streaming services—as analysis this month from research firm Parks and Associates this month states—many would agree with Springsteen that it still feels like there’s “nothin’ to watch.”

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.
