Here's How The Airbus A350 Stacks Up Against Boeing's 777X For The Next Decade Of Long-Haul Flying

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

The two powerhouses of aerospace that divide the global industry of jetliner building are locked in a years-long struggle for domination of the next-generation widebody airliner market. Boeing has promised to revolutionize the size and scale of twinjets with its 777X, which integrates the legacy 'triple seven' airframe with 787 Dreamliner technology and industry-first folding wingtips. Meanwhile, Airbus has already delivered many A350 jets and is now working on an ultra-long-range variant.

The problem for Boeing is the fact that all three variants of this 777X remain uncertified for full-scale production. Seven years after the original launch date, it still has just a handful of examples sitting on the flight line in Washington; there are no jets ready to be delivered. Airbus may not have handed over many of its largest A350 variants, but the airframe has proven itself in revenue service and remains in full-rate production with continued orders coming in.

The A350 represents a lightweight, highly optimized, all-composite platform built for flexible point-to-point networks, while the 777X is a massive, metal-composite hybrid giant designed to unlock maximum passenger and cargo volume on high-density trunk routes. Boeing aims to have the 777-8 and 777-9 certified next year, but Airbus will already be moving on to deliver Qantas' A350-1000ULR for the unique 'Project Sunrise' that will pressure any new era of ultra-long-haul flying.

The next decade of long-haul flying will be defined by how airlines choose to route their passengers. Airbus has optimized the A350 family for a fragmented market, betting that travelers prefer direct, point-to-point flights between mid-tier cities. Because the A350 is inherently lighter due to its carbon-fiber fuselage, it does not suffer a massive financial penalty if a flight is only 70% full, giving the carrier 'right-sized' versatility to open new, experimental long-range routes.

On paper, the 777X’s massive engines, the largest commercial turbofans in the world, achieve a 10% reduction in fuel burn per seat compared to older legacy aircraft, closely rivaling the A350-1000's efficiency. Thanks to its revolutionary wing design, the 777X can fit into gates where the 747 and A380 could not, yet it maintains a worse fuel efficiency baseline than the A350. The A350-1000 does not suffer from this scaling penalty.

The 777X wing is so wide that Boeing had to engineer a revolutionary folding wingtip mechanism that retracts 11.5 feet (3.5 meters) on each side upon landing. Boeing also had to design that wing to support a staggering maximum takeoff weight of 716,000 lb (325,000 kg) and General Electric GE9X engines delivering 105,000 lb of thrust (467 kN) each. Because the 777X is significantly heavier than the A350, its advantages only begin when it is full.

The A350 relies on a lighter, all-composite fuselage to cut fuel burn, and Airbus limited its wingspan to 212 feet (64.7 meters). While this shorter span yields a slightly less optimized ratio than the 777X, Airbus offsets the deficit by constructing 53% of the entire airframe, including the fuselage, out of carbon-fiber-reinforced plastic.

Airbus also integrated bifunctional winglet technology that actively twists and morphs the trailing edges of the wing during flight via the fly-by-wire system. This continuous, automated optimization reduces drag in real time as fuel burns off. Such granular engineering ensures the A350 remains efficient across various mission profiles, whereas the 777X requires specific capacity thresholds to justify its heavier airframe.

The commercial aviation industry has definitively entered a post-quadjet era, leaving the 777X to claim an uncontested monopoly as the absolute largest passenger aircraft in production. By designing the 777X to seat up to 450 passengers, Boeing is betting that mega-hub congestion will compel airlines to buy the massive twinjets. Airbus, conversely, has intentionally conceded the capacity crown, positioning the A350-1000 as the "right-sized flagship designed to align perfectly with the average demand of a point-to-point market.

The 777-9 was specifically designed by Boeing to be a direct replacement for the 747. The 777-9 fills the void left by the retirement of the double-deck jumbo aircraft, aiming for an astounding 33% lower operational cost per seat than the legendary 747-400. Additionally, because of its folding wingtips, it may enter regular Code E gates instead of just Code F gates designed for the A380, increasing destination alternatives and lowering operating costs.

Qatar Airways, British Airways, Delta Air Lines, United Airlines, Riyadh Air, Philippine Airlines, Air Canada, China Airlines, Japan Airlines, Etihad Airways, Virgin Atlantic, Air France, Ethiopian Airlines

Atlas Air, Air France-KLM, Etihad Airways, Singapore Airlines, Cathay Cargo, Air China Cargo

Emirates, Qatar Airways, Singapore Airlines, Cathay Pacific, Lufthansa, Etihad Airways, British Airways, All Nippon Airways, Air India

Qatar Airways Cargo, Cargolux, Korean Air Cargo, Lufthansa Cargo, All Nippon Airways Cargo, Ethiopian Airlines

In the meantime, Airbus built the A350-1000 with the idea that networks with scattered city pairings, rather than expansive hub-to-hub airways, are the way of the future for long-haul aviation. Rather than squeezing 450 people onto a single mega-twinjet, Airbus scaled down the A350-1000 to a more modest 350 to 410 seats. Outside of specific mega-hubs, the A350's smaller size offers less risk and more flexibility, allowing airlines to operate varying long-haul routes even during off-peak seasons.

Hub-centric airlines that rely on maximum volume to conquer slot constraints remain entrenched in the 777X concept. Emirates alone anchors the program with a massive backlog of 270 units to preserve its mass-transit business model. However, with the vast majority of international carriers expanding their networks, Airbus’ biggest twinjet is sweeping the market. Recently, airlines like Riyadh Air firmed up their orders to 31 A350-1000s, Philippine Airlines doubled its commitment to 18, and Air Canada finalized a fleet pivot to eight of the type.

The aircraft has exceptional ultra-long-range capabilities.

Airbus enjoys an absolute, uncontested monopoly on the extreme frontiers of commercial aviation, in large part thanks to the A350-1000ULR being chosen for Qantas’s upcoming 'Project Sunrise' nonstop flights connecting Sydney to London and New York. As the first completed example progresses through its flight testing campaigns ahead of its April 2027 delivery to Qantas, Airbus is reinforcing a halo status for the entire A350 program.

By capturing the contract for the most demanding mission of the decade, Airbus has proven that its jet can adapt to any point on the globe. Thanks to an ultra-light 53% composite airframe with a heavily stripped-down, low-density cabin, Airbus had the extra payload margin to permanently mount a 35,274-lb (16,000 kg) Rear Center Tank. That pushes the plane’s range to an unparalleled 9,700 nautical miles (17,900 km).

Its composite airframe gives the A350-1000 a massive 'weight budget' advantage, but Airbus is swapping the weight of 170 passengers for the RCT, which makes the ULR variant possible. Even with a carbon-fiber airframe, flying 10,000 nautical miles (18,520 km) requires further weight sacrifices, which is why Qantas’s Project Sunrise configuration is strictly limited to a low-density layout of just 238 seats. Meanwhile, the 777X is incapable of playing this low-density weight game because it is trapped by its own size.

When Boeing unveiled the 777X program, the aerospace industry took note.

The 777X's conventional widebody cross-section is positioned to dominate the lower-yield freight markets of the upcoming ten years, even while passenger capacity takes center stage. The 777X specifically increases its capacity for large, containerized cargo, which enables airlines to make money on long-haul flights even when passenger cabins are only partially filled. A 777-9 can transport up to 40 standard LD3 cargo containers on profitable freight corridors around the Pacific, surpassing the A350-1000's 44 lower-volume position limit.

The 777X can transport enormous, tall, and oddly shaped cargo pallets that fit in the A350's hold thanks to its 10% volumetric advantage. For airlines like Cathay Pacific or Singapore Airlines, this under-floor freight capability represents the thin margin between a profitable flight and a loss-making one when passenger load factors dip. The Airbus A350 utilizes a unique ovoid, or egg-shaped, fuselage cross-section designed primarily to maximize passenger shoulder space in the main cabin.

Mothballed giants are coming back online.

The sharp differences in delivery schedules are the most obvious division in this ten-year contest. With more than 600 aircraft now in active service, the Airbus A350 is a well-established, fully approved program that enables airlines like Delta and Lufthansa to regularly plan fleet renewals. On the other hand, Boeing's current goal of having the 777-9 enter service in 2027 comes after a startling seven-year delay from its initial 2020 launch schedule.

The 737 MAX disaster and the Federal Aviation Administration audit that followed, which took away a large portion of Boeing's previously granted compliance power, are directly responsible for this bottleneck. The FAA now enforces an unprecedented level of independent verification. Compounding this regulatory inertia, GE Aerospace has faced rigorous scrutiny over a durability flaw regarding an engine seal in the massive GE9X powerplants, demanding extensive engineering fixes.

The visible consequence of this certification bottleneck is vividly on display across storage airfields in Everett and Moses Lake, Washington, where more than 30 fully assembled Boeing 777-9 jets sit parked in limbo. Boeing currently faces a significant mechanical challenge in the form of change incorporation as a result of its decision to maintain low-rate manufacture of 'at-risk' airframes before completing the drawn-out certification procedure.

Even ardent, traditionally exclusive Boeing operators are carefully hedging their long-haul bets in the interim. By methodically taking advantage of Boeing's structural weakness, Airbus has made the fully developed and certified A350 program the standard long-haul architecture for the late 2020s. Airbus' overall firm order book for the A350 family has increased to 1,628 total orders, whereas Boeing has written off nearly $15 billion in pre-tax charges because of development failures and client compensation.

Air India Cancels 8 Major International Routes: See Full List Now

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Since the beginning of 2026, Air India, which is India's largest international airline, has made several changes to its international network. The carrier has added new destinations but has also withdrawn from several long-haul markets such as Chicago, citing high jet fuel prices.

In addition, the Star Alliance member has been transferring several international routes to its low-cost subsidiary, Air India Express. It has particularly been doing so from airports outside its main hubs.

Three of the cuts are to Europe. Perhaps the most notable is its service between Indira Gandhi International Airport (DEL) and Birmingham Airport (BHX), which ended earlier this month. According to Sabre Market Intelligence data cited by Aviation Week, approximately 207,300 passengers traveled between Birmingham and India in 2025, representing an 8.3% decline from the previous year.

Delhi was the second-largest market, accounting for around 53,600 passengers, behind Amritsar. Another destination the Indian flag carrier has withdrawn from is Vienna International Airport (VIE), which had also served as a stopover for some of its North American flights. This has left the Austrian capital without a nonstop connection to India.

No more direct Delhi flights from Birmingham

Replaced by Air India Express (4x weekly from December)

Replaced by Air India Express (5x weekly)

Replaced by Air India Express (up to 2x daily)

In terms of the numbers, Delhi was Vienna's largest Indian market in 2025, accounting for approximately 68,400 of the 173,500 passengers who traveled between Vienna and Indian cities that year. Elsewhere in Europe, the Star Alliance member has also discontinued its service to Copenhagen, Denmark.

Furthermore, Air India has also completely withdrawn from the international market at Chennai International Airport (MAA). Last year, the airline ended its daily flights to Bandaranaike International Airport (CMB) in Colombo, Sri Lanka. In March, it discontinued its daily service to Dubai International Airport (DXB), followed by its Singapore Changi Airport (SIN) route in May.

The latter had operated twice daily until April but was reduced to a daily service in May. However, Chennai has not lost its nonstop connection to Singapore. The route has been transferred to Air India Express. The low-cost subsidiary resumed twice-daily flights between the two cities on October 25, using its Boeing 737 MAX 8 aircraft. It had previously served the route until July 2025.

The Dubai route was also transferred to Air India Express, but the carrier operated it only briefly. According to scheduling data from aviation analytics company Cirium, the budget airline operated just seven flights on the route in March, and no nonstop services are currently scheduled between Chennai and Dubai.

Three of these are international long haul routes.

2026 has been a difficult year for airlines worldwide due to geopolitical tensions in the Middle East and rising jet fuel prices. For Air India, the situation has been further complicated by Pakistan's airspace closure, which has been in place for Indian airlines since April 2025. These restrictions have particularly affected its long-haul flights from Delhi to Europe and North America.

The airline now has to take lengthy detours over the Arabian Sea, adding up to four hours to flight times and increasing operating costs. As a result, the Star Alliance member has suspended some routes and reduced frequencies to several US destinations.

It has also reported substantial financial losses; it recorded a consolidated net loss of around $2.4 billion (₹22,238 crore) in the 2025–26 financial year. Air India reportedly lost a further $1.12 billion (₹10,500 crore to ₹10,800 crore) between April and September 2026.

The airline is now looking to make changes to its long-haul network. As reported by Eastern Eye, speaking at the Aviation India and South Asia 2026 conference in New Delhi, CEO and Managing Director Tewolde Gebremariam said Air India plans to shift some of its long-haul operations from Delhi to Mumbai as part of a wider network rebalancing. Bengaluru is also being considered as another hub.

Sega Almost Had a Legit Madden Competitor — Until EA 'Sabotaged' It

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

A bit of games industry subterfuge you didn’t see in the Madden movie

Trip Hawkins isn’t the sort of guy who’s afraid to take a risk and bet big. He founded Electronic Arts in 1982, around the same time that Atari’s fortunes were fading — and threatening to take the entire video games industry down with it. Ever since he was a kid, he’d wanted to create his own football simulation game, first as a tabletop experience, and later as a computer game for the Apple II and Commodore 64.

Hawkins is a major character in David O. Russell’s new film Madden, which hits theaters this week. It traces the football icon’s journey from coach to commentator to video game legend. Early in the story, Hawkins (John Mulaney) approaches Madden (Nicolas Cage) at a glitzy afterparty for Super Bowl XI in 1977, moments after the Oakland Raiders clinched their first NFL championship. Hawkins eagerly pitches a football computer game, but the coach is understandably distracted by the thrill of the occasion.

Hawkins keeps trying as the years tick by, eventually getting Madden’s ear during a cross-country bus trip. (Madden was notoriously afraid of flying and was driven to NFL games each week in a custom Greyhound bus called the Madden Cruiser.) Though the film takes some creative license with the timeline of how the game series came to life, Hawkins told Polygon in an interview ahead of Madden’s release that it gets a lot right about his relationship with the football icon.

“I was plotting and maneuvering for over a decade before I founded Electronic Arts. I was planning and preparing over a long period of time,” Hawkins said. “But I certainly didn't have the budget to go to Miami and try to sneak into a Super Bowl party.”

Overall, Hawkins sees the film as an accurate portrayal of both sides of the John Madden he knew: a calculating on-field strategist and a Turducken-loving primetime commentator.

“When you're dealing with John in a business setting, he's deadly serious. And while he's deadly serious and also dropping an F-bomb like every third word, John is a very intelligent guy. This is just one example of how smart he is — he never had a problem needing to get bleeped, you know, when he was on the air,” Hawkins explained.

“He figured out how to communicate with everybody,” Hawkins added. “He created a persona that was kind of emphasizing the lovable side of him, the big bear side of him. It was pretty clever of him to just create a character that he got to play as an announcer.”

The massive popularity of the Madden games is a major plot point in the film, but the series’ outsized role in the console wars of the early 1990s is only referenced obliquely. Armed with Madden’s original playbooks from the Oakland Raiders — yes, that plot point really did happen IRL — Hawkins was ready to bring John Madden Football to a 16-bit console. But he didn’t want to deal with Nintendo’s pricey third-party licensing. Instead, a team within EA spent a year reverse-engineering the Sega Genesis. That meant EA could produce software without Sega's assistance, which gave them leverage to negotiate more favorable licensing terms with Nintendo’s biggest rival.

“I basically called Sega and said, ‘Look, this is what we've done. You know, we can all go to court and have a big lawsuit and waste everybody's time. Or we can be partners and work together in the marketplace and try to beat out Nintendo in this 16-bit market segment.’ We were able to work out a lucrative arrangement where we could do that. And they knew that if they didn't make it a really good deal for me, I would just go do it on my own anyway,” Hawkins explained.

Sega was working on a football game of its own, Joe Montana Football. Like Madden, it was set to be released in time for the holiday season of 1990, shortly after the Genesis launched in North America. But development wasn’t going according to plan. Sega CEO Hayao Nakayama called Hawkins in a panic, asking if he’d be willing to “cancel” Madden and instead release the game under the name Joe Montana Football. Hawkins came up with a clever solution that worked for both EA and Sega.

“Of course, I'm thinking to myself, ‘I really don't wanna disappoint him and our relationship is really important to me, but there's no way I'm gonna do that,’” Hawkins said. “I knew that this debut 16-bit game was a really great NFL football game. I knew it was gonna be the pivot point for EA Sports, and it was gonna really be able to make it take off. And as it turned out, I was able to tell Sega, ‘We're still gonna put Madden Football out. ‘But I'll make Montana Football for you also.’ And of course, like 98% under the hood is the program code from Madden Football. And I just gave a facelift and, no offense intended, but I sabotaged the game to make it inferior to our game.”

“The Montana game that we made had maybe less than 10% of the number of plays that we had at the Madden game,” Hawkins added. “I was able to deliver them the final version of Montana in six weeks.”

A weekly cheat sheet to what you’ll eventually love to play. No buying, no commitment — just saving for later.

Joe Montana Football omitted Madden’s pseudo-3D field and reduced the playbook from 113 plays to 13. Even so, Montana sold more than two million copies, compared to Madden’s 400,000. Still, Madden was a massive hit for EA and spawned a sequel the following year. The series has gone on to sell more than 150 million copies. For Hawkins, the last-minute project was worth it to get the game he’d been dreaming about for so long in players’ hands.

“I thought to myself, ‘Well, their marketing budget is way bigger than mine.’ Sega's marketing is gonna dwarf what Electronic Arts can afford, and I don't want anybody to know that they're really, fundamentally the same game. I mean, our game's better, you know, we took a bunch of stuff out,” Hawkins explained. “But that was part of the contract, that they couldn't tell anybody where the game came from.”

Madden may not have touched on a lot of the behind-the-scenes games industry lore, but Hawkins says the coach was just as fond of talking about his namesake video game over a heaping plate of Santa Maria barbeque as he is in the movie.

“Uh, yeah. We ate a lot of meat,” Hawkins recalled with a chuckle. “John is a big, big time eater.”

Apple TV 4K review: The best streamer comes at a cost

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

It’s been four years since Apple last updated the Apple TV 4K, but a new version is here. There’s Siri AI support, a faster chip, an improved remote, and more.

Here’s everything that’s changed, what’s better, and whether the new Apple TV 4K justifies its new $299 price.

The Apple TV 4K’s overall design is largely unchanged. It’s still the same familiar black box that sits underneath your television. The only visual change is that the front status light has moved from the right side to the center.

Inside, Apple has upgraded the A15 Bionic to the A19 chip, touting 50% faster CPU performance and twice the GPU performance.

Those are great improvements on paper. In practice, I’m not doing anything on my Apple TV that really stresses the performance. Compared to my third-generation Apple TV 4K, I’ve occasionally noticed slightly smoother animations and faster performance in apps that handle more processing locally, like Infuse.

I think the biggest advantage of the A19 chip is future-proofing. People tend to buy and keep these devices for years. In fact, I’m still using a first-generation Apple TV 4K from 2017 in my bedroom, and it still works just fine nearly a decade later. The A19 gives the new Apple TV plenty of performance headroom for future tvOS updates and more demanding apps, even if those advantages aren’t particularly noticeable today.

The Siri Remote looks the same as before, but it now supports Precision Finding in the Find My app, making it easier to track down when misplaced. You can also play a sound on the remote to help locate it. I lose my Siri Remote all the time, so this honestly might be the change I’ve noticed and appreciated most in my 10 days testing the new Apple TV.

It’s worth noting Apple also sells the new Siri Remote separately for $59, and it’s compatible with every previous Apple TV 4K generation. You do, however, need a newer iPhone model to use Precision Finding.

One of the more interesting new features is support for a four-HomePod surround sound system. You need four HomePod (2nd generation) or HomePod mini 2 speakers, arranged in matching pairs at the front and rear of the room. Apple TV automatically calibrates the speakers based on their positions in the room.

For context, I typically use my Apple TV with a stereo pair of full-size HomePod (2nd generation) speakers. I’ve spent some time testing the new four-speaker surround sound setup by adding a pair of HomePod mini 2 speakers.

My house’s layout isn’t particularly well-suited for a traditional surround sound setup, but to my admittedly untrained ears, this sounds great. I can’t speak to how it compares to a dedicated surround sound system, but I appreciate Apple continuing to expand what’s possible with HomePods and Apple TV.

One of the biggest changes with the new Apple TV 4K is the addition of three far-field microphones. This means you can now interact with Siri without picking up the Siri Remote. In conjunction with this, Siri AI is also coming to Apple TV.

Siri AI brings the same broad world knowledge and personal-context capabilities you’ve experienced on iPhone and HomeView, but adapts them to finding and watching content on your television. Siri AI on Apple TV requires the owner of your Apple Home setup to have an Apple Intelligence-enabled iPhone or iPad.

Personal context requests also require a paired compatible device connected to the same Wi-Fi network as Apple TV.

What was that TV show Lily recommended a few weeks ago?

What are some baseball movies I might like?

What did the vet say after Blake’s vet appointment?

For each example, tvOS responded with a dedicated interface on Apple TV, showing the answer along with poster art, cast information, and more. It also offers built-in contextual suggestions when you ask for recommendations, so you can refine the results further.

You can also ask Siri contextual questions about the show or movie you’re watching. This includes things like “where was this show filmed?” and “when is the next season coming out?”

I’ve found Siri AI’s responses to those contextual questions less accurate. It struggles to accurately identify the show or movie that’s playing, leading to completely inaccurate responses to those types of questions.

It’s worth noting that Siri AI is also coming to the second-generation Apple TV 4K from 2021 and the third-generation model from 2022. The far-field microphones are what’s exclusive to the new hardware. This lets you shout Siri commands into the air without talking directly into the Siri Remote.

In my testing, hands-free Siri works relatively well, and it’s convenient to control Apple TV without reaching for the remote. It does a good job at picking up commands

I can’t go any further in this review without talking about the price. The Apple TV 4K (4th generation) costs $299. That’s a huge increase over previous Apple TV models. Here’s a brief history of Apple TV 4K pricing over the years to set the stage:

After Apple introduced the $129 Apple TV model in October 2022, I started to notice a change. More and more people had Apple TVs when I would visit. Friends and family who had previously bought an Amazon or Roku box, or just used their TV’s built-in software, had purchased an Apple TV.

At $299, I worry that type of adoption is going to come to a screeching halt. And it’s unfortunate because the Apple TV is so much better than anything else out there. tvOS is fast, clean, and relatively free of the clutter and advertisements you find on competing platforms. The Siri Remote is excellent, and the integration with the rest of Apple’s ecosystem is hard to beat.

I could justify the $299 price for the Apple TV 4K (4th generation) if it were just one option available in a broader lineup. If there were lower-priced options for people who don’t need all the bells and whistles. That’s not the case, though. In fact, this is the first Apple TV 4K generation to be sold in just one configuration. There’s no cheaper version with less storage or fewer features.

To Apple’s credit, these are devices that can last for years. The fact that I’m still using an Apple TV 4K from 2017 makes the $299 price a tiny bit easier to swallow, but it doesn’t really change my frustration.

Apple seems serious about its new smart home strategy. There’s the HomeView smart display, a new HomePod mini, and the Apple Home Collection family of smart home accessories launching later this year. One of the best ways to get deeper into people’s homes is by controlling how they watch TV. I can’t help but think pricing the Apple TV 4K out of reach for so many people will hinder those ambitions.

Don’t get me wrong, I love the Apple TV. I have three of them in my house. If one of them broke, I would reluctantly pay $299 for a new one. Whether you fall into that category is for you to decide. All I can say is that the new Apple TV 4K is the best streamer on the market.

Tom Hanks’ ‘You’ve Got Mail’ Officially Getting a Reboot

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Tom Hanks was back in the big-budget box office race earlier this year, after a difficult run of theatrical releases. As his famous, genre-breaking cowboy Woody, Hanks returned alongside Buzz (Tim Allen), Jessie (Joan Cusack), and the rest of the gang in Toy Story 5. After a mixed response to the fourth installment, Toy Story 5 was a return to form for Pixar's most important franchise.

Collider's Taylor Gates gave the movie an 8/10 rating in her review, saying, "The impressive fifth installment not only justifies its existence but feels like a necessary response to how much the world has changed in the past three decades." This was one of several near-perfect responses to the film, which no doubt helped it on its way past the $1 billion barrier at the global box office, currently ranking as the year's third-highest-grossing film. As Hanks fans bask in the glory of a successful return to form for the actor, they will rejoice further, as it has been confirmed that one of his most endearing movies is being given the reboot treatment.

Officially, Hanks' 1998 rom-com favorite, You've Got Mail, is being rebooted at Warner Bros. Jason Orley is attached to direct, 17 years after he was Nancy Meyers' assistant whilst working on the film It's Complicated. Orley is also reportedly working on a script that is based on a previous take by Deborah Kaplan and Harry Elfont, with Neal Moritz and Tania Landau set to produce under the Original Film banner. Sadly, this is about all we know so far, with plot details being kept tightly under wraps.

Four love stories, four completely different ideas of what it means to be somebody's person. This is the one that matches how you actually fall, and what it would ask of you.

You do not fall for strangers. You fall for the person you have already been talking to for years, which is the slowest and by some distance the most durable way anybody has found to do this.

You want a partner, not an audience. Somebody who wants their own life as badly as you want yours, and who would never ask you to be smaller to make the relationship easier.

The intensity was never the problem. The secrecy is. You are drawn to a love that lives entirely in private, and to the particular ache of wanting somebody you are not supposed to want.

You do not do this cautiously. When it happens for you it is immediate and total and it rearranges everything, and you would not accept a smaller version of it.

After successfully teaming up on 1993's Sleepless in Seattle, director Nora Ephron and stars Hanks and Meg Ryan reunited for 1998's You've Got Mail to even greater box office success. Against a reported production budget of $65 million, the film returned more than $250 million worldwide, split between a domestic haul of $115 million and a further $135 million from overseas markets. The film came out during a golden period for Hanks, released in the same year as his critical darling Saving Private Ryan and just a year before the beloved Toy Story 2 and The Green Mile.

Leica Is Giving the M-A and M11-P Hammertone Special Edition Variants

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Leica Is Giving the M-A and M11-P Hammertone Special Edition Variants

Leica has announced a new special edition version of both the M-A and M11-P cameras that brings what the company describes as a classic design that it also rarely uses: hammertone.

“Since the 1930s, the hammertone finish has symbolized the combination of durability and technical aesthetics. This distinctive texture is formed during the drying process when metal pigments and special additives create subtle variations in surface tension. In industrial design, particularly in Germany, this finish has come to symbolize functionality and reliability,” Leica says.

While it might be somewhat common generally in Germany, that’s not the case for Leica cameras – at least modern ones. It has appeared in the past, such as from the Leica MD documentation camera for the Leitz Reprovit system to the exceptionally rare Leica MD (of which only ten were produced in 1964), but it’s certainly unusual.

“With their distinctive finish, both variants draw inspiration from rare Leica special editions, including the 1964 Leica MD Hammertone, the 2003 MP LHSA, the 2011 Leica M9 Hammertone, and the 2016 Leica M-A Vienna Edition. The new Leica M-A Hammertone and the new Leica M11-P Hammertone continue this lineage in their own unique way, thereby adding two exceptional, valuable variants to the Leica M portfolio,” Leica says.

Leica says it has reworked the process of applying the pattern for these special editions to achieve a uniform finish of the texture across the entire camera.

Both the M-A and the M11-P have the hammertone finish on the top plate and base plate. It is made from solid brass with a cross knurling on the control elements and a shiny silver shutter-release plate. The cameras also have a black leatherette covering in a vulcanite style with white engravings.

The Leica M11-P Hammertone also has newly rounded, classically styled edges on the top plate and base plate to go with the hammertone texture, as well as a new version of the screw on the front featuring a silver-chrome finish.

This pattern has the special distinction of making every camera unique due to the way it is applied.

Both the Leica M-A Hammertone and the Leica M11-P Hammertone will be available starting on November 12 from Leica Stores, the Leica Online Store, and authorized dealers. The retail price for the Leica M-A Hammertone is $8,000, while the Leica M11-P Hammertone costs $10,950.

The Woven Tote Bag Trend Is Taking Over Runways and Workdays

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Woven bags have officially reached icon status.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Is there any other bag that works as hard in real life as the tote bag? When it comes to being on duty and looking good, probably not. The humble tote has come in all iterations: lightweight cotton, supple leather, and suede. But more recently, the runways have been obsessed with making the oversize tote-style bags in luscious jumbo woven iterations. We saw them on the spring/summer 2027 runways at Dries Van Noten, Courrèges, Bottega Veneta, and Altuzarra. And then, labels like Bembien, Naghedi, and Dragon Diffusion are pumping out woven totes like there’s no tomorrow.

A big woven bag offers up texture and interest in a sea of ubiquitous canvas logo totes. It also gives you a bit of resilience so you don’t have to baby your bag on, say, a subway commute or a brisk morning walk where the risk of bumping into others is second nature. It is the working girl’s best friend. That’s just one of the reasons why fashion retailers have fallen head over heels for the style lately.

“Regardless of lifestyle, everyone needs a bag that carries a lot, and potentially the kitchen sink,” Ryan Kleman, Moda Operandi’s VP of buying, accessories, tells ELLE. The great news about a woven bag is that it also seamlessly goes with everything. Tone it down for a day at the office with a button-down top or a casual dress, or take it out for a nice dinner; a silk scarf tied about the strap can be the pop of color or embellishment you might crave for a more formal occasion.

In the case of some of these brands, the woven tote is literal fashion history. Bottega Veneta, for example, first introduced its signature “Intrecciato” woven leather technique in 1975. The fashion house’s spring/summer 2027 collection modernized the technique by applying it to enormous, hulking bags that swayed when worn over the shoulder. Paired with layered sweaters and matching leather belts, the bags felt like a decidedly fresh take on wearable artisanship by creative director Louise Trotter.

In the case of Dries Van Noten, one model carried an absolutely huge woven raffia-effect foldover clutch. Styled with a heather gray hoodie rolled up to reveal leather bubblegum-pink opera gloves with a biker-style cutout and buckle at the hand, the bag itself proved just how versatile a woven piece can be.

If you’re indeed thinking of upgrading your everyday work bag for a woven tote, it’s better to think bigger. “The oversized scale of the bags and the woven construction provide the perfect details to convert a chore into a fashion statement,” says Kleman. “At Moda, we have dubbed this trend the Big A** Bag and our customers are loving it.” This, too, is true: the bigger the weave, the better the carryall.

Tested: The V-12 Mercedes-Maybach S680 Is Too Much, Which Is Just Right

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

The Maybach is a throwback rich with modern comforts, and for those with the dough, it’s a deal.

Some things are meant for showing off: engagement rings, watches, fancy manicures, face tattoos. If nobody notices, what was the point of spending all that money? Other purchases are more subtle, like a well-cut jacket or a youthful neck. They make you look rich, but nobody can quite pinpoint why. The 2027 Mercedes-Maybach S680 is the first kind. There’s no modesty to the Maybach, but as a symbol of prosperity, it won’t be ignored.

Maybach models are for buyers who think the S-class is nice but could make do with more. More bespoke badging, more unique colorways—and more cylinders, at least for lucky shoppers in Asia, the Middle East, and here in the United States. We get the option of a twin-turbocharged 6.0-liter V-12, a gem rarer than a diamond in these mini-mill times. Maybach also offers the S580 with a twin-turbo 4.0-liter V-8, which is far more sensible, quicker, and more fuel efficient . . . but nobody should shop a Maybach with any of those descriptors in mind.

See all results for used cars for sale near 06704

The Maybach is nearly 18 feet of two-tone paint and illuminated callouts. The front grille is a waterfall of chrome flanked by lacy insets of repeated Maybach logos. The LED headlights are three-pointed stars surrounded by rose gold. Even the Maybach badges on the C-pillars glow to ensure that at no time, day or night, will anyone fail to notice what you’re driving.

And people do notice. At a gas station stop, a man in a Honda called over from the opposite pump, “Hey, that’s some hard work right there.” If I had purchased the car myself, I would have felt proud to have my effort recognized. As it was, I admitted to stolen valor, but I still drove off floating on air. That wasn’t from the compliment, though; the Maybach uses a new air-spring suspension that can anticipate bumps and potholes using cloud-based data from other Mercedes cars on the same road for real-time predictive damping.

With its long, 133.7-inch wheelbase and outrageously kitted-out back seat (we’ll get to that), it’s obvious that the S680 was designed with rear passengers in mind, but it’s so fun to drive that I’m tempted to take up a chauffeur job. The V-12 is as smooth as a beurre blanc and as meaty as the filet beneath it. It doesn’t move out with the instant response of a V-8 or an electric setup, but such haste is unseemly. Instead, it steps out with authority and builds up steam like a locomotive pulling a private railcar. The only stumble it makes is when the stop-start system kicks the V-12 back into action. Unlike with the V-8’s 48-volt assist, a noticeable shudder accompanies the return of the 12-pot’s combustion. Thankfully, it’s a problem easily solved by deactivating the system with a single push of a button. It’s not like you’re getting good fuel economy with this monster anyway.

See all results for used cars for sale near 06704

The S680 is a thirsty machine, with an EPA combined fuel-economy estimate of 14 mpg. Our test car was a mossy silver and white two-tone with a sage leather interior, and that’s the only thing green about it. At least you’ll get lots of compliments at every single gas station.

The steering and its optional rear-axle assist makes the long car turn in a relatively small circle, and the brakes (15.4-inch ventilated and cross-drilled rotors in the front, 14.2-inch ventilated units in the back) do a heroic job of stopping all 5336 pounds. At the test track—where technical editor Dan Edmunds would not let me ride in the back during testing despite my assertion that it would be more accurate to the car’s use case—the Maybach accelerated to 60 mph in 4.5 seconds. At the world’s fanciest drag strip, it would cross the quarter-mile mark in 12.6 seconds at 118 mph. Edmunds brought it back down to zero from 70 mph in 177 feet without a hint of brake fade. Most impressive, the S680 whipped up 0.90 g of grip around the skidpad. Not bad for something my neighbor Shirley described as “bigger and nicer than my whole house.”

The Maybach is not actually bigger than Shirley’s house, but she doesn’t have a leather-covered massaging recliner. The S680 has four. Our test car was equipped with the $6000 Executive Rear Seat Package Plus, among many other extras, and be forewarned that if you invite neighbors, gym buddies, or your hair stylist to check out the leather-covered footrests, Maybach-branded pillows, yacht-striped wooden console, folding lunch trays, and back-seat wine cooler with champagne-flute-specific cup holders, they might be unwilling to vacate the premises. I thought I might have to give the car back with a full crew inside.

The Maybach is excessive. It’s not about providing a quiet space or restrained elegance. Every surface that isn’t covered in leather or wood is an interactive screen. The Maybach gets Benz’s theater-wide Superscreen with a 12.3-inch driver display that merges with a 14.4-inch center screen and a 12.3-inch passenger display. Everything glows, or is gold, or is both golden and glowing. In some of Mercedes’s other cars, the Superscreen feels tacky, like a Times Square billboard, but among the lights and quilted details of the Maybach, it’s just one more sparkly surface.

The screen is the main control center for all the electronic luxuries, which Maybach is equally generous about providing. Driver-assistance technologies include all the usual crash-avoidance systems and multi-view cameras, plus parking assist with wheel rim protection (something you’ll want if, like our car, your example has $1300 worth of 21-inch wheels). While the assists can feel overbearing, like a too-attentive waiter hovering over your table with the crumb scraper, the cameras and sensors make parking and maneuvering this massive car much less stressful.

At $291,600 as tested, the V-12 Maybach is not going to be on everyone’s shopping list, but in the elite scene of ultra-high-end taxicabs, it’s a bargain compared to offerings from marques like Rolls-Royce or Bentley. As a bold statement, the Maybach announces your arrival loud and clear, from all 12 cylinders.

Like a sleeper agent activated late in the game, Elana Scherr didn’t know her calling at a young age. Like many girls, she planned to be a vet-astronaut-artist, and came closest to that last one by attending UCLA art school. She painted images of cars, but did not own one. Elana reluctantly got a driver’s license at age 21 and discovered that she not only loved cars and wanted to drive them, but that other people loved cars and wanted to read about them, which meant somebody had to write about them. Since receiving activation codes, Elana has written for numerous car magazines and websites, covering classics, car culture, technology, motorsports, and new-car reviews. In 2020, she received a Best Feature award from the Motor Press Guild for the C/D story "A Drive through Classic Americana in a Polestar 2."  In 2023, her Car and Driver feature story "In Washington, D.C.'s Secret Carpool Cabal, It's a Daily Slug Fest" was awarded 1st place in the 16th Annual National Arts & Entertainment Journalism Awards by the Los Angeles Press Club.

John Legend, Lynn Nottage, and Whitney White on Reclaiming an American Classic From a Black Perspective

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

The three artists have teamed up on a musical adaptation of Imitation of Life

In 2020, against the dual backdrops of the isolating Covid-19 lockdown and the righteous fury of Black Lives Matter protests erupting across the country, John Legend and Lynn Nottage began exchanging messages. Though the EGOT-achieving musician and the two-time Pulitzer Prize–winning playwright couldn’t meet in person, the pair quietly became collaborators on a long-gestating project, now finally reaching the stage: a musical adaptation of Imitation of Life.

For nearly a century, Imitation of Life—which began as a 1933 best-selling novel by Fannie Hurst, became a John M. Stahl melodrama the following year, then was adapted for the screen again by Douglas Sirk in 1959—has been a cinematic fixture in Black American households, though not an uncontroversial one. Stahl’s iconic telling centers on Bea Pullman, a white widow and single mother, who takes in Delilah, a Black single mother, as a maid in exchange for housing her and her light-skinned daughter, Peola (Sarah Jane in Sirk’s version). When Bea boxes Delilah’s family pancake recipe and puts a caricature of her likeness on the package—an unmistakable nod to Aunt Jemima—she becomes a wealthy businesswoman. But Delilah, content simply to care for Bea and her daughter, turns down any share of the money. Meanwhile, Peola, seduced by her adjacency to whiteness, wrenchingly disavows her mother to pursue a life of passing.

As Sterling Brown’s scathing and influential 1935 review for Opportunity: A Journal of Negro Life made plain, the novel and film are mired in racist tropes. Yet despite its flaws, the story has continued capturing Black audiences, perhaps for the complex humanity clearly latent within Delilah’s and Peola’s characters. Now the story is being retold in a limited-run production opening December 3 at the Shed in New York, this time with a fully realized Delilah as the lead and an all-Black chief creative team at its core. Nottage wrote the book, while Legend—who also recently released his 11th studio album, Muse—composed the entire score. Whitney White, of Jaja’s African Hair Braiding and Liberation, directs, with Adrienne Warren starring as Delilah and Jessica Vosk as Bea. Leading up to rehearsals for the show, Nottage, Legend, and White—so in demand that she was pulled into a production meeting midconversation—took time to discuss their own relationships to Imitation of Life, their experience working together, and why the story’s themes remain as relevant as ever.

Lynn Nottage: I come from a generation where both films, Imitation of Life from the 1930s and 1950s, were fairly ubiquitous on television. I ended up seeing the films probably once or twice a year and found myself in sort of a tense, love-hate relationship with Imitation of Life, in that I was frustrated by the treatment of Delilah and Peola in 1934, then once again by Delilah and Sarah Jane in the 1950s. And yet I was fascinated by the racial politics and this young woman who had a really angsty relationship with her own identity. So I felt, when I was invited to work on this, that I had an opportunity to delve deeper into the character of Delilah and right some wrongs. She is so fascinating; she’s a mother, she’s an entrepreneur, she’s a woman who defies the expectations of the time. And yet throughout history, she has not been given her just deserts.

John Legend: I didn’t grow up knowing this film, but my parents and their generation know it very well. So when I told them that I was involved in this musical, they were so excited. And it made me realize how important this work was in our culture. But like Lynn, it was a fun challenge to reimagine the storytelling, where Delilah was centered, her emotionality was centered, her humanity was centered, and Peola’s humanity was centered as well.

And then, of course, I’ve never written a musical before. So part of the joy and educational experience that I’ve gotten from doing this is working with people who have so much brilliance and experience and understanding of a medium that I didn’t bring to the table. It was the perfect introduction, for me, to writing a musical. It’s been such a welcoming and edifying way to enter into this new branch of my career.

Nottage: John is a natural in this medium. It’s almost as if he had gotten a master’s degree in musical-theater writing. I think that, John, you have so many musicals in you and the world is going to be blown away.

Legend: Thank you. It really did make me think, why hadn’t I done this before?

Whitney White: For me, I was raised by a single mom, and she raised me at great personal cost—spiritual cost—in Chicago, on her own. So the 1930s film, I oddly was more familiar with it because I liked it more. I would sit with my grandmother when she was having her coffee, like, at whatever-o’clock in the morning, because neither of us would ever sleep. And for some reason, I attached myself to the 1930s film. The visuals of that film are so iconic. It’s like the foundation of Americana as it exists in global culture is visualized in that film. But also the story of Delilah echoes in so many ways. It’s almost like touching a stove that’s too hot for me. Because I’ve watched, my whole life, the world not see my mother in this way: her brilliance, her intelligence, her spunk, her grit. So I just knew that I could bring something to it. But I also knew that John and Lynn were going to give that character the eulogy and the eternal life that she’s always deserved.

Legend: Yeah, I think because of Lynn’s perspective and storytelling, we see other sides of Delilah. We see her being romantic. We see her being a brilliant businessperson, making a way out of no way—something that a lot of our ancestors, especially, had to do. They did what they had to do to realize the American dream.

Nottage: At the core, this is a story about the American dream, with all of its contradictions and complications. It’s a story about motherhood; it’s a story about friendship, but it’s also a story about passing. It’s what we are forced to do to negotiate and survive in a world that is sexist, in a world that is racist. Why, in America, do we constantly have to make compromises in order to reach for the things that we desire? Those sacrifices, most often, people of color have to make, women have to make. We are often asked to reshape our identities to fit into some sort of template that’s not necessarily comfortable for the shapes and forms of our bodies and our ideas and our identities. I think that’s why we keep coming back to this story, because the struggle remains real.

White: And I think, in the midst of having to constantly reshape our identities and bodies as Black folk, at the same time, we got the goods. We make culture. Here you have this character who saves this business, and what does she make? Delicious food that is worth millions of dollars. So while we’re forced to be at the bottom of the totem pole, we also make the goods that make capitalism work. That’s something I’ve really been sitting with: We’re so often plundered and taken from the most, and yet we are the foundation of capitalism.

Nottage: Can I ask you, Whitney, you’re looking particularly luminous today. Where are you going?

White: Girl, I’m sweaty. I just walked my child [to school]. My child is very heavy. I have this giant toddler; maybe he’s going to be LeBron. He didn’t want to walk to school, so he made me push him in his stroller.

Legend: I met your husband last week, so I see where he got it from.

Nottage: I totally digress, but I was like, “Look at you, you have, like, a rosy green glow.” [Laughs]

White: [Laughs] That beat that just happened is so beautiful because it’s so indicative of the show. Like, we’re talking about this heavy stuff, and then we all have this beautiful laugh. When I read the text and hear the music, it’s like I go through 10 cycles of emotions. I laugh, I cry, I fall in love, then I laugh and cry and fall in love again.

Legend: One of my favorite things from the workshops is watching the music come to life. You have all these amazing performers embodying all the things that I wrote in my little piano room at home.

Nottage: Theater is often so difficult and fraught to make because it is collaborative. I think one of the things that’s been really lovely about this process is the ease with which we, as collaborators, are capable of having conversations, because there’s so much mutual respect and appreciation. The bottom line in making a musical is that trust is necessary. It’s a tremendous leap of faith.

White [as she is pulled into production meeting]: I just want to add one last thing. Oh God, I don’t want to get too emotional. Never in my wildest dreams did I imagine that I would be sitting here with two of my favorite living artists. When I started in this business, this kind of makeup for a musical team was impossible. I can’t tell you how much pride and excitement that brings me, to be working alongside John and Lynn.

Nottage: Thank you. We love you, Whitney.

White: I’m going to go lovingly yell at people—lovingly, lovingly. Bye.

Legend: Our writing process started—we were meeting during Covid, so a lot of our first interactions were on Zoom. Lynn would send me pages with story dialogue and possible lyrical ideas. I would sit in my piano room and sketch out an idea with maybe a couple lyrics, but mostly just mumbling musical ideas around the theme.

Legend: One of the things that happens more in musical theater than I’m used to is the revision process and the workshopping process. … I’ve definitely done more revision in this show than I’ve ever done in my pop-music career.

Nottage: John, do you remember one of the very first conversations that we had? I said, “We’re probably going to write that opening-number song at least 15 times.” You’re like, “Oh, I don’t think so.” Here we are!

Legend: Here we are. We’ve rewritten it multiple times. [Laughs] I don’t have an extensive knowledge bank of musicals that I’ve seen and been a part of. So it was interesting, thinking about how to write for one, because part of it was like, “Well, let me just bring my own musical experience in the church and popular music and, you know, singing in a jazz a cappella group and singing show tunes in the choir in high school.” I just brought my own experience and said, “Let me just write the songs that I can be proud of and that I believe will work.” And then Lynn really taught me how to incorporate the storytelling into the songs.

Nottage: It’s so interesting to hear you say that, John, because I think one of the things that made the process so smooth is that you are a natural-born storyteller. There’s something that you understand instinctively and fundamentally about dramaturgy that I didn’t have to explain to you. I would venture to say that perhaps musicals are the hardest form of theater-making just because it is so layered.

But I also want to circle back to something that you said earlier, John, about the beginning of the process. It was Covid, and we were all thinking and making in isolation. There was something really lovely about reaching across the country to John and having these conversations about a book, even though we didn’t really, at that point, know each other. We spent a lot of time talking about ideas. It was absolutely thrilling. When I got that first mumble, I was like, “Oh my God, it’s John Legend mumbling, and it still sounds beautiful.” It was a song that we never, ultimately, ended up using. I think that eventually you should do an album of the outtakes.

Nottage: I just want to add, because we’ve been talking about some heavy themes, that the musical is not delivering spinach. The musical embodies all of the complexities of a beautiful woman like Delilah, a beautiful woman like Bea, a beautiful woman like Peola. It really is, in some ways, a celebration. It is a love letter. And it is a gift to be able to be in dialogue with these women.

Misha &amp; Puff’s First Womenswear Collection Gives Knitwear a New Point of View

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

The cult-favorite children’s knitwear brand is officially expanding to adults.

Women’s fashion has some of the best designs out there—full stop. That said, every now and then I see a well-dressed kid and think, “Damn, I wish I had something like that.” This is especially true when I see a little one sporting a design from none other than Misha & Puff, a brand known for its ethically crafted, handmade, vintage-inspired children’s clothes. And while my wardrobe carries a playful touch, I’m not necessarily looking for a supersized version of a child’s sweater, but rather the essence of that whimsy. Thankfully, I don’t have to look too far; Misha & Puff just launched its first-ever women’s collection—and it’s more than I could have hoped for.

“Knits are hot. Knits are sexy. The way that they drape, the way that they accentuate your neck, I really do feel like we can do a lot with a knit,” Sophia Roe told ELLE.com over the phone. “For me, I feel really beautiful when I put these clothes on.” The James Beard Award-winning chef—along with her daughter, Mizu—came together with artist Mara Hoffman behind the lens to create a campaign that captures the essence of Misha & Puff, highlighting the craftsmanship and expression of the brand’s new womenswear collection.

“Everything I do with the brand is very authentic and really about what I’m interested in...That’s how the kids’ brand started, with me having my son and then growing up,” Misha & Puff’s founder, Anna Wallack, said on a phone call. “The choice to launch women’s in a bigger way really comes from the pull that I’m feeling as a woman. For me, dressing women and dressing myself is about how we’re working these pieces together. How do I feel in these silhouettes? Who is this character today? I want to keep playing with that in a bigger way.”

Wallack went on to tell me that designing for the collection—which launched today—started with what she described as a “fiber-first” approach. “We work with a Pima cotton in Peru that worked so well for kids because it’s incredibly soft and it’s so wearable, and then to find that I want to wear that all the time for myself; I want to wear our merino wool,” she explained. “Our decision to launch women’s with a bigger collection really was a commitment to doing it instead of just making one sweater here and there.” The result? A range of expressive knits in a color palette reminiscent of the ’70s that encourage the wearer to be who she is as a woman today. With every solid- or heather-hued piece there’s another featuring an eye-catching print or embroidery detail. “I feel a little more permission to explore other ideas in the adult collection and what it means to be a women, too.”

This exploration extends into Misha & Puff’s campaign. Roe shared on a phone call that the idea of partnering with Misha & Puff stemmed from just being a fan of the brand. “I said, ‘I don’t know what we can do but I really want to do something with Anna.’ Little did I know that in the background she was working on this unbelievable adult collection,” she said. Though this isn’t the first project Roe has starred in with her daughter, she explained it was the first where she was shot by another mother, making the experience feel truly special—especially as she comes into her body 15 months postpartum. “I feel kind of hot as a mom. There are a lot of narratives out there about getting back to whatever that pre-baby thing is, and I just kind of rebuke that in every single way. I think what I am now is kind of amazing and I really do feel like Mara was able to bring that out in a way that no one else could have.”

On the vision for the campaign, Hoffman shared that the focus was “seeing how does a woman exist through a space or on the street, and how does she live in it?” Working to capture Roe in a documentarian way, the artist wanted to make sure the imagery felt authentic to both the brand and the people wearing it. “We are lucky to be able to capture little shots of that along the way.” Of course, shooting with a baby can come with its own unpredictability. Luckily, as Roe shared, working with someone like Hoffman helped her relax on set with the reminder, “This is just life.”

There May Be a Point in Life When Stress Ages You Fastest. The Pandemic Just Helped Us Find It.

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Epigenetic clocks suggest puberty is a pivotal window when stress leaves a deeper mark on our DNA.

On New Year’s Eve 2019, health officials in Wuhan, China, reported a mysterious cluster of pneumonia cases. It was the first sign of what would become the years-long Covid-19 pandemic. Across the world, every facet of life was interrupted, and many societies, including a growing anti-vaccine movement in the U.S., continue to grapple with the pandemic’s legacy, not to mention the millions of loved ones lost to the virus.

However, a new study led by researchers at the Max Planck Institute in Germany suggests that the pandemic didn’t impact every age cohort equally—at least, according to four separate epigenetic clocks that measure age-related changes in DNA methylation patterns. This chemical process changes predictably as we age and is a standard measurement for gauging someone’s genetic age. The team got lucky with its timing. Just before the pandemic took hold in March 2020, a project called the TwinLife Epigenetic Change Satellite had collected DNA samples from children, adolescents, and young adults in Germany. It sampled them again through spring 2022.

Senior author Elisabeth Binder studies how genes and the environment shape psychiatric disorders. She and her colleagues tracked how each age group’s epigenetic clocks changed over time. They also looked at data on socioeconomic background and physical health to see how pandemic stress affected each group. At first glance, the data showed some expected correlations between certain groups and health outcomes.

“Across all age groups, stress was most significantly felt by those who were already at risk before the pandemic, whether due to poorer physical health, lower subjective well-being or lower socio-economic background” Binder said in a press statement.

Because stress is a well-known driver of aging and key driver of inflammation, it wasn’t surprising that all age groups showed accelerated aging during one of the most stressful global eras in recent memory. But when the researchers dug deeper, they found something surprising. Adolescents showed more stress-related epigenetic aging than either children or young adults. According to the study, this suggests that mid-to-late puberty may be a pivotal window for “subjective stress-related alterations.”

“This suggests that adolescence may be a time period that is especially sensitive to the effects of stress—this could be due to the changes happening in the body during puberty, or because lockdown measures, like restrictions on seeing peers, had a particularly strong impact on this age group,” Alicia Schowe, a post-doctoral researcher and lead author of the study, said in a press statement.

The authors say lifestyle changes during lockdowns, such as less physical activity and weight gain, may also have played a role. But those factors alone can’t explain why adolescents showed more epigenetic aging than the other age groups.

The findings are a reminder that human aging is complex—our biological age isn’t set by our genes alone. Events far beyond our control, like a global pandemic, can leave their mark too. As these young people grow further from the pandemic, researchers will keep following them to find out whether the years the virus added to their epigenetic clocks will fade or stay with them for life.

Darren lives in Portland, has a cat, and writes/edits about sci-fi and how our world works. You can find his previous stuff at Gizmodo and Paste if you look hard enough.

2028 Honda CR-V Will Go Hybrid-Only, Debut a New Platform

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

The new “Honda Hybrid” system is more efficient, increases power, and comes paired with an electric AWD setup.

We’re not done with 2026 quite yet, but the 2028 Honda CR-V is already coming in hot. On Thursday, the brand offered up its first official details about the next-gen version of the company’s highly important crossover—and big changes are in stow

First off, the 2028 model will be a complete redesign: underpinned by a new platform, boasting a fresh design inside and out, and powered by a new “Honda Hybrid” system. Not only is the hybrid new, but it will be the only powertrain choice available for the seventh-gen CR-V. This hybrid-only approach follows in the successful footsteps of the current Toyota RAV4, Honda’s biggest rival in the compact SUV segment.

When it comes to details, Honda is being purposely vague, but still giving us a few items to chew on. The new platform this CR-V will ride on increases body rigidity and reduces weight compared to the current car. It also has greater modularity built in, which Honda says will allow for greater parts commonality between models.

The Honda Hybrid powertrain itself will build on the two-motor hybrid system we’ve seen for years. Honda says it’s aiming for a 10-percent improvement to fuel efficiency gained by expanding the range where the engine operates most efficiently and an increase in the efficiency of the hybrid unit. More power is promised from the four-cylinder Atkinson cycle engine, and we’ll also see a new electric AWD system make its debut on this CR-V. Per usual, Honda is also claiming the CR-V will bring further enhancements to the “joy of driving,” which is always heartening to hear.

A single image of the new CR-V logo on a dark metallic blue paint was provided as a teaser. It’s rather edgy in appearance, far more so than the current logo’s font. We suspect this new sharper logo design will translate into the rest of the car, but Honda is holding back from showing us the SUV’s design for now.

This next-gen CR-V will continue to be built in North America with production spread out across three plants, two in the U.S. and one in Canada.

Next-Gen 2028 Honda CR-V Follows the Toyota RAV4 as a Hybrid-Only SUV

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Honda has confirmed that the next-generation CR-V will debut a new platform and a new two-motor hybrid system.

The current Honda CR-V can be had with or without a hybrid powertrain. However, when the compact SUV’s newest generation arrives sometime next year, it’ll only be available as a hybrid. Honda’s announcement comes a year after Toyota applied the same strategy with its new RAV4. And considering over half the CR-Vs Honda sells are hybrids, the move makes a lot of sense.

Apart from introducing a standard hybrid powertrain, the 2028 Honda CR-V will mark the start of the bestselling nameplate’s seventh generation. It will also introduce a next-gen platform, which the automaker claims will prioritize stiffness and lightness; its modular nature will promote parts sharing too. While we’re still eagerly waiting for Honda to release more details about its next CR-V, the automaker has shared some breadcrumbs about its hybrid powertrain.

See all results for new 2028 Honda CR-V for sale near 80031

The new fifth generation of Honda’s two-motor hybrid system will once again pair with an Atkinson-cycle four-cylinder gas engine. We don’t yet know how much power the combo will make, but Honda says it “will greatly increase the standard power and efficiency.” When it comes to efficiency, the next-gen hybrid system is targeting an improvement of over 10 percent compared with the outgoing version. For reference, the current CR-V hybrid makes 205 horsepower, and it’s rated at up to 38 mpg combined.

We expect the 2028 Honda CR-V to be revealed sometime later this year. Honda says it’ll be the first of several hybrid models that it plans to launch by 2030.

Eric Stafford’s automobile addiction began before he could walk, and it has fueled his passion to write news, reviews, and more for Car and Driver since 2016. His aspiration growing up was to become a millionaire with a Jay Leno–like car collection. Apparently, getting rich is harder than social-media influencers make it seem, so he avoided financial success entirely to become an automotive journalist and drive new cars for a living. After earning a journalism degree at Central Michigan University and working at a daily newspaper, the years of basically burning money on failed project cars and lemon-flavored jalopies finally paid off when Car and Driver hired him. His garage currently includes a 2010 Acura RDX, a manual '97 Chevy Camaro Z/28, and a '90 Honda CRX Si.

Upwind launches Response as AI-powered exploits outrun the patch cycle

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Upwind Response cuts off an attacker’s access to the targeted service in seconds, without waiting for a person to act. Security teams choose what it handles alone, and every action can be rolled back.

Nearly one in four vulnerabilities attacked in the first half of 2026 was exploited on or before the day its CVE went public. That single figure explains why Upwind, the Cloud and AI security platform, has introduced Upwind Response, a capability designed to stop AI-driven attacks within seconds and keep intruders from going any further into a cloud environment.

The announcement, made on November 12, 2026, reflects a shift in how quickly defenders need to act. Waiting for a disclosure, a patch, and a maintenance window is a sequence that assumes attackers will wait too. Increasingly, they do not.

For years, vulnerability management followed a familiar rhythm. A flaw was disclosed, teams scanned for it, and patches were scheduled. That rhythm depended on a gap between disclosure and exploitation. When a quarter of attacked vulnerabilities are exploited on or before publication, the gap effectively disappears for a large share of real-world threats.

That head start is what allowed patch programs to work. Without it, even a well-run program can find itself reacting after exploitation has already begun.

AI has accelerated this compression. AI agents can now probe an environment from hundreds of angles and chain dozens of small weaknesses into a breach. They can work around the clock to achieve success. A human attacker might give up after a few dead ends. An automated one simply keeps trying.

JPMorgan Chase put the problem plainly in a recent post on AI and security, writing:

“Vulnerability discovery and attack automation are moving at warp speed. Remediation is not.”

That mismatch is the market condition Upwind is responding to. Finding risks and detecting attacks remain the foundation of cloud security. But when a new flaw can be used within hours, a scan-and-patch cycle cannot close the window quickly enough. Backlogs of risky settings and unused permissions continue to pile up in the meantime. Each one is another potential entry point.

The result is a difficult position for defenders. Many know where their weaknesses are, yet fixing them takes longer than attackers need to use them.

The obvious answer would be to let software fix problems automatically. Many security teams have resisted that approach. Their worry has been that false positives or over-reaching responses would create a different set of ongoing problems, with legitimate traffic blocked and business services interrupted.

Upwind’s approach is built to get past that hesitation by being precise about what it touches and by keeping every action reversible.

That matters for adoption. A tool that teams trust enough to leave switched on provides far more protection than one held back because the risk of disruption feels too high.

Upwind Response unifies signals across cloud, runtime, code, and identity to understand the full picture of a threat. When Upwind sees an attacker try to exploit a cloud application, Response cuts off that attacker’s access to the targeted service and stops the kill chain, without waiting for a person to act.

The first line of action is a network block. When Response spots malicious activity, such as an attempt to exploit a vulnerability or to take remote control of a server, it blocks the attacker’s access point from reaching the specific service. This happens at the cloud network level and takes effect in seconds, while normal customer traffic keeps flowing.

That combination of speed and narrow targeting is what separates Response from a blunt shutdown.

Response is built to escalate in proportion to the threat:

The network block stops the attacker’s access point from reaching the targeted service.

If the attacker gets further, Response can terminate a specific malicious process and keep it from starting again, avoiding large disruptions to regular operations.

Response can trim a compromised machine’s cloud identity so that it loses access to object storage and databases.

As a last resort, Response isolates the machine completely, taking it off the internet and the rest of the network while keeping a secure tunnel into it.

Each level targets a narrower slice of the environment than a broad shutdown would, which is how Response limits collateral impact on the business.

The more forceful options remain available, but they are not where Response starts.

Security teams decide which threats Response mitigates on its own and which need their approval. Organizations can start with approvals required before Response acts, then let it act autonomously as confidence builds. Every action is temporary and visible, and teams can easily roll it back.

That flexibility allows each organization to set its own pace rather than adopting full automation on day one.

Amiram Shachar, Co-Founder and CEO of Upwind, tied the launch directly to the speed of today’s attackers.

“Finding risk will always matter, but against attackers that move at machine speed, defense has to be active,” Shachar said. “Because Upwind sees what is actually happening on the servers running our customers’ applications, we know exactly which address sent the attack and which service it hit. That precision is what makes automatic blocking safe. Every blocked attempt costs the attacker time and money, while our customers’ business keeps running.”

Response also contributes to a wider defensive effort. Every attack is cataloged, anonymized, and added to the knowledge of the Upwind Platform. This allows every Upwind customer to achieve better security through the collective experience of their peers.

Where AI agents can test the same techniques against many targets, that shared knowledge gives defenders a way to benefit from attacks seen elsewhere.

That value compounds over time, as the platform’s knowledge grows with each attack it records.

Upwind Response is part of the Upwind Cloud & AI Security Platform and builds on the detections customers already rely on. Admins can enable it today within the console. Because Response uses cloud- and AI-native primitives, there is no extra firewall to deploy.

Upwind Response is available today for all Upwind customers. More information and demo requests are available at https://www.upwind.io/response.

Disney's 'Star Wars' is coming to Cosm domes ahead of 50th anniversary as part of multiyear film deal

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

On Thursday, the immersive entertainment technology company announced a new studio partnership with The Walt Disney Company that will bring the original "Star Wars" to its domes early next year as part of a multiyear, multifilm collaboration.

The financial terms of the deal were not disclosed.

Cosm's massive concave screens will drop film fans into a galaxy far, far away starting in March, just ahead of the Academy Award-winning film's 50th anniversary in May.

"We are thrilled to add the Disney partnership," Alexis Scalice, head of Cosm Studios, told CNBC. "We do look for those opportunities of these like anniversary and commemorative moments. We look for those opportunities to celebrate with the fans, but also find this mutually beneficial partnership between both parties."

This will be Cosm's sixth theatrical collaboration with Hollywood, having already partnered with Warner Bros. to bring "The Matrix," "Willy Wonka & the Chocolate Factory," "Harry Potter and the Sorcerer's Stone" and "It" to its locations. The company is set to launch immersive screenings of Universal's "Shrek" in December.

Each Cosm dome has stadium-style seating, with booths and tables set on each level. The dome's width fills much of the viewers' periphery, giving the sensation that they are part of the onscreen environment.

In this case, "Star Wars" will be displayed in a large rectangle in the center of the dome while the background is transformed into the iconic planets of Tatooine and Yavin IV that are featured in the film as well as the infamous Death Star. Audiences will also see elements that will make it appear like they're traveling at lightspeed and getting trapped in an intergalactic trash compactor.

Cosm is working directly with studios Lucasfilm and Industrial Light and Magic as well as creative studio MakeMake to create the visuals and the sound mixing for the unique presentation of the film.

"We look for those moments where sound, music ... listening to Darth Vader's breath for the first time ... you feel those moments differently in an 87-foot diameter dome and 12k resolution with the sound system we have," Scalise said. "And it's one of the things that with experiential cinema and our technology that we were so excited and honestly privileged that we can take these defining moments for fans and work closely with our partners that doesn't really change the authenticity of it, it just enhances it in a different manner."

The premium experience will also be paired with a themed menu. Cosm has done this with other titles, such as "Harry Potter and the Sorcerer's Stone," serving guests throughout the screening with house-made shepherd's pie, pretzel wands with cheese dip and a box of Bertie Bott's Every Flavour Beans.

Cosm is trying to establish itself as a destination for fandom — both for sports and for film. It has a wide variety of live event offerings, which it telecasts using proprietary cameras mounted in arenas that broadcast back to the dome, as well as its expanding library of movies.

"What I love is that we really are kind of building these Cosm communities built on fandom," Scalise said. "It's the No. 1 foundation for our business."

And Star Wars is a powerful fandom. The franchise remains one of the top-ranking pieces of intellectual property across film, television and retail annually, and Disney is gearing up for a massive celebration of its semicentennial including merchandise launches, theatrical re-releases, its biannual Star Wars Celebration convention and collaborations with other brands.
