Corsair HX1000i Shift Crystal power supply review: 1000W Platinum efficiency with an eye-catching transparent aesthetic

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

In-depth features, interviews and insider stories from the world of hardware.

Expert insights and analysis delivered to your inbox.

Dive into our proprietary testing data and compare hardware with detailed benchmarks.

Go beyond the headlines with expert reporting on the hardware industry.

Track upcoming CPUs, GPUs and tech releases before they arrive.

In-depth features, interviews and insider stories from the world of hardware.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Built by CWT, the HX1000i Shift Crystal is a showpiece that backs its looks with real substance. Efficiency is excellent, regulation and ripple are tight, and the fanless window covers half of its rated output at room temperature. Taiwanese capacitors, a noisy hot run and a 200 mm long chassis are the compromises, while the ten-year warranty and iCUE LINK integration add genuine value.

Why you can trust Tom's Hardware Our expert reviewers spend hours testing and comparing products and services so you can choose the best for you. Find out more about how we test.

Corsair needs very little introduction to anyone who has built a PC over the past two decades. Founded in California in 1994 as a manufacturer of memory modules, the company has since grown into one of the largest brands in the PC component and peripheral market, with a catalog that covers cases, cooling, keyboards, mice, streaming gear, and of course, power supplies. Corsair entered the PSU market in the second half of the 2000s and built its reputation there by choosing its OEM partners carefully rather than manufacturing in-house. Channel Well Technology (CWT) has been one of the most important of these partners, producing a large portion of the RMx and HXi units over the years. The HXi series in particular has long been Corsair's high-end, digitally monitored family, positioned just below the flagship AX models.

We take a closer look at the HX1000i Shift Crystal to see if it deserves a spot in our best power supplies list. The HX1000i Shift Crystal is a bit of an odd bird, and Corsair is clearly fine with that. First shown at Computex 2026 and launching on November 12th, it combines the side-mounted connector layout of the SHIFT models with the digital HXi platform, and then wraps everything in a chassis with clear polycarbonate panels on three sides, turning the internals of the PSU into a part of the build's aesthetics. Beneath the show, the platform is built by CWT. It holds a Cybenetics Platinum certification at both 115 VAC and 230 VAC, and it is fully compliant with the ATX 3.1 and PCIe 5.1 design guides. It also integrates an iCUE LINK System Hub and Corsair's PinProtect+ per-pin current monitoring for the native 12V-2x6 connector. The 1000W model that we are reviewing today is rated to deliver its full power output continuously at ambient temperatures up to 50°C and comes with a ten-year warranty.

The HX1000i Shift Crystal ships in a very large and sturdy black cardboard box, with a large render of the unit on the front that does a good job of showing off the transparent panels and the lit fan. The model name is printed in big yellow and white letters across the bottom half, while the badges in the top right corner - ATX 3.1 with PCIe 5.1 and 12V-2x6 support, the Cybenetics certifications, and the iCUE LINK logo- tell you most of what you need to know before even opening the package.

The bundle is rather spartan for a product of this class. Inside the box, we found the AC power cord, four mounting screws, a handful of black cable ties, and a leaflet with safety and compliance information. There are no Velcro straps, no cable combs beyond those already on the cables, and no other accessories of any kind. It is not a deal breaker by any means, but a premium unit like this that is focused on aesthetics could have been a little more generous in this department.

All of the cables are fully modular and black, with individually sleeved wires and low-profile cable combs. There is a 24-pin ATX cable, two 4+4-pin EPS cables, three PCIe cables carrying four 6+2-pin connectors in total, and a native 12V-2x6 cable rated for 600W, alongside two SATA and two Molex cables with four connectors each. Eight Molex connectors on a modern 1000W unit is extremely generous, frankly more than any reasonable build will ever need. Corsair also supplies two iCUE LINK cables of different lengths and a USB cable that connects the PSU's monitoring interface to the motherboard.

With dimensions of 150 x 86 x 200 mm, the HX1000i Shift Crystal is a long ATX unit, which is a non-issue if you are getting the matching Corsair 4500X case anyway. In any other case, you will need to check your case's specifications before buying one. The side-mounted SHIFT connectors also require some free space next to the PSU, as the cables plug into the side of the unit instead of its face, which is great for cases with a hidden PSU compartment but not suitable for every chassis. The design is unlike anything else on the market. The steel frame is black, with Y-shaped cutouts forming the fan grille and a debossed pattern of the same shape on the opposite panel, while three of the sides are clear polycarbonate panels that reveal the white fan frame, the bulk capacitors, the coils, and the white PCBs. With the RGB fan lit, the unit very much looks like a piece of display hardware rather than a power supply.

The rear of the unit is a little more conventional, with the AC receptacle, a rocker on/off switch marked with the "HX1000i SHIFT" logo, and a large perforated area with the same Y-shaped cutouts for the exhaust. The modular connector bay is on the side, behind the clear panel. The face opposite the AC receptacle hosts the white control PCB, also visible through its clear panel, with two iCUE LINK ports, a USB Type-C port, and a small port marked "TACH". The hexagonal specifications sticker is on the solid steel top side of the unit and is an envelope-type that can be closed to hide the text.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The CRYSTAL is fully compatible with Corsair's iCUE software, which can be used to monitor the PSU, adjust its fan speed, and switch the 12V overcurrent protection between single- and multi-rail modes. Corsair also integrated an iCUE LINK System Hub that, according to the company, supports up to 24 iCUE LINK devices without the need for a separate hub. The most interesting addition, however, is PinProtect+, Corsair's per-pin current monitoring for the 12V-2x6 connector. If the current on a pin exceeds a user-customizable threshold, the PSU cuts power to the connector, and the RGB fan lighting turns red to signal the fault. Given the history of melting connectors, this is a genuinely useful feature rather than marketing fluff. We can confirm that it is working as intended, as we tried pulling 20A from a single wire of the 12V-2x6 connector and the PSU shut down instantly.

Cooling is handled by a 140 mm Corsair NR140HL-RGB fan. Corsair calls it a magnetic dome bearing design, and in practice it is a rifle-bearing fan with a half-domed shaft magnet, rated at 0.55A on 12V. The fan is white with addressable lighting, which also doubles as the visual alarm for PinProtect+. It is a decent fan, and the magnetic shaft should help with longevity, but it is worth mentioning that the regular HX1000i SHIFT uses a fluid dynamic bearing fan, which we would normally consider the better choice for a premium unit.

The OEM behind the HX1000i Shift Crystal is Channel Well Technology (CWT), Corsair's long-time partner for its HXi units. Almost every part inside the unit has been relabeled as "Corsair", including the capacitors and the magnetic components, so the OEM is not obvious at a glance, but the Cybenetics report confirms it. The layout is extremely clean and obviously optimized for aesthetics, which also explains the excessive length of the chassis. The bridge rectifiers have their own heatsink, the APFC components sit on another large heatsink, and the primary switchers have their own heatsink as well, while the secondary side components are on a vertical PCB right after the main transformer. All heatsinks are of good size and quality.

The transient filtering stage has four Y capacitors, three X capacitors, and two filtering inductors. Two bridge rectifiers sharing a large heatsink feed the APFC stage, which relies on two Infineon 60R099P6 MOSFETs and two fully encased filtering coils. The APFC stage also includes two bulk capacitors, one Corsair 560 μF / 420 V capacitor and one Corsair 470 μF / 420 V capacitor, for a combined capacity of 1030 μF. According to the Cybenetics report, these capacitors are supplied by Elite, a relatively reputable Taiwanese manufacturer, and they are rated for 105°C. They are not capacitors that we frequently encounter in anything above mid-tier, though, and we feel they are not a great match for a premium unit.

The primary inversion stage uses two more Infineon 60R099P6 MOSFETs in a half-bridge LLC resonant topology. On the secondary side, ten Infineon 014N04LS MOSFETs handle the synchronous rectification of the 12V rail, and the minor rails are derived from DC-to-DC converters. All of the capacitors on the secondary side are rebranded as Corsair, with black and white sleeves that match the color scheme of the unit, and are also made by Elite. These are mostly good parts, but it is a bit ironic that Corsair advertises 105°C Japanese electrolytic capacitors for the regular HX1000i SHIFT, while the version built to show its capacitors off uses Taiwanese ones.

For the testing of PSUs, we are using high-precision electronic loads with a maximum power draw of 2700 Watts, a Rigol DS5042M 40 MHz oscilloscope, an Extech 380803 power analyzer, two high-precision UNI-T UT-325 digital thermometers, an Extech HD600 SPL meter, a self-designed hotbox, and various other bits and parts.

At 25°C ambient, the HX1000i Shift Crystal is a very efficient power supply. It averaged 92.1% at 230 VAC and 90.5% at 115 VAC across the nominal load range, with the efficiency peaking at 93.6% (230 VAC) and 92.2% (115 VAC) at around 40% load. The curve is relatively flat after that, with the unit still achieving 91% at 230 VAC and 88.5% at 115 VAC while delivering its full 1000W. The efficiency at very low loads is good as well, at just over 88% with a 50W load on 230 VAC. These figures are in line with the unit's Cybenetics Platinum certification at both input voltages. There is no 80 PLUS certification for this unit.

The acoustics at room temperature are very good. The fan stays completely off up to 50% load, so a typical gaming system with a powerful graphics card will rarely ever wake it up. Once it starts, the unit registers 31.6 dB(A) at 500W, and the fan ramps up very gently, staying around 33 dB(A) at 700W and below 37 dB(A) at 900W. Only at full load does the noise climb to about 39 dB(A), which is still a low figure for a 1000W unit. Thermal performance gives no reason for concern at all, with the primary and secondary heatsinks reaching just 55°C and 58°C respectively at full load.

Inside our hotbox, at roughly 45°C ambient and still within the unit's 50°C rating, the HX1000i Shift Crystal hardly lost any efficiency at all. It averaged 91.7% at 230 VAC and 90.1% at 115 VAC, a drop of just 0.4 percentage points compared to the cold test. The peak efficiency dropped to 93.3% and 91.9%, respectively, and the full-load efficiency to 90.6% and 88.1%. That is a very strong result that shows how well the platform tolerates high ambient temperatures.

The fan is the part that changes its behavior the most in the heat. It starts spinning a bit earlier, at about 400W, registering 32.2 dB(A), and the noise climbs relatively fast to about 36 dB(A) at 600W and 39.5 dB(A) at 700W. Beyond 800W, the fan profile becomes much more aggressive, with the unit reaching 45.7 dB(A) at 900W and 46.8 dB(A) at its maximum output. That is definitely loud, though it takes very adverse conditions to get there. Internal temperatures rose considerably, with the primary heatsink reaching 84°C and the secondary heatsink 89°C at full load, figures that are high but comfortably within what the platform can handle.

Voltage regulation is very good. Across the 20% to 100% load range, the 12V rail deviated by 1%, from 12.10V down to 11.98V; the 5V rail by 1% as well; and the 3.3V rail by just 0.7%. These are not the sub-0.5% figures of the very best digital platforms on the market, but all of the rails are well controlled, and the 12V rail stays very close to its nominal voltage across the whole load range. The ripple suppression is excellent. The 12V rail peaked at 48 mV under maximum load and 44 mV during the 12V cross-load test, while the 5V and 3.3V rails both topped out at 26 mV. Compared to the ATX design limits of 120 mV and 50 mV, that leaves a margin of 60% on the 12V rail and about 48% on the minor rails, which is very comfortable headroom for a 1000W unit.

During our thorough assessment, we evaluate the essential protection features of every power supply unit we review, including Over Current Protection (OCP), Over Voltage Protection (OVP), Over Power Protection (OPP), and Short Circuit Protection (SCP).

All protection mechanisms were verified and triggered correctly during testing. With the unit hot, OCP tripped at 148% (37A) on the 3.3V rail, 144% (36A) on the 5V rail, and 132% (110A) on the 12V rail, with OPP shutting the unit down at 130% of its rated output, or 1300W. These thresholds are on the high side, especially for the minor rails, but they are not unusual for an ATX 3.1 unit.

The HX1000i Shift Crystal is a power supply designed to be looked at, and that is not something we often get to say. Corsair took a CWT-built HXi platform, cleaned up its layout to the point that it can be shown off, and placed it behind three polycarbonate panels with an RGB fan on top. The result is unique, and it is backed by a genuinely modern feature set, with ATX 3.1 compliance, full iCUE support, an integrated iCUE LINK System Hub, and a native 12V-2x6 connector with PinProtect+ per-pin current monitoring. The latter is more than a bullet point on the box, as the unit shut down instantly when we pulled 20A from a single wire of the 12V-2x6 connector.

Most importantly, the looks did not come at the expense of performance. The efficiency is excellent, with averages of 92.1% at 230 VAC and 90.5% at 115 VAC, and it barely moved when we raised the ambient temperature to 45°C. Voltage regulation is tight on every rail, and the ripple suppression is excellent, leaving a large margin against the ATX limits. At room temperature, the fan does not even spin up before the load reaches 500W and it stays quiet all the way up to the maximum output.

The compromises are few, but some of them are hard to ignore on a unit like this. The capacitors are all made by Elite and rebranded as Corsair. Elite is a relatively reputable Taiwanese manufacturer, but its capacitors are not something we frequently encounter above the mid-tier, and they are not a great match for a premium unit when the regular HX1000i SHIFT advertises Japanese capacitors. The fan uses a rifle bearing with a magnetic shaft instead of a fluid dynamic bearing, and it becomes loud under heavy load in a hot environment. The bundle is also spartan for an aesthetics-focused premium product. Finally, the 200 mm long chassis and the side-mounted connectors mean that not every case will accommodate the unit, although most buyers are likely to pair it with Corsair's matching 4500X case anyway. On the bright side, the ten-year warranty covers the unit for a period that very few users will ever need, and it makes the component choices easier to accept.

At the time of this review, Corsair had not made the price of the HX1000i Shift Crystal public, which makes a definitive value judgment impossible. The regular HX1000i SHIFT is the obvious comparison, as it offers the same side connector layout, iCUE integration, and iCUE LINK hub for $339.99 at Corsair's own store, along with a fluid dynamic bearing fan and Japanese capacitors. Compared to other showy high-end units, such as the Asus ROG Thor 1000W Platinum II or the Lian Li Edge 1000, the CRYSTAL is the only one that actually puts its internals on display, and its Cybenetics Platinum efficiency and tight electrical performance mean that it does not need its looks to justify itself. If it arrives at a reasonable premium over the regular HX1000i SHIFT, it is an easy recommendation for anyone building a showcase system, and a well-performing 1000W unit for everyone else who simply likes how it looks, as long as they can live with its parts selection.

Dr. E. Fylladitakis has been passionate about PCs since the 8088 era, beginning his PC gaming journey with classics like Metal Mutant and Battle Chess. Not long after, he built his first PC, a 486, and has been an enthusiast ever since. In the early 2000’s, he delved deeply into overclocking Duron and Pentium 4 processors, liquid cooling, and phase-change cooling technologies. While he has an extensive and broad engineering education, Dr. Fylladitakis specializes in electrical and energy engineering, with numerous articles published in scientific journals, some contributing to novel cooling technologies and power electronics. He has been a hardware reviewer at AnandTech for nearly a decade. Outside of his professional pursuits, he enjoys immersing himself in a good philosophy book and unwinding through PC games.

I tested the Apple HomeView for a week — and I was expecting a lot more

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Unlock and manage exclusive Tom’s Guide member rewards.

Join the Tom's Guide Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Apple HomeView is built well, but as a smart display, it can't do as much as rivals from Amazon, and Siri isn't as capable as other AI assistants.

No physical button to turn off camera or mic

Fewer streaming apps than other smart displays

Why you can trust Tom's Guide Our writers and editors spend hours analyzing and reviewing products, services, and apps to help find what's best for you. Find out more about how we test, analyze, and rate.

After years of waiting, Apple has finally come out with a smart display, but is it already too late? The Apple HomeView is a sleek device with a nice-looking interface, but scratch deeper than the surface, and you’ll find that it quickly comes up short against the best smart displays. Yes, Amazon’s are the only other game in town, but that company’s much longer history when designing a smart home device — and AI — shows just how far Apple needs to go.

Yes, there are some redeeming qualities of the HomeView — it’s great at music, and integrates well with your iPhone — but when testing it alongside an Amazon smart display, I was left wanting for features time and again.

A post shared by Tom's Guide (@tomsguide)

The HomeView goes on sale November 13, 2026, for $299. It comes in two versions, a freestanding model, and a wall-mounted version. The freestanding HomeView is offered in four colors (silver, starlight, rose pink, space gray) while the wall version comes in just silver or space gray.

If you opt for the wall-mounted version, you’ll have to pay an additional amount for either the USB-C wall connector ($59) or the hardwired mount ($79); if you choose the latter of these two, you’ll probably want to call an electrician to install it.

The base of the HomeView is a small aluminum cylinder that’s rounded at the top, where it connects to the display. The bottom of the base has several rows of perforated holes for the display's speaker.

In an era where most smart home speakers (Apple’s included) are using more fabric-covered finishes, the HomeView reminded me, more than anything, of the love child of a first-generation Amazon Echo and the iMac G4.

The HomeView’s screen is smaller than I would have imagined from the early videos and hands-ons. It definitely won’t take up a ton of room on your counter, which is a nice thing if you have limited space.

Get instant access to breaking news, the hottest reviews, great deals and helpful tips.

Apple’s specs say the display is 7 inches on the diagonal, but owing to the bezels, the usable space is about 6.4 inches. Measuring vertically and horizontally, the screen is 4 7/8 inches in each direction.

It’s very nice to look at; its 1088-by-1088-pixel resolution (220 pixels per inch) made all of my photos look excellent, and the display can automatically adjust its brightness from 0.3 to 500 nits.

For a device that’s smaller than the HomePod mini, the HomeView does a pretty impressive job with music. Apple says that the HomeView has two custom full-range drivers and a custom down-firing woofer, which delivered stronger bass than I would have expected for a device this size.

I was able to blast the “Hamilton” soundtrack throughout my kitchen, and it was loud enough that I had to turn down the volume. In order to do so, though, I had to either yell at Siri, or tap the screen; there’s no dedicated volume controls.

A 12MP camera with Center Stage sits at the very top center bezel; it was perfectly adequate when making FaceTime calls, and the Center Stage feature was welcome, as it let me move around my kitchen while chatting.

You can set up the HomeView with any number of faces. As a Peanuts fan, one of my favorites was an animated clock with Snoopy and Woodstock doing a bunch of shenanigans — and easily swipe between them. However, due to its size — or because of Apple’s design decisions — the HomeView’s homescreen feels more limited than Amazon’s.

Here’s an example. Like the Echo Show devices, the HomeView has a face, called Home Modular, that lets you control smart home devices, and even get a live look from any cameras connected to Apple Home. However, if you want to see pictures of your kids, know what the weather is, and see what’s happening at your front door, good luck. You can create a custom Modular face that incorporates some of these elements, but you can't fit them all.

By contrast, the Echo Show 8, which admittedly has a larger 8.7-inch screen, lets you pack more on there, so you get a much more holistic look at everything going on both in and outside of your home.

In order to simplify things for developers, the HomeView uses the same widgets that you can install on an iPhone. But what works for a smartphone doesn’t necessarily work for a smart display.

You can add Widgets to the HomeView, but surprisingly — and somewhat aggravatingly — some of them don’t work, or aren’t optimized for the device. For example, I added the Disney+ widget, but when I tapped on it, it displayed a message saying “The Disney+ app is only available on iPhone.” Same with the Anker Eufy app and YouTube. The only widget that lets you play videos natively is Apple TV.

Even more frustrating for me was the Photos widget. After setting it up — which requires you to use your iPhone to select the photos you want — the widget then displays a picture from your collection. However, if you tap on the widget to change the image, you get a message saying “App unavailable. The Photos app is only available on iPhone.” I felt a bit like Charlie Brown right after Lucy swiped the football away.

Those widgets do serve a purpose — you can start playing a video on your iPhone, and then cast it to the HomeView, but it’s an extra step that you don’t need to take with any other smart display.

What’s odd is that the HomeView lists dozens of widgets, but few are optimized for the HomeView. Perhaps this will change once the product goes on sale. And, what Widgets are available aren’t that many. While there’s a Stocks widget, there’s none for News or Sports, so you’ll have to ask Siri for updates on the game.

Forget using the HomeView as a smart TV; while you can watch Apple TV content, there aren’t any other widgets for most popular streaming services, such as Hulu, Peacock, or Netflix. One of the main reasons I like smart displays is that they can act as mini TVs, so I can cook Sunday dinner in the kitchen, but still be able to watch football games without running into the living room.

In keeping with Apple’s admirable insistence on privacy — and because it’s a nice feature — the HomeView’s screen will only display personalized content if its camera recognizes that individual’s face.

So, for example, if I walk up to the HomeView, I’ll see my photos and calendar appointments, but if my wife were to approach the device, she would see her content, provided she were a registered user. In either instance, our iPhones would also have to be on the same Wi-Fi network for any personal content to appear.

If the Homeview recognizes your face, a small orange dot appears in the upper right corner. Curiously for a company which prides itself on privacy, there’s no button on the HomeView to disable its camera.

One of the reasons that the HomeView was delayed was that Apple was waiting for Siri to get smarter. So, I put it to the test versus Alexa+ on an Echo Show 15. Tl;dr — Apple needs to do a bit more work.

The first challenge was asking for the weather. The first time I asked Siri, I received a very terse “Expect partly cloudy skies today,” and the HomeView’s screen changed to show me the current temperature. The next time I asked, I got a slightly more detailed response: “Expect clear skies today with a high of 59 degrees and a low of 47 degrees.”

By contrast, Alexa+ was much more conversant. “Right now it’s cloudy and 56 degrees, feeling 53. Today’s high is 61 with a low of 45, and it’ll stay breezy with decreasing clouds and no rain expected.”

Next, I asked both Alexa+ and Siri to look up local pizza places. Again, Siri gave me a list, but Alexa+ was more effusive, telling me how late some of the more popular restaurants were open.

I then asked both AIs to order me a pizza from one particular restaurant. Siri couldn’t, but offered to place a call, but Alexa+ linked to Uber Eats, and walked me through ordering a large pie.

Another key function for the HomeView is that it can act as a central control panel for all of your smart home devices. Not only can you tap widgets on the screen to turn your lights on and off, but you can also use your voice to control things. For example, I said “Hey Siri, show me my video doorbell” and the HomeView brought up a live view of my front door nearly instantly.

I also liked that you can add a widget to show a live view of your security cameras. I have a Eufy camera, which worked well with the HomeView, but this is one area where Apple needs to incentivize its partners a bit more; there are far fewer security cameras compatible with Apple Home than there are for Amazon and Google.

How well can a home assistant get you where you need to go? I asked both Siri and Alexa+ to get me directions to Newark airport. Siri gave its usual brief response: “It will take about 13 minutes to drive to Newark Liberty International Airport in light traffic.” I then asked Siri to send the directions to my phone, which it did as a text message with a link to Apple Maps to my phone.

Alexa+ was more chatty, but less helpful. “Navigation isn’t supported on this device, but I can give you the route info. It’s about 12 minutes and 5 miles to Newark Liberty International Airport, with light traffic on I-78.” I then asked Alexa to send the directions to my phone, but the best it could do was send a copy of the conversation to the Alexa app.

When I asked Alexa+ to book me an Uber ride, it went so far as to ask me if I wanted to start from my work or home location, and went through all the rest of the steps. The best Siri could do was offer to open the Uber app.

One thing I did like is that Siri can scan through your calendar and messages to surface things you might have missed, or forgot about. For example, I asked “what time are my neighbors having people over today?”

It then dug through all of my messages and replied “Your neighbors are having people over for a fire today at 4:30 p.m.” On the screen, it added further detail: “There will be drinks for the adults and marshmallows to roast for kids.”

Historically, Apple has generally not been first to any particular category, but when it enters a market — MP3 players, phones, tablets, etc. — it’s generally learned from the mistakes of the competition, and delivered a device that outshines everything else on the market.

The HomeView is not that. While the physical design of the device is lovely, its software does not live up to the same standard. It can do far less than rival smart displays from Amazon, and Siri is still a less capable home assistant AI than Alexa+ or Gemini.

I think Apple probably had to release it; there have been reports that the HomeView was languishing in a warehouse for the better part of a year, which makes sense considering it’s using the A18, rather than the A19 chip — but maybe it should have stayed there.

Before his tenure at Tom's Guide, he was the Reviews Editor for Laptop Magazine, a reporter at Fast Company, the Times of Trenton, and, many eons back, an intern at George magazine. He received his undergraduate degree from Boston College, where he worked on the campus newspaper The Heights, and then attended the Columbia University school of Journalism. When he’s not testing out the latest running watch, electric scooter, or skiing or training for a marathon, he’s probably using the latest sous vide machine, smoker, or pizza oven, to the delight — or chagrin — of his family.

I asked Google how they're going to overcome the 'pervert glasses' backlash: Here's their plan to beat Meta

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Can Google make smart glasses less creepy?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The smart glasses category is at a tipping point, and Google knows it.

2026 has been utterly dominated by negative headlines around smart glasses. Not only did a black market develop for disabling Meta glasses LEDs, a whole cottage industry formed around recording surreptitious pickup videos, putting unsuspecting women in the crosshairs. No wonder the "pervert glasses" moniker took off.

While Meta has updated its software to thwart tampering and removed harassing smart glasses videos from its platforms — and even released camera-free glasses — the stigma is still very much prevalent. Enter Google, which has the very challenging task of convincing the masses that its new Intelligent Eyewear is not just cool, but safe.

Co-developed with Samsung, the new smart glasses come in slick designs from two of the biggest names in eyewear: Warby Parker (from $395) and Gentle Monster ($449 to $489). And they have a very powerful assistant inside with Gemini. But none of that will matter if shoppers stay on the sidelines over privacy fears.

“Our goal is not to compete on who has the most private glasses,” said Juston Payne, Google’s senior director of product management for XR. “What we want to do is to set a standard and then have people rise up to that standard.”

I spoke with Payne as well as Chris Breithaupt, group product manager for AI on glasses, about how Google plans to take smart glasses from a menace to a wearable companion you actually trust.

During a demo of the new Intelligent Eyewear, I noticed that the LED is fairly bright and that it pulsates while recording video. It’s pretty hard to miss, and it’s even harder to tamper with — at least for now. It was critical for Google to build in protections from the outset.

Get instant access to breaking news, the hottest reviews, great deals and helpful tips.

“If you cover the LED, then the camera stops working,” said Payne. “So whether it's a simple cover like putting your finger over it, or a piece of tape, or a sharpie, or you do something more sophisticated, like buy a camera cover or tamper with the hardware, in all of these cases, from day one, we have protections against those things.”

In addition, Payne told me that his team is collaborating with other parts of Google to help with glasses privacy. For example, Google Ads will not run or accept ads for LED covers for smart glasses. And Google Shopping isn’t going to sell them.

Payne admitted that there’s never going to be a perfect solution for covering the LED, but that Google is committed to staying one step ahead of hackers and bad actors.

“Somebody will figure out a way around it,” Payne said. “We publish a patch and so on. But we're actually very happy with where we're starting.”

I was actually somewhat frustrated that I had to explicitly tell Gemini to use the camera a couple of times during my demo. Unless it’s super clear to Google’s assistant that you’re looking for it to see what you see, you’ll have to specifically ask for it to stream video.

This is a safeguard Google built in so that Intelligent Eyewear doesn’t start streaming to the cloud when you don’t want it to. But these new smart glasses go a step further than that.

“We're treating this data really differently,” Payne said, “differently than actually Google has before, and also than how other companies have been treating it. So that visual data, it's not used for targeting ads. It's not used for training Gemini. Human raters can't look at it.”

The way the pipeline works is that the video goes from Intelligent Eyewear glasses to your phone, and then from the phone to the cloud.

“So before it actually even gets to the Gemini app on your phone, we redact faces,” Payne said. If you remember back to the Snapdragon Summit, you’ll know that this face censoring happens at a silicon level on the chip — as confirmed by Ziad Asghar, Qualcomm’s SVP and GM of XR.

Visual privacy is only part of the battle; ambient audio is the other. Google is also trying to make bystanders’ sound private when you’re chatting with Gemini. The glasses use beam-forming mics so that it pays attention to your voice, but not the people around you.

It’s one thing to focus on the hardware and software when it comes to privacy, but Google is also working on curbing demand for the very types of “cold approach” and “pickup artist” content that has gone viral over the past couple of years.

For example, the Intelligent Eyewear team is working with YouTube to block problematic videos captured with smart glasses.

“We're looking for videos with harassment in them, sexualization of people from covert sources, and then we'll be enforcing policies around those,” Payne said.

That includes video takedowns, Google’s Breithaupt told me.

Assuming Google can assuage people’s fears around smart glasses and privacy — which is a huge ask — Intelligent Eyewear could be a potential breakthrough for the nascent category.

Instead of unlocking your phone hundreds of times a day, you might use Intelligent Eyewear to get quick answers to questions via Gemini, troubleshoot a home project with real-time answers, listen to music or podcasts or take calls on the go. And while Meta’s Muse is quite capable, I’ve found Gemini to be smarter and rely on it every day.

During my hands-on time I used the smart glasses to get spoken directions via Google Maps while I was walking around New York City, to help mix a cocktail using the ingredients in front of me and do live translation during an in-person conversation. It would be even better if the glasses had a built-in display, but that feature is coming with future products.

For just a moment, I forgot about all of the privacy concerns around smart glasses and appreciated the sheer utility of these specs, even if they’re thicker than my usual Warby Parker reading glasses.

And that’s what Google and all of its partners need to do to make Intelligent Eyewear more than a curiosity. Establish trust so the privacy concerns start to recede. “If there's a simple message, we care a lot about trust. We care a lot about responsibility here, and we're really trying to move the needle on how this category is perceived. Because once that happens, then you get back to people talking about how incredibly helpful these [glasses] are.”

The 'eclipse of the century' will plunge these 8 wonders of the Roman Empire into darkness on Aug. 2, 2027

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Ancient Roman cities, temples and amphitheaters across the Mediterranean will fall into the moon's shadow during the total solar eclipse on Aug. 2, 2027.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Ancient Egyptian temples will inevitably dominate the story of the total solar eclipse on Aug. 2, 2027.

After all, the path of totality crosses Luxor, home to Karnak, the Valley of the Kings, and some of the ancient world's most famous monuments. But follow the moon's shadow west and another ancient civilization comes into view. Before reaching Egypt, totality will sweep across the Mediterranean and North Africa, passing over an extraordinary collection of Roman cities, fortresses, theaters, temples and triumphal arches. Many lie within what became Rome's North African provinces, a region that produced some of the empire's wealthiest and most impressive cities.

From beachside Baelo Claudia in Spain and military Tamuda in Morocco to Timgad and Djémila in Algeria and the jaw-dropping El Jem Amphitheater in Tunisia, Roman ruins will experience between two and more than five minutes of darkness — creating extraordinary possibilities for eclipse chasers and photographers. Here are some of the best places to watch the moon's shadow cross what was once the Roman Empire. All times, totality durations and historical cloud cover data come from Time and Date.

Start of totality and duration: 10:44 a.m. CEST; 4 minutes, 31 seconds

Altitude of the sun: 37 degrees above east

Roman ruins, an Atlantic beach and the mountains of Africa could all share the frame with the eclipsed sun at Baelo Claudia. Founded in the second century B.C., this remarkably well-preserved Roman city near Bolonia beach includes a forum, basilica, theater, temples, baths and a decumanus maximus (east-west street), all set against forested hills and a vast coastal sand dune. For photographers, the possibilities are exceptional — columns and temples may provide an ancient foreground, while wider compositions could incorporate the beach and dunes. On particularly clear days, Morocco is visible across the Strait of Gibraltar — potentially putting Roman ruins, two continents and a total solar eclipse into one extraordinary scene.

Start of totality and duration: 10:48 a.m. CEST; 1 minute, 57 seconds

Altitude of the sun: 39 degrees above east

Few eclipse locations can offer two civilizations in the same photograph. Málaga's Roman Theatre was built in the first century B.C., when the city was part of the Roman province of Hispania, and today sits directly beneath the Alcazaba, the imposing Muslim-era palace-fortress that dominates the hillside above. Although its totality is considerably shorter than locations farther south, the photographic possibilities are compelling. From a carefully chosen position, it may be possible to frame the darkened sun with the theater's ancient stonework and the walls of the Alcazaba rising behind it. Another major advantage for eclipse chasers is that this extraordinary historical setting sits in the heart of Málaga, making it one of the most accessible ancient sites on the path of totality.

Start of totality and duration: 08:45 a.m. GMT; 4 minutes, 51 seconds

Altitude of sun: 38 degrees above east-southeast

Long before the Romans arrived in northern Morocco, Tamuda was already a thriving Mauretanian settlement. Founded around the third century B.C. in the Martil Valley, near modern Tétouan, the Romans established a fortified military camp here in the first century A.D., whose massive stone walls remain the defining feature of this archaeological site. Tamuda combines long totality, promising weather and an atmospheric archaeological foreground — yet remains far less famous than the Roman sites across the Strait of Gibraltar.

Start of totality and duration: 10:01 a.m. CET; 5 minutes, 20 seconds

Altitude of sun: 51 degrees above east-southeast

Few places in the 2027 path of totality combine ancient architecture and long totality quite like Timgad. Founded around A.D. 100 as the Roman colony of Thamugadi, this remarkably well-preserved city sits on an open plateau at the foot of Algeria's Aurès Mountains. Its grid of streets, forum, theater, temples, baths and columns survives alongside its most famous monument, the three-arched gateway traditionally known as the Arch of Trajan. A relatively high sun will make close architectural compositions challenging, but Timgad's wide-open site provides plenty of possibilities for carefully planned wide-angle images.

Start of totality and duration: 10:01 a.m. CET; 5 minutes, 20 seconds

Altitude of sun: 51 degrees above east-southeast

Just 12—15 miles (20—25 km) west of Timgad lies its military counterpart. Lambaesis was the principal base of Rome's Legio III Augusta, the legion that helped secure Rome's African Empire. Rather than an entire gridded colonial city, the attraction here is monumental military architecture, most notably the imposing remains of the heart of the fortress. The eclipse circumstances are almost identical to nearby Timgad, with more than five minutes of totality and historically excellent prospects for clear skies. Scout both sites beforehand, study where the sun will stand above the ruins, and decide which offers the strongest alignment — and the easiest access — on eclipse morning.

Start of totality and duration: 10:02 a.m. CET; 2 minutes, 2 seconds

Altitude of eclipsed sun: 50 degrees above east-southeast

Djémila trades a long eclipse for one of the most spectacular settings on the path of totality. Known to the Romans as Cuicul, this exceptionally well-preserved city was built in mountainous terrain around 2,950 feet (900 meters) above sea level — an unusual setting for a Roman settlement — and its ruins include temples, forums, houses, baths, arches and a theater. The surrounding green hills and mountains give eclipse photographers a very different backdrop from the flatter, more arid landscapes farther south. The catch is totality, which is significantly shorter than at other Roman ruins.

Start of totality and duration: 10:06 a.m. CET; 5 minutes, 19 seconds

Altitude of sun: 54 degrees above east-southeast

Sbeitla could be one of the great archaeological settings for the 2027 eclipse. Known in Roman times as Sufetula, the city prospered under Roman rule and today contains some of Tunisia's most impressive ancient ruins. Its centerpiece is an unusual group of three remarkably well-preserved temples dedicated to Jupiter, Juno and Minerva, standing above the forum and reached through the monumental Arch of Antoninus Pius. That high sun will require some careful positioning and probably a wide-angle composition, but eclipse photographers will have an extraordinary collection of arches, columns and temples to use as foregrounds.

Start of totality and duration: 10:09 a.m. CET; 4 minutes, 23 seconds

Altitude of eclipsed sun: 56 degrees above east-southeast

It may be the third-largest and one of the best-preserved Roman amphitheaters in the world, but El Jem offers another reason to consider it for totality. Unlike Rome's Colosseum — which it closely resembles — visitors can explore its multi-tiered seating, climb high into the surviving structure for panoramic views and visit the underground passages once used by gladiators and animals. Built around A.D. 238, El Jem combines one of Rome's greatest surviving monuments with some of the most promising eclipse conditions on the entire track.

Jamie is an experienced science and travel journalist, stargazer and eclipse chaser who writes about exploring the night sky, solar and lunar eclipses, the Northern Lights, moon-gazing, astro-travel, astronomy and space exploration. He is the editor of WhenIsTheNextEclipse.com, author of A Stargazing Program For Beginners, co-author of The Eclipse Effect, and a senior contributor at Forbes.

Newark Museum of Art restores ownership of David Drake jug to artists’ descendants

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

David Drake, Jug, 1852. Stoneware, 36.8cm by 27.9cm. Collection of The Newark Museum of Art. Ethically acquired from the Dave the Potter Legacy Trust LLC, established for the benefit of the artist’s descendants. The work was returned to the artist’s descendants by the Newark Museum of Art and subsequently purchased by the Museum from the Trust. Purchase 2026 Mr. and Mrs. William V. Griffin Fund and Barbara Harrison Wescott Fund. Photo by Richard Goodbody

This past summer, the Newark Museum of Art in New Jersey initiated a dialogue with the family of David Drake (around 1801-74), the enslaved potter from South Carolina, regarding a large brown ceramic jug in its collection, dated 1852. Just a few months later—lightning speed in museum time—The Art Newspaper has learned that the institution has reached an agreement with Drake’s heirs to restore ownership of the work and purchase it back for its permanent collection.

The arrangement comes just over a year after the Museum of Fine Arts, Boston reached an historic agreement with Drake’s family to return two major stoneware vessels, one of which the museum purchased back. Momentum built from there and return agreements were reached with institutions including the Telfair Museums in Savannah and the Georgia Museum of Art in Athens, Georgia, the Virginia Museum of Fine Art, and others.

“What happened this July was really special,” says George Fatheree, an adviser to the Drake family. A letter the Newark Museum sent to the artist’s descendants and shared with The Art Newspaper states: “We acquired a jug by your ancestor in 2022 to help us more powerfully teach the history of enslavement and enslaved labour, as part of the 2023 reimagining and reinstallation of our American art galleries, Seeing America: 18th and 19th Century.” Noting the MFA Boston partnership, the museum extended the opportunity to meet and “identify ways we might collaborate”.

According to Lisa Funderburke, the Newark Museum’s director and chief executive, it was the museum’s senior director of registration and collections management, Amber Germano, who first approached her over the summer and asked if she would be willing to sign a letter.

“It’s not uncommon for curators to visit my office with a stack of papers of things that are either for consideration or ongoing engagement on articles of repatriation,” Funderburke says. “Always my first question is: ‘Can you articulate to me what we’re doing here and what the impulse behind that is?’”

According to Yaba Baker, one of Drake’s great-grandsons, Funderburke credited her staff with the initial action. “Lisa understood immediately what needed to be done,” Baker says. “One thing my family all agreed on: Dave is such a powerful figure in art history and Black history that to take the pots out of public view would be a disservice.”

Things moved quickly after that, with additional family meetings and Funderburke’s conversations with board members and other leaders at the museum. “I felt like this would be a strong north star for our board about where we are and where we want to be, and position ourselves as a museum into the future,” the director says.

After the museum commissioned several appraisals of the ceramic vessel, a value of around $135,000 was decided on. Numerous Drake pots have sold for five- and six-figure prices; the current record at auction for one of his works is the $1.65m paid for his 25-Gallon Poem Jar (1858), achieved at Crocker Farm Auctions in Maryland in 2021.

“Our approach is this should not be a zero-sum game,” Fatheree says, describing the outcome in Newark as a “win-win”.

Baker concurs, stressing the enduring lessons of his great-grandfather’s legacy. “He was a slave. He was considered three fifths of a person by the US constitution,” Baker says, adding that anti-literacy laws in South Carolina at the time forbade teaching enslaved people to write.

“For our family, it’s telling the story of someone who was considered by a lot of people to be nothing, but he conducted himself with such excellence,” Baker says, before quoting a famous inscription Drake wrote on another of his vessels that begins, “I wonder where is all my relation”, and concludes: “Friendship to all.”

Around 160 institutions hold looted Benin artefacts, but how many are prepared to give them back? We asked museums in five countries for their position on restitution

The two-foot-tall artefact, which hails from Ancient Egypt's Ptolemaic Dynasty, will remain on view in Cleveland for "a few years"

The clay coffin, which the MFA Boston acquired in 1985 with apparently false documentation, has been missing from the Gustavianum, Uppsala University Museum since at least 1970

Dangerous and unusual

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Type to search articles, cases, and authors.

Justice William O. Douglas announced his retirement on this day in 1975, more than 36 years into his tenure on the Supreme Court. Douglas is the longest-serving justice in Supreme Court history, though Justice Clarence Thomas will surpass Douglas’ record if he remains on the court through May 2028.

The Supreme Court Building was closed on Wednesday for Veterans Day.

Tomorrow, the justices will meet in a private conference to discuss cases and vote on petitions for review. Orders from Friday’s conference are expected on Monday at 9:30 a.m. EST.

In 'The Spot', Claire Danes and Ewan McGregor Unravel Together

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Claire Danes was standing in the operating room, dressed in scrubs and a mask, when a surgeon’s assistant gave her the opposite of a pep talk. “You may think you’re badass, but if you think you’re fainting, you’re fainting, and we can’t have that, so just let me know,” she told Danes.

Danes, luckily, did not faint, though she says she was incredibly aware of her breathing as she watched an intricate brain surgery performed by a team of doctors.

The three-time Emmy winner was observing in preparation for her role in The Spot, a new Hulu series in which she plays a successful neurosurgeon who begins to suspect that she may be responsible for the hit-and-run death of a child. As she tries to investigate the horrific possibility, she and her husband (Ewan McGregor) find themselves falling deeper and deeper into a web of suspicion and dark secrets.

“I’ve always thought of it as an exploration of a marriage through the lens of a crime and maybe exploration of a crime through the lens of a marriage,” says Solomon of The Spot.

“I’m fascinated with this notion of what if your worst-case scenario way in the back of your head started to really look like it was true, and how could you dramatize that?” says writer, creator, and showrunner Ed Solomon.

The bold psychological thriller, which Vanity Fair can exclusively reveal will debut on January 13, follows a woman unraveling at the possibility of her worst fears coming true, and a marriage under enormous strain. There’s been a steady stream of whodunit miniseries in the last decade, but here Solomon, whose past work includes writing The Christophers and Now You See Me, has created a highly watchable, page-turning series that’s more of a “did I do it?”

“I never want it to be pretentious,” says Solomon. “I just want it to be fun and entertaining, but also elevated, respectful of the viewer.”

"I had wanted to work with Claire for years and we just had great fun acting and playing together while making this show,” says McGregor.

Danes was initially drawn to the series because of the portrait of the marriage at the heart of it, and Solomon’s particular gift for weaving the tragic into the comic. “It gets really very dark pretty quickly,” she says, “but it’s absurd too.”

She not only watched two brain surgeries in preparation but also shadowed a surgeon on his rounds, being with patients before they went into surgery and while they were recovering with their families. “I felt horrible that there I was in this room with these people who couldn’t be more vulnerable, and there I was, trying to be discreet in the corner,” she says.

But the experience gave her insight into what kind of person her character Lou Shadwick would be: intense, hypervigilant, and high achieving. “There’s a lot of marathon runners in there, and highly, highly focused people,” she says. “And they carry a lot of responsibility, and I think they compartmentalize very effectively.”

Danes had previously worked with Solomon on the 2023 series Full Circle, which was directed by Steven Soderbergh. When Kate Winslet, who was initially attached to play Lou, exited, Danes stepped in. Solomon needed an actress who could portray a mother and surgeon who is obsessed with doing the right thing and being a good person. “To be able to see someone who has a very, very present and active inner life that when you look, you can see well beyond the eyes and you can see someone who can be basically playing many things at one time, which was really important and interesting to me,” he says.

Danes had never worked with McGregor before, though they both had previously worked with Baz Luhrmann (on Romeo + Juliet and Moulin Rouge, respectively) and she thinks they had met briefly around that time. “We were definitely of a certain time, the ’90s,” she says. “So we share a lot of references, but it was just an immediate rapport.”

Lou is doing what many mothers juggling work and family have done: driving home from work, talking on the phone about dinner while also checking an unending flurry of text messages. It’s at that moment when she thinks she might have accidentally hit a child on a bike and not realized it until she heard about it later. “It’s so effective, this circumstance that he’s drawn for us,” she says of Solomon. “It’s entirely relatable, and I think it’s the fear that we all have experienced and hosted many, many times, if not on a daily basis.”

Michael Patrick Thornton as Pete Porphyry

As she tries to piece together what might have happened, a police officer (Daveed Diggs) and a detective (Michael Patrick Thornton) are doing the same, and begin to suspect Lou. Her husband, Damon, gets involved, making more trouble for both of them.

Lou and Damon met in medical school, but Damon dropped out and is now pursuing medical aid work in the shadow of his wife’s illustrious career. “I really like the idea of a man, who is charming and good-looking and therefore used to feeling very much in control when he walks into a room, having a spouse who is quite significantly on the rise,” says Solomon. “What does that do to your sense of ego if you’re a guy like that?”

Lou begins to see signs that she may be the guilty party.

McGregor says he had wanted to work with Danes for a long time, and was drawn as Damon as a character. “I think the character has an amazing arc throughout the story from where he starts to where he ends,” he says. “He has a very deeply hidden, shameful secret that gets uncovered as the story goes on.”

McGregor brings to the role a kind of playfulness. “I’ve always loved a certain quality of Ewan’s where there’s a maturity and a boyishness happening simultaneously,” says Solomon. “The boyish quality within this character is really important and the ability to cover a sense of wound.”

Set in upstate New York, the new series will take viewers on a wild ride following two people who are putting themselves in an increasingly dire situation and web of lies. “She’s a decent person, and so is her husband, really,” says Danes. “And yet, they allow their moral compass to become increasingly compromised.” Solomon hopes that even as the story gets darker and wilder, it will leave viewers with at least one big question they’ll have to grapple with: “I would hope that they’re asking questions, like what would I do if I were in that situation?”

“In Ewan, you can see the fragility in him,” says Solomon of McGregor.

Apple’s HomeView Tries Face Authentication Without FaceID

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Apple's HomeView, which debuts on Friday, is a smart home device—think of it as a an iPhone-Apple Watch hybrid sitting on your counter—that can be personalized for up to six people and connected to each of their iCloud accounts. Using ambient face and voice data, it is designed to seamlessly switch between users while maintaining each of their privacy. You can ask HomeView about your schedule for the day and what movie your friend recommended in a text, but you'll be stopped from asking about your roommate's travel plans or her texts from last night. The feature doesn't rely on one of Apple's battle-tested biometric locks, like FaceID or TouchID, though. Instead, it uses new “person recognition” technology built off of an Apple architecture for securing sensor data.

This is a key departure for Apple. On the one hand, the company has still been very careful to think about security and privacy comprehensively. Not only does HomeView check for your face and voice before accessing your data from iCloud, it is also designed like an Apple Watch to check that your iPhone or iPad is on the same Wi-Fi network as the HomeView and has been recently unlocked (with FaceID or a PIN). On the other hand, expanding the architecture used to process biometric data and authenticate users creates more opportunities for mistakes and potential vulnerabilities. And when it comes to attempting to access users' Apple data, both law enforcement and exploit brokers consistently go to great lengths to attempt to break the company's on-device defenses.

FaceID is built for the use case where a person is staring at their device head-on from less than an arm's length away. It provides reliable security that’s difficult to defeat because it stores your biometric data on a special, physically isolated chip known as the Secure Enclave—first introduced in 2013 for the iPhone 5s. The architecture is so robust that Apple also relies on the Secure Enclave as the foundation of a device's entire encryption scheme. But Apple concluded that FaceID wasn't the best fit for the HomeView, because it needs to bounce quickly between users and relies on AI processing, which can't all be squeezed into the Secure Enclave.

As a result, HomeView uses Apple's recently announced Secure Exclave (note the “x”) to handle the video and audio data the device uses to evaluate who is in a room at a given time. Apple says it has actually been laying the groundwork for the Secure Exclave for a long time—early versions of the architecture were already in place for the iPhone 16 and 17 and MacBook Neo. Instead of being an entirely separately chip, the Secure Exclave is a dedicated, memory-protected space in recent Apple processors. It is in Apple's latest S11 chip for Apple Watch Series 12 and Apple Watch Ultra 4, as well as the A20 Pro chip for iPhone 18 Pro and iPhone Duo.

Even though the Secure Exclave isn't based on the tried and true Secure Enclave technology, Apple argues that it is robust and expands the company's ability to secure a range of features, including improving the security of existing mechanisms. In a blog post on Thursday, the company even goes into some detail about how it used the mathematical technique known as formal verification to vet the “microkernel” designed for the Secure Exclave.

“This work both benefits our newest features and strengthens the security of features we already ship, such as ‘Hey Siri’ detection,” the company writes in a blog post. “Our goal is to keep ambient sensor data secure even against highly sophisticated attacks.”

HomeView and the MacBook Neo also use the Secure Exclave to govern the indicator light that tells users when their camera or microphone is active. The goal is to isolate this process and the chain of command governing it to make it even more difficult for attackers to try to disable an indicator light when it should be on or stop it from coming on in the first place.

The HomeView's “person recognition” technology is simply not as comprehensive as FaceID, though, and it is possible that the technology is more susceptible to reconstructed 3D depth map attacks—and potentially voiceprint attacks—than FaceID.

Of course, you don't have to have a HomeView in your house. Or if you do, you can use it on guest mode or a less involved “personalized” mode that only incorporates things like your playlists for other users to access as well and doesn't access calendar or Messages data. But as Apple forges deeper into AI integration, HomeView's departure from FaceID provides insight into the scale of the changes Apple is planning.

Here’s How Google Says It’s Making Its Smart Glasses More Private

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Smart glasses have a public perception problem. People wearing Meta's popular Ray-Ban glasses have been labeled “Meta Creeps.” But it didn't start with Meta—when Google Glass launched in 2013, “Glasshole” was the word used to describe people wearing them.

It boils down to the fact that these smart glasses have cameras on them that let people record whatever’s in front of them with the push of a button. The cameras aren't easy to see, and while an indicator light activates when the glasses are recording, they're far less conspicuous than someone holding a smartphone up to record video or photos. It also doesn't help that people have tried to tamper with the LEDs and block them, and have recorded women without their consent.

But Google is on a mission to change the perception of smart glasses as socially intrusive tech. The first Android XR Intelligent Eyewear smart glasses—made in collaboration with Samsung, Warby Parker, and Gentle Monster—are now available for preorder, with sales kicking off on December 10. You can read about how they work and what they can do in this separate story.

But you might be more interested in how Google is trying to separate its glasses from Meta's optics and positioning them as the more privacy-minded option to assuage public concerns.

“There's this whole feature set of AI privacy that we think is super important,” says Juston Payne, Google's senior director of product management for XR. “Ultimately, what we're trying to do here is say: Look, these are choices. These are, honestly, not hard technical problems. They're decisions. And we would like other companies to make different decisions, sort of like a bar-raising thing.”

All of the Intelligent Eyewear glasses have a power switch to turn them off completely, so you can wear them as regular glasses. They also have a recording light that illuminates when the glasses snap a photo or record a video, which is intentionally limited to 90 seconds for privacy purposes.

Here's a collection of the things Google says it's doing to make its smart glasses more private:

Payne hopes these policies and actions will encourage other companies to follow suit. “We hope that never again do glasses launch from some other company that doesn't have good algorithms built in,” he says. “That should not have happened; hopefully it doesn't happen again.”

Privacy experts I spoke with acknowledged that Google is trying not to repeat Meta's mistakes, but they point out that smart glasses are still vehicles for collecting sensitive data. Even with an indicator light, it's harder to tell when someone's recording you in public than if someone is holding out their smartphone.

Reem Suleiman, senior campaign director at the nonprofit digital rights advocacy group Fight for the Future, says camera-equipped glasses shouldn't be normalized in public because they are a precursor to mass surveillance and have the capacity to identify people. (This summer, Meta silently added a face-recognition code to its smart glasses and then subsequently deleted it after a WIRED report; in 2024, two Harvard students built a face-recognition tool for Meta's glasses.)

“People find ways around it,” Suleiman says. “There's no actual aspect of consent.”

Janusz Swierczynski, who researches human interactions with emerging tech at the University of Oxford's Saïd Business School, tells WIRED these privacy measures are meaningful. However, they still largely address privacy at an individual level rather than thinking about people affected by them. That's partly because the technology is advancing faster than policy and research.

“A lot of privacy is managed by social norms, by what we think is acceptable to record, and that prevents people from recording anything and everything,” Swierczynski says. “Normalizing recording does have unintended consequences, which is why it's important to focus on bystander privacy.”

Intriguingly, Payne highlighted that tech companies like Google need to make clear, declarative statements about what the glasses are doing.

“Ambiguity is not your friend here,” Payne says. “I think that a lot of that concern came from some trust being broken. So there is a rebuilding thing happening right now … You can hopefully have your cake and eat it too, which is cool glasses that everybody's excited about—that you're comfortable with.”

Google and Samsung’s Android XR Smart Glasses Are Finally Here

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Google and Samsung's next face computers have arrived. Nearly two years after the companies first announced the Android XR platform that powers the hardware, the smart glasses are finally on sale.

The “Intelligent Eyewear” is a collaboration between the two tech companies, with Samsung providing its hardware expertise and Google handling much of the software platform. Smart glasses are just one part of Android XR—there are also mixed-reality headsets and glasses for more immersive experiences, like last year's Samsung Galaxy XR and the upcoming Xreal Aura.

Eyewear brands Warby Parker and Gentle Monster are heavily involved in the new smart glasses, responsible for the design and fit, similar to how Meta tapped Ray-Ban for its competing—and market-leading—wearables. Google and Samsung are leveraging Warby Parker and Gentle Monster's brick-and-mortar presence and retail expertise, which lets people demo the glasses before buying them. (Kering Eyewear, which owns brands like Lindberg and Maui Jim, has also signed up to make glasses for Android XR in the future.)

The first models are available for preorder now, with official sales starting on December 10. Warby Parker's frames start at $395 and come in three styles—Alden, Reid, and Wren—as optical or sunglasses, with various prescription, lens, and width options. Gentle Monster's glasses start at $449 and come in six designs, various colors, and tints, with prescription options for most frames.

The hardware is identical across both brands. The glasses weigh around 41 grams (10 grams less than Meta's new Gen 3 glasses), have a nine-hour battery life, and include a charging case that can recharge the glasses up to seven times. They have a 12-megapixel camera with an LED indicator that shows everyone in eyeshot that you’re recording. There’s a button to capture photos and 90-second videos at up to 3K resolution with a press. A touch-sensitive temple piece lets you tap to activate the onboard Gemini assistant, or slide two fingers back and forth to control the volume of the audio coming out of the glasses' speaker.

They are the first devices to support the “Hey Gemini” hotword, though you can use the well-known “Hey Google” hotword if you'd like. Still, the new spoken wake phrase distinguishes the glasses as Gemini's “optimal form,” according to Juston Payne, Google's senior director of product management for XR.

“We felt that these are a Gemini-forward device,” Payne tells WIRED. “It's really you and Gemini going through your day together, so it was feeling a little dissonant for us. You're not talking to Google, you're talking to Gemini.”

Oddly, these glasses are only compatible with certain devices—Google Pixel phones, Samsung Galaxy phones, and iPhones—and only select models. You'll need a Pixel 7 or newer, Galaxy S or Z Fold running Samsung's One UI 9.0 or higher, and the iPhone 13 Pro or newer. A Google spokesperson tells WIRED the company will share more about how it plans to expand support to other phones in 2027 after launch.

The arms of the glasses are similarly thick.

Since you'll be buying the glasses from an eyewear brand, they will be the customer-facing party to handle any problems you may have with the device or prescriptions. On the backend, Payne says there are elaborate business integrations and handoffs, so the customer service workload is split between all the companies.

Google has already previewed Intelligent Eyewear glasses with displays built into the lenses—useful for showing navigation instructions or real-time captions for folks with hearing issues. However, they are not ready yet; Payne says they're currently being tested by “non-Googlers” and are still on the road map, though he didn't share a specific release window. As for plans to launch audio-only smart glasses, like Meta's recent Ray-Ban Audio, Warby Parker co-CEO Dave Gilboa tells WIRED, “There's a pretty robust pipeline of additional products.”

Smart glasses have been around for a few years, but they come in many forms. Unlike display smart glasses designed to plug into a laptop to mirror or extend your screen, or more immersive glasses like Meta's VR Glasses, which layer virtual experiences over the real world, Intelligent Eyewear is more akin to what Google pitched years ago with the original Google Glass.

You can hear notifications pipe through from your connected smartphone and respond to messages by speaking to Gemini. You can listen to music, take phone calls, and get audio navigation through Google Maps. Yes, you can snap photos and videos too, though Payne quickly notes that these glasses are designed more to have Gemini assist you throughout the day than to capture content.

That's likely because Meta has faced public scrutiny over how people use its smart glasses, so much so that it has spawned a new term for people wearing them: the Meta Creep (harkening back to when anyone wearing Google Glasses was called a “Glasshole”).

“We learned through Glass that you can make people uncomfortable if you're not clearly communicating,” Payne says.

To that end, Google is announcing a series of policies and features alongside the glasses to assuage concerns. They include tamper detection, so the camera will know if you tried to disable the indicator light. If you try to block the indicator light while recording, the camera will stop. When you ask Gemini to look at something, those visuals are not saved in your Gemini History, and Google says it will not use them to train AI models or target ads. The glasses will automatically redact the faces of people around you before Gemini processes the visual data. We've highlighted all of Google's privacy-focused announcements around its glasses in a separate story.

Gentle Monster's Intelligent Eyewear comes in six different styles.

Most of the features that Google is positioning around Intelligent Eyewear rely on Gemini. See a poster for a comedy show? Just ask Gemini for more information, or add it to your calendar. You can ask Gemini to add items to your shopping list or whip up a travel itinerary with hotel recommendations and save them to a note. Some of these are saved in the Gemini app, but it can leverage other apps on your smartphone. For example, Gemini can play music on Apple Music if you're on an iPhone. And if you use Samsung Notes on a Samsung phone, it'll know that's where you likely want your notes saved.

When you activate Gemini, it's technically a “Gemini Live” experience, meaning the assistant activates and you can have a free-flowing conversation. A swipe back on the glasses' arm will end the interaction. When you want it to “look” at something in front of you, be specific in your query; otherwise, it won't use the camera. You can ask it to look at a page in a book and summarize the content, or if you have a bunch of cocktail ingredients in front of you but don't know what to make, you can ask Gemini for suggestions and step-by-step instructions to craft a drink—at your pace.

Some Gemini controls are on-device and don't need to travel to the cloud, like asking the assistant to play the next track. Speaking of, early next year, Google says users can use gestures on devices like the Fitbit Air or Google Pixel Watch to control music playback or activate Gemini on the glasses right from their wrist.

Another big capability is translation. Gemini will automatically detect languages and translate them to your preferred tongue. You do need to stare at the individual to hear their words translated, and there's a limited range; the quality depends heavily on the environment and noise level, but it worked remarkably well in my controlled demos, with only a few errors. The translation tries to mimic the speaker's tone and voice so it feels like their own words.

There's an accessibility feature called Guided Vision, which provides audio descriptions for people with impaired vision, like when reading fine print or locating everyday items. Google is also collaborating with Aira so you can stream the glasses' feed straight to a human interpreter for real-time visual support.

To help demystify these features—and convince consumers that smart glasses aren't just a fad for tech bros—Warby Parker and Gentle Monster are both launching pop-up experiences in New York City this week. These pop-ups will run for about a month, and they’ll give people the chance to walk through the experience with a pair of nonprescription glasses to demo the Gemini capabilities.

“These are the original wearables that have been around for hundreds of years,” Warby Parker's Gilboa says. “A lot of people start by getting an eye exam in one of our stores, and then come in, do a demo, buy these—we just want to make it as easy and seamless as possible.”

Google launches Intelligent Eyewear glasses: $395+, pre-order today, ships Dec. 10

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

After years of development, Google is launching its second foray into smart glasses with Intelligent Eyewear that supports Android and iOS. Pre-orders start today at Gentle Monster and Warby Parker, with the audio glasses shipping on December 10.

Hands-on: Google and Samsung’s vision for Android XR glasses is 20/20

Intelligent Eyewear is meant to be worn all day rather than the sunglasses-heavy approach taken by others. The frames are 40-44 grams with the weight balanced evenly.

There is a 12-megapixel ultra-wide camera for photos and videos (3K 30FPS or 1080p). You get image stabilization and HDR processing, while four microphones work to provide spatial audio capture. Google is also quite proud of its noise cancellation work.

There are two open-ear speakers for balanced audio and clear vocals. Control is done by a touchpad that accepts taps and swipes, or via voice commands, with Google debuting a new “Hey Gemini” hotword.

Another way to control Intelligent Eyewear is through your Pixel Watch or Galaxy Watch, with Fitbit Air support coming next year:

Each pair features a physical on/off switch so you can “wear them as regular glasses only.” On the privacy front, there is a white indicator light when you take a photo or video that gets saved to your gallery. If the light is covered or maliciously disabled, the glasses will stop recording, with Google running a hardware check when they are charging. Recording also stops when you take off the glasses.

Notably, Gemini commands that invoke the camera — like visual search or translation — will not save images or videos to your Gemini History. These visuals won’t be used to train AI models or for ad targeting. Additionally, the glasses will “automatically redact bystanders’ faces from these visuals before Gemini processes them.”

Battery life (245mAh) is rated at nine hours on a single charge, with a fully topped-up charging case (Gentle Monster’s shown below) offering seven full charges. Power is transferred via contact pins located on the underside of the nose bridge.

Android XR runs on the Qualcomm Snapdragon AR1 Gen 1 with 2GB of RAM and 64GB of storage. Other specifications include Bluetooth 5.4, Wi-Fi 6E, and IPX4 water resistance. Samsung is responsible for engineering the hardware, while there are two eyewear brands at launch.

Warby Parker Intelligent Eyewear is available in three styles and a total of 14 variations. Alden, Reid, and Wren are inspired by the brand’s “most beloved silhouettes.” Pricing starts at $395 for optical and sun styles in Pine, Saddle, and Jet Black.

The Gentle Monster Intelligent Eyewear collection features six frames in bold colors like dark red, pink, gray, and yellow, with 16 total variations. They can be paired with tinted, clear, blue, or brown lenses, with prescription options available for most designs. Pricing starts at $449 and will be available in the US and South Korea.

Intelligent Eyewear can be used for contextual visual searches, asking Gemini about your day, and having it accomplish tasks.

Google Maps offers turn-by-turn directions, while there’s real-time Google Translate in over 70 languages.

Like on phones, Gemini Guided Vision “offers conversational audio descriptions to people with vision impairments.” This can be used to read fine print, identify color differences, find items, and explore unfamiliar places.

These glasses also serve as always-available headphones with support for YouTube Music, Spotify, Apple Music, and Audible. The Glasses companion app supports:

Pre-orders for Gentle Monster and Warby Parker Intelligent Eyewear start today with retail availability on December 10.
