Gift Cards Below Face Value – A Discount on Spending You’d Do Anyway

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

Buy discounted gift cards for the stores and restaurants you already use, and everything you buy with them costs less. Look for warehouse club multipacks, holiday bonus offers, and resale sites that guarantee the balance. Pay with a credit card that earns rewards on gift cards, and spend each one within a few months.

Paying $80 for $100 in restaurant gift cards takes 20% off meals you’d have eaten anyway. The catch is finding cards that cheap, then keeping the discount once you have them.

Start at a warehouse club. Costco says its restaurant gift cards typically sell at a 20% to 25% discount, as eGift cards or physical multipacks. Some restaurant chains add a bonus card over the holidays. The Cheesecake Factory’s annual holiday offer has given a $15 bonus card for every $50 in gift cards, usable only from Jan. 1 through Feb. 28.

Resale sites such as CardCash sell other people’s unwanted cards at discounts that vary by store. Kroger, the grocery chain, gives 2 loyalty points for every dollar spent on most gift cards, and every 100 points takes $1 off groceries, a 2% return.

Paying with a rewards credit card adds to the discount, but check its terms first. Cards that earn American Express Membership Rewards points, for one, earn none on gift cards. For online orders, start at a cash back shopping portal, which pays you back a percentage of the order, then pay with the gift card. Some stores pay no portal cash back on the part of an order paid by gift card, so read the store’s terms on the portal before you buy.

Then protect the discount. Check a resale card’s balance the day it arrives, since CardCash refunds a balance that comes up short only if you report it within 45 days of purchase. Don’t stockpile cards, either. A chain that closes unexpectedly can leave its cards useless, the Consumer Federation of America warns. And skip prepaid Visa and Mastercard gift cards, which add a purchase fee to the face value, such as $5 on a $50 card at Target.

Start with the store where you spend the most, and buy no more than about three months of your usual spending there at a time.

Nov. 12, 2014: The Philae lander touches down on a comet

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

The European Space Agency’s Rosetta mission launched March 2, 2004, on a 4-billion-mile (6.4 billion kilometers) path to Comet 67P/Churyumov-Gerasimenko. The mission consisted of the Rosetta orbiter and the Philae lander, both designed for in-depth study of the comet. The spacecraft arrived at 67P/C-G on Aug. 6, 2014, and began orbiting the body a month later; on Nov. 12, 2014, the Philae lander descended to the comet surface. Both the spacecraft orbiting a comet and the landing on the comet’s surface were firsts in space exploration history.

Unfortunately, there were some complications with the landing: The harpoon system that was designed to hold the lander in place didn’t work, causing Philae to bounce and to float and drift above the surface. When it finally came to rest, it was in a shadowy location now known as Abydos, limiting to the sunlight its solar panels received.

Despite these challenges, Philae was able to operate for three days on battery power, and still sent back photos and data about the composition of the comet’s dust and its analysis of the body’s magnetism. (In short, the comet didn’t have a magnetic field.) Additionally, the orbiter transmitted data about water and oxygen in the tail that led to new understanding of such comets’ makeup.

Elisa Neckar is senior production editor of Astronomy magazine, and the editor of the Today in the History of Astronomy feature on Astronomy.com.

2028 Honda CR-V Will Go Hybrid-Only, Following The Toyota RAV4's Lead

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

We've been living with affordable hybrid vehicles for almost three decades now, but it's taken a surprisingly long time for some of the best-selling cars in the U.S. to go hybrid-only, even for models that have offered reliable hybrids for years. Toyota started doing it with the current Camry and new RAV4, and now Honda has confirmed that the next-gen CR-V will only be sold with hybrid powertrains.

Honda says the seventh-generation CR-V will be revealed next year, in time for the SUV's 30th anniversary, and it will ride on a new platform and use the company's new two-motor hybrid system, which our own Andy Kalmowitz got to try out in a Civic prototype last year. This new powertrain is said to be more powerful and more efficient, and the modular architecture is more rigid while being lighter-weight. It should be cheaper for Honda to produce, too, and a new electric all-wheel-drive system will be a big improvement.

Somehow we didn't get a hybrid CR-V in the U.S. until the 2020 model year, when the fifth-gen model was facelifted. Since then, annual CR-V hybrid sales have grown almost tenfold, and it currently makes up more than 50% of total CR-V sales. With the price difference between the hybrid and the regular CR-V being so relatively minor, and the gains in driving experience being so large, it makes sense to just hybridize the entire lineup. This year will be the third straight year in which Honda sells more than 400,000 CR-Vs, and upping the average efficiency of that many cars can make a big difference.

Many U.S.-spec CR-Vs are currently built in Ohio (the rest being made in Indiana), and Honda just announced plans for a new $3 billion plant in Ohio that will focus on hybrids, though gas-only and fully electric cars will be built there as well. Production there won't start until 2029, but Honda envisions an annual capacity of 240,000 units. Honda says it will introduced 15 new hybrids by 2030, most of which will be oriented towards the American market.

One of those hybrids will be the model at the top of this slide, or at least something similar to it. That rakish fastback is officially called the Hybrid Sedan Prototype, and it's probably just the next-gen Accord that's due to come out in the very near future. It wears what will be Honda's new design language, and you don't have to imagine how it'll look on the CR-V — lightly camouflaged prototypes have already been spied, as you can see from the Instagram post below.

I think it's looking pretty good, though it's a shame that the CR-V will seemingly lose its signature vertical taillights. We probably won't have to wait long to see the CR-V; expect an actual auto show debut, simply because a car this popular can actually take the spotlight and be of interest to the regular people who still go to shows. A Detroit Auto Show debut in January seems like a safe bet, but Honda could wait for Chicago in February or New York in March.

Xash Brings Gold Rewards to Solana Stablecoin Users

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Home » Altcoins » Solana (SOL) » Xash Brings Gold Rewards to Solana Stablecoin Users

The platform aims to tackle DeFi’s reliance on short-term incentives.

Xash, a Solana-based application that rewards users with tokenized gold for stablecoin activity, has announced its public launch at Solana Breakpoint 2026 in London, scheduled for November 15–17.

The platform aims to change how users earn rewards in decentralized finance (DeFi) by offering gold instead of temporary token incentives. Users can earn rewards by lending stablecoins, providing liquidity and trading across established Solana DeFi platforms.

The launch comes as DeFi protocols continue to face a challenge: attracting users who stay after promotional rewards expire. Xash is betting that gold-backed rewards could offer a more sustainable alternative.

Many DeFi platforms use token rewards to attract deposits and trading activity. However, users often move their funds elsewhere once those incentives end, creating a cycle known as mercenary capital.

Xash wants to address this problem by rewarding ongoing stablecoin activity with tokenized gold rather than short-lived token campaigns.

The company says the approach is intended to encourage longer-term participation in DeFi while giving users an opportunity to accumulate gold.

It also aims to benefit DeFi platforms seeking sustained activity and stablecoin issuers looking to expand the use of their tokens.

“Stablecoins don’t have a supply problem. They have a usage problem. Incentive campaigns rent users for a few weeks, then they’re gone,” said Kevin Huynh, co-founder and CEO of Xash.

Xash aggregates DeFi opportunities into a single interface, evaluating available strategies daily and presenting users with options based on potential gold rewards.

Before committing funds, users can review associated costs and estimated 30-day rewards. They can then select an opportunity and authorize transactions directly through their own wallets.

As part of the launch, the company is introducing direct withdrawals of accumulated tokenized gold rewards to users’ wallets. It is also adding a curated liquidity vault built on Kamino, allowing users to deploy stablecoins through a single deposit instead of managing individual transactions.

Xash says its trading activity does not involve directional price bets or borrowed funds. However, this does not eliminate the risks associated with DeFi. Users remain exposed to the underlying protocols and market conditions, including potential losses when using the Kamino vault.

The project was founded by Kevin Huynh, who serves as CEO, alongside Vinny Lingham, co-founder of Civic and Gyft and a former general partner at Multicoin Capital; Alen Ribic, co-founder of SweepSouth; and Llew Claasen, former executive director of the Bitcoin Foundation.

Xash’s launch comes amid growing demand for tokenized real-world assets (RWAs), particularly gold-backed digital tokens.

According to the World Gold Council, the tokenized gold market has expanded fivefold since late 2024, reaching approximately $6 billion.

Trading activity has also accelerated. Tokenized gold generated more than $90 billion in spot trading volume during the first quarter of 2026 alone, surpassing the total recorded throughout 2025, according to CoinGecko research.

Xash tests whether real-asset rewards can retain DeFi users better than short-term token incentives. Its launch also reflects rising demand for tokenized gold within the Solana ecosystem.

SoFiUSD goes live on Polygon as bank-issued stablecoin expands reach

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The bank-issued stablecoin joins USDC, USDT, and PYUSD on Polygon Open Money Stack.

SoFiUSD is now live on Polygon, giving SoFi’s bank-issued stablecoin another blockchain for payments and settlement as the fintech expands its digital asset business.

In a statement shared with The Block on Thursday, Polygon Labs said the stablecoin is available on Polygon Open Money Stack, adding to existing Ethereum and Solana support. Polygon Labs said SoFi’s more than 17 million members can buy, sell, hold, and convert SoFiUSD on Polygon through the SoFi app.

SoFiUSD is issued by SoFi Bank, N.A., an OCC-regulated national bank, and is redeemable one-to-one for U.S. dollars. It joins Circle's USDC, Tether's USDT, and PayPal's PYUSD on Polygon Open Money Stack.

According to the statement, the integration gives SoFiUSD access to wallets, fiat rails, stablecoin conversion, and cross-chain settlement on a single interface, allowing customers to use the asset without the need for a separate wallet or manual bridging.

“SoFi is well positioned to bring stablecoins into mainstream finance because it combines the regulatory foundation of a national bank with the reach of a major consumer and enterprise technology platform,” Polygon Labs CEO Marc Boiron said.

The development is the latest in a series of crypto initiatives from SoFi this year. Last month, the company reported 503,000 crypto products at the end of the third quarter, while gross crypto transaction revenue reached $161.6 million, up 20% from the prior quarter.

SoFi also launched its tokenized deposit product in October, allowing customers to convert deposits into SoFiUSD at a one-to-one rate. In September, the bank began settling card transactions using its stablecoin on Mastercard’s network, weeks after agreeing to connect its SoFi Exchange Network with Kraken’s infrastructure.

Securitize and Ethena’s $100 million BlackRock BUIDL liquidity facility goes live

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The launch follows an earlier integration between Ethena Labs and Securitize to offer 24/7 atomic swaps between BlackRock's tokenized fund and Ethena's stablecoin.

Securitize and Ethena Labs have launched a $100 million, 24/7 liquidity facility for BlackRock's USD Institutional Digital Liquidity Fund (BUIDL). It will allow eligible investors to exchange shares of the tokenized fund for supported stablecoins, including USDC, outside traditional market hours.

The facility launches with $50 million in initial liquidity, split evenly between BUIDL and USDC, with another $50 million available within an hour as liquidity is used.

The initiative builds on the companies' existing collaboration around BUIDL and USDtb, Ethena's digital dollar product backed primarily by BUIDL. The firms first announced the $100 million facility in June.

"With this facility now live, we're taking another step toward a financial system where institutional investors can access liquidity when they need it, not just when traditional markets are open," said Carlos Domingo, co-founder and CEO of Securitize. "That's the infrastructure we believe will help bring more of the world's assets onchain."

The launch follows an earlier integration between Ethena Labs and Securitize in June 2025 to offer 24/7 atomic swaps between BlackRock's tokenized fund and Ethena's stablecoin.

"By bringing this facility online with Securitize, we're helping close the gap between traditional market hours and the always-on nature of blockchain markets," said Guy Young, founder of Ethena Labs. "It's another important step in making tokenized assets more useful for institutional participants."

The latest development also comes as BlackRock expands BUIDL's presence in onchain markets. In February, BlackRock and Securitize tapped Uniswap for direct onchain BUIDL trading, allowing eligible investors to trade the $2.4 billion tokenized Treasury fund through UniswapX.

Review: Harold Halibut (Switch 2) - A Creative Marvel That Suffers In The Back Half

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

This won’t be the first Harold Halibut review to mention being wowed by one of the game’s pre-launch trailers during its release on other platforms back in 2024. Back then, the game’s stop-motion aesthetic — with character models based on scans of real marionettes, and its tactile hand-wrought sets — looked like nothing I’d ever seen.

Coupled with the atmospheric music and the promise of engaging characters and ambitious emotional storytelling, it became, in my eyes, an absolute must-play. And of course it did – its story of a crashed spaceship and its crew, stranded on an ocean planet, looked like a Wes Anderson animation, like Isle of Dogs or Fantastic Mr Fox. In terms of defining what games are capable of, I was ready for Harold Halibut to set new standards. I was convinced it would be a sure-fire 10/10.

Now as I write this review, I wonder if part of the stunned feeling was just a little confusion about how such an ambitious storytelling production would translate into a game. And it’s a fair question – after finishing Harold Halibut and watching that trailer again, I notice very little gameplay. A little walking around perhaps, but not much else.

2024’s reviews were, indeed, mixed. The development team, Slow Bros, an independent game studio based in Cologne, had certainly left an impression. Some reviewers declared Harold Halibut a breath of fresh air and a work of art, while others clearly hit a wall when it came to a lack of gameplay. For me, at different times, I found myself in either camp.

My notes from the opening few hours are as excited as I’ve ever written. The pleasant world and characters set out their stall early. Harold Halibut is an enjoyable presence, a handyman surrounded as he goes about his days by a variety of memorable characters.

I enjoyed the atmosphere, the story, and how Harold’s Ship, the Fedora 1, is run by a quirky corporation called All Water, who themselves are balancing the commercial benefits of their transportation system with a lack of resources. Despite the risk for on-the-nose satire, nothing ever felt heavy-handed. At all times ‘character’ came first, to the game’s great benefit.

I controlled Harold as he went about his janitorial tasks — cleaning filters, clearing rooms of boxes, fixing machinery — and liked the enjoyable if simple gameplay loop of exploring the ship, the social areas, the science areas, and having the freedom to choose where to go and who to chat to. These moments — controlling Harold’s marionette, seeing all the lovely sets, and discovering layers and depth in the characters, helped by impressive voice acting — fulfilled the initial promise I’d seen in that trailer.

Harold Halibut was over 10 years in the making, and took less than 10 seconds to fall in love with. As a whole, it’s a beautiful work of art, a thought-provoking story with excellent character development. However, it’s best played docked due to performance issues (primarily frame rate) whilst in handheld. Regardless, still worth a play.

I was effectively playing an animation, and it was wonderful. For the best part of five hours, I was never bored, and in fact often delighted as little variations in the gameplay kept things fresh, as characters bonded, and as the plot became alive with subplots and the promise of a grand finale I just had to see.

But then, as my playtime extended, a few elements brought me back down to earth.

First, I sensed some bloat creep into the gameplay, and I started to feel a little less happy about the repetitiveness of running about delivering messages and catching up with the crew. Second, there was a subtle slowdown in the overall pace of the story. And third, the narrative clearly started taking over the gameplay a little too much. There were long periods where I either didn’t even need to press a button, or otherwise do little more than walk around to trigger the next cutscene.

Harold Halibut is, of course, a narrative-focused adventure. I’ve enjoyed games like Night in the Woods and Oxenfree, but for me the 11-hour runtime for the main storyline here could have done with a trim in the second half.

It’s not that the cutscenes were a problem, but coming back to short snippets of gameplay after long breaks only to be tasked with running back and forth multiple times between, say, two locations (which happened once) felt a bit like hard work.

Just when I wanted the storytelling momentum to build, Harold Halibut lost a little of its focus. The themes and creativity established during the early interactions with the cast — the delightful running-enthusiast postman, the shopkeeper trying to adjust his work-life balance, and Harold’s ex Sunny, struggling to define her life goals — faded into the background as Harold’s storyline took over.

I loved Harold, but except for one soliloquy, he had, for much of the game, seemed more of a fun, pleasant vessel for meeting the other citizens of the spaceship. This isn’t a big complaint, but it contributed to a sense of the second half leaving behind some of the things that made the opening so great.

The last thing to say is that the Switch 2 port isn’t quite as pretty as other incarnations, while still being playable and enjoyable. The camera stutters during zooms and pans, which are frequent thanks to the game’s cinematic style.

It’s a shame because the visuals are clearly one of Harold Halibut’s big strengths, but, ultimately, this is still a very playable port. I can’t say these issues impacted my enjoyment at all.

Back in 2024, I imagined writing a review for Harold Halibut and rounding up the final score to a 10. I like Harold Halibut a lot. I enjoyed the story, the heart, the characters — and of course I loved the visuals — but I also felt the balance of gameplay, narrative, and pacing wasn’t quite there for me, and that this had an overall impact on how well it works as a game.

Now looking back at those reviews from the original release — the spread of besotted 10s to the more cautious 6s — I think they all have a point. Harold Halibut is a creative marvel. It is a work of art and it’s worth playing to appreciate all that wonderful craft. But the pace can also drag and sometimes gameplay takes a little too much of a back seat. Ultimately, it’s a curiosity with a lot of heart. It struggles to balance its elements, but I’m very happy to have played it. If the visuals and storytelling style appeal, then it’s definitely worth a look.

5 Years Ago Today: Andrew Garfield Gave An Oscar-Worthy Performance In A Netflix Movie

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

There are, in my estimation, many actors who should have Oscars who don't. Amanda Seyfried should have one (and might be eligible for one in 2027 thanks to one of 2026's most confounding films). Amy Adams' various near-misses have led her to stumble headfirst into awful Oscar bait projects like "Hillbilly Elegy." John Goodman has never even been nominated. When it comes to the best actors who don't have Academy Awards, the list is long ... and Andrew Garfield is definitely on it.

Garfield, who rose to prominence in projects like "Doctor Who" and "Lions for Lambs" before earning international fame and acclaim for his pivotal role in "The Social Network," is genuinely one of the most talented performers of his generation. You could point to any number of his projects and make a case, but I'm going to say that Garfield should have an Oscar for playing "Rent" writer Jonathan Larson in the 2021 movie musical "Tick... Tick... Boom!" (for which he did earn a Best Actor nomination).

Even if you're not a reformed musical theater kid like me, you know about "Rent" — but generally, we need to talk about "Tick... Tick... Boom!" way more. Before "Rent," Larson — who died at the age of 35 in 1996 after suffering from an aortic dissection — penned the mostly-autobiographical "Tick... Tick... Boom!" about his struggles to make it in musical theater, coming on the heels of his failed futuristic musical "Superbia." Initially written as a solo show performed just by Larson on piano, the musical got a full-length treatment thanks to David Auburn after Larson's tragic passing, and "Hamilton" and "In the Heights" scribe Lin-Manuel Miranda adapted it into a film that released on November 12, 2021. Garfield's performance as Larson is electrifying, pitch-perfect, and gripping, and he should have an Oscar for it.

The "plot" of "Tick... Tick... Boom!" is relatively simple — Andrew Garfield plays Jonathan Larson as he works days at the Moondance Diner and spends his free time perfecting that aforementioned musical "Superbia" for an upcoming workshop. Along the way, he grapples with the idea of leaving the arts behind for a more lucrative and stable career — like his best friend Michael (Robin de Jesús), who works for an ad agency — while also stressing about turning 30 years old.

If you're familiar with "Rent," you may not be surprised to learn that the HIV-AIDS crisis plays a role; his friend Freddy (Ben Levi Ross) is HIV-positive, and it's eventually revealed that Michael is as well. As Jonathan tries to manage his personal and professional lives, he crosses paths with his idol Stephen Sondheim (played by Bradley Whitford, though the real Sondheim did lend his voice to an answering machine message before he passed away in 2021) and frets about whether or not "Superbia" has any chance of success.

Usually, the gift of hindsight doesn't really serve an actor, but I think knowing that Larson passed away before "Rent" won a Pulitzer Prize (and Tonys) gives Garfield a great perspective here. His Jonathan is frenetic, a live wire, and lives his life to the fullest; Garfield's passionate performance throughout the film paints a picture of a man who's running out of time, and even though that's obviously expressed through the character's anxieties about turning 30, it's also a poignant representation of an artist whose life was cut tragically short ... and in numbers like "Why," "30/90," and "Louder than Words," Garfield pays perfect homage to Larson's life and legacy.

I think I've been pretty clear about the fact that Andrew Garfield should have an Oscar for "Tick... Tick... Boom!" He should, however, have won his second Oscar for that movie — because Garfield should have an Academy Award for best supporting actor for playing Facebook co-founder Eduardo Saverin in "The Social Network."

Under careful direction from David Fincher and working with one of Aaron Sorkin's all-time best scripts — which he ultimately betrayed with his dog-brained sequel "The Social Reckoning" — Garfield is extraordinary as Eduardo, best friend to Jesse Eisenberg's Mark Zuckerberg who provides money and support during the initial founding of Facebook. In one timeline, we see Mark and Eduardo as Harvard University students trying to get their social media platform off the ground; in another, we see Eduardo suing Mark after Mark cruelly cuts Eduardo out of the company and brings his shares down drastically on the advice of new partner Sean Parker (a ridiculously well-cast Justin Timberlake).

Garfield should probably have several awards just for the way he delivers the line "Sorry, my Prada's at the cleaners, along with my hoodie and my f***-you flip flops" to Eisenberg's Mark after he discovers his betrayal ... but throughout the entire film, he's uniformly excellent as a man whose closest friend is also the most destructive force in his life. Garfield needs to be taken more seriously as an actor across the board, and an Oscar nomination for "Tick... Tick... Boom!" is a good start. In my opinion, though? He should be swimming in a pool full of trophies every day when he wakes up ... and he should have Oscars for "The Social Network" and "Tick... Tick... Boom!" Because the musical is a Netflix original, you can screen it there and see for yourself.

All of These Nebula Projectors Are up to $700 Off Ahead of Black Friday

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Pradershika Sharma is a tech deals writer for Lifehacker. She has a Master’s degree in English Literature, a B.Ed., and a TESOL certification. She has been writing professionally since 2018, creating product reviews, affiliate articles, and search ads for global clients while working with Rubix Agency and Cognizant. Previously, she spent a year teaching English at the junior high level.An avid reader since childhood, Pradershika's idea of extreme sports is staying up to read “just one more chapter.” She lives in India.

Black Friday is still a couple of weeks away, with the big shopping event falling on November 27, but Amazon's early Black Friday sale is already bringing some worthwhile discounts on Soundcore's Nebula projectors. Prices start at $299 for the compact Nebula P1i and go up to $2,799 for the premium X1S 4K laser projector, with savings of up to $700 across the models we've rounded up. There's quite a bit of variety here, too, from battery-powered projectors you can take camping or move between rooms to more powerful 4K models designed for a dedicated movie-night setup.

The Soundcore Nebula P1i is down to $299 from $399, while the Nebula P1 is $699.99, down from $799. Both offer 1080p resolution and Google TV, but their speaker setups are what make them interesting. The P1i has two 10W speakers that flip out and rotate to direct sound toward your seating area, while the P1's speakers detach completely and can run wirelessly for up to 20 hours. The P1 is also brighter at 650 ANSI lumens versus the P1i's 380, though you'll need a power outlet for either projector since neither has a built-in battery.

Small enough to slip into a bag, the Nebula Capsule 3 and Capsule 3 Laser are worth considering if you want something you can easily take on trips or move around the house. The standard Capsule 3 is down to $399 from $529, saving you $130, while the Laser version is $649.99, down from $749.99. Both offer 1080p resolution, automatic image adjustments, and up to 2.5 hours of battery life, although the Laser model is a little brighter at 300 ANSI lumens compared with the standard model's 200.

Taking movie night outdoors is a little easier with a battery-powered projector, and both the Nebula Mars 3 Air and Mars 3 are designed for just that. The smaller Mars 3 Air is down to $519.99 from $599.99, offering 1080p resolution, 400 ANSI lumens of brightness, and up to 2.5 hours of playback. The larger Mars 3, now $899.99 from $999.99, steps things up with 1,000 ANSI lumens, up to five hours of battery life in Eco mode, and a more powerful 40W speaker. It also has a weather-resistant design that's useful for camping or backyard screenings, though its extra bulk might make it less convenient to carry around.

If you're ready to move beyond 1080p, the Nebula Cosmos 4K SE at $1,099.99 (originally $1,299.99) offers a more affordable way to get 4K resolution. Its hybrid LED and laser light source delivers up to 1,800 ANSI lumens, while its Dolby Vision support helps bring out more detail in compatible movies and shows. You also get Google TV and automatic image adjustments for easier setup, though it doesn't have a built-in battery, so you'll need a power outlet nearby.

At the premium end of Nebula's lineup, the X1 and newer X1S are designed for a more serious home theater setup. The X1 is down to $2,299 from $2,999, saving you $700, while the X1S is $2,799 from $3,299. Both use triple-laser technology with 4K resolution, Dolby Vision, and optical zoom, but the X1S has a considerable advantage in brightness, reaching up to 6,000 ANSI lumens compared with the X1's 3,500. That extra brightness is useful in rooms with some ambient light, but the tradeoff is louder fan noise.

In short, yes, Black Friday still offers discounts that can be rare throughout the rest of the year. If there’s something you want to buy, or you’re shopping for gifts, it’s a good time to look for discounts on what you need, especially tech sales, home improvement supplies, and fitness tech. Of course, if you need to save money, the best way to save is not to buy anything.

Use price tracking tools to determine if the deals you're keeping tabs on are actually a deal. If you feel like that's too much work, we already do that for you, so follow our Black Friday coverage for the latest and best deals our team finds. Another good tip is to only buy what you need. It sounds simple, but making a list of the things you want before jumping into a sale with millions of products makes you focus and make smarter decisions.

Not all Black Friday deals are as good as they might seem. Unfortunately, Amazon is known to manipulate prices to trick you, but there are tools at your disposal you can use to see if the deals are actually good or not. If that seems like too much work, you can just follow our Black Friday coverage (we're already doing all the searching and vetting for you). Some other tips are making sure you're not being sold an older or cheaper version, and to always read reviews.

Mount Thor raises $45 million to build Mac data centers for AI agents

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Apple @ Work is exclusively brought to you by Mosyle, the only Apple Unified Platform. Mosyle is the only solution that integrates in a single professional-grade platform all the solutions necessary to seamlessly and automatically deploy, manage & protect Apple devices at work. Over 45,000 organizations trust Mosyle to make millions of Apple devices work-ready with no effort and at an affordable cost. Request your EXTENDED TRIAL today and understand why Mosyle is everything you need to work with Apple.

Mount Thor, a Bay Area startup building cloud infrastructure on Apple silicon, announced today that it has raised $45 million across Seed and Series A rounds. The pitch to its investors was simple: AI agents are incredible on Apple silicon, and nobody is renting them at data-center scale.

About Apple @ Work: Bradley Chambers managed an enterprise IT network from 2009 to 2021. Through his experience deploying and managing firewalls, switches, a mobile device management system, enterprise grade Wi-Fi, 1000s of Macs, and 1000s of iPads, Bradley will highlight ways in which Apple IT managers deploy Apple devices, build networks to support them, train users, stories from the trenches of IT management, and ways Apple could improve its products for IT departments.

CRV and Conviction co-led the Series A. Chad Byers and Offline Ventures co-led the Seed. CSC Leasing, Enea Capital, Long Journey Ventures, and Upper90 participated, along with angel investors from OpenAI, Meta, Cognition, Together AI, and Vercel. The company also raised separate non-dilutive financing for new hardware as well.

Mount Thor runs macOS on Apple silicon as elastic compute instances, just as a cloud provider rents Linux VMs. Customers get on-demand fleets of Macs for work that only happens on macOS: training computer-use models in reinforcement learning environments, running secure macOS sandboxes for agents, long-running instances that let an agent work across a user’s devices under one Apple ID, and build and test farms for Apple software.

There are two ways to use its solution. With managed compute, customers pick a machine class and image, and get either a full bare-metal Mac or an isolated macOS VM with administrator access, provisioned through a web portal, a command-line tool, Kubernetes, or the API, and billed per Mac on a 24-hour basis. With customer-managed compute, an organization runs its own Macs at a Mount Thor site using its own MDM, while Mount Thor operates the facility and provides inventory by serial number, problem reporting, and event notifications.

The argument is that agents are becoming macOS users, and the demand for Apple’s platform now scales with the number of agents rather than the number of people.

“Making Apple’s operating system and silicon available at datacenter-scale is a massive untapped opportunity in AI infrastructure,” said Peter Voell, Founder and CEO of Mount Thor. “Decades of investment in accessibility and human-computer interaction make macOS the best place for agents to work and for humans to hold them accountable. And with chips like the M5 Ultra, Apple silicon is becoming a high performance, more power- and cost- efficient platform for production inference.”

The other half of its pitch is inference. The M5 Ultra Mac Studio shipped in August with up to 512GB of unified memory, which puts large open-weight models within reach of a single machine at a fraction of the power draw of a traditional GPU-based server. Mount Thor says a focus of the new funding is building what it calls the best inference lab for open-source models on Apple silicon.

“Mount Thor sits at the intersection of two important shifts: the rapidly expanding capabilities of autonomous agents and the increasing demand for compute that can support both local and cloud workloads,” said Brittany Walker, General Partner at CRV. “We’re thrilled to back Peter and the team as they make it easier to use Apple’s platforms at scale for agentic AI.”

The team comes from Cerebras, CoreWeave, Fluidstack, Nebius, OpenAI, and xAI. The company was founded this year and deployed its first data center in under six months.

Macs in the cloud are not new as AWS has rented Mac instances for a few years, and MacStadium has been doing so longer than AWS. Mount Thor’s pitch is that the larger customer is an AI agent that needs a Mac to do its job, and that demand grows with the number of agents rather than the number of developers.

The company says the funding will go toward expanding its compute footprint and building out the team, and that it plans to add capacity quickly to keep up with customer demand.

Emotional Massaro pays tribute to Baresi: “Every day it’s harder and harder”

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Daniele Massaro has spoken about how much Franco Baresi meant to him, and how much he now misses the AC Milan icon.

The incredibly sad news broke over the summer that Baresi had passed away following a battle with illness. Thousands of fans lined the streets to pay tribute at his funeral, while the club have honoured him in several ways, including in the first home game against Venezia.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

Given his importance to Milan both as a player and then as an ambassador, it has left a huge hole in the hearts of many, above all his former team-mates and colleagues who shared a dressing room and/or an office with him for many years.

At the presentation of Mauro Suma’s book ‘Il Milan nel Mondo’ many former players spoke, including Daniele Massaro. He was visibly emotional during his speech, admitting it is actually getting harder to arrive at work each day and not have Baresi there.

“It was something rare in football. For me every day it’s harder and harder to walk into the office where he used to sit, I miss him. Franco will always be a part of me,” he said.

“I was lucky enough to meet him as an opponent one of the few times we played against each other in Serie B with Monza, then in ’82 we played in the World Cup together. At Milanello I would get changed in front of him, it seemed unreal.”

‘Star Wars: A New Hope’ to Premiere in Shared Reality From Cosm and Disney

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

The galaxy far, far away is about to get a lot closer.

Cosm and Disney have announced a multi-year, multi-film partnership that will bring the studio’s films to the theater’s shared reality experience, beginning with “Star Wars: A New Hope.”

Set to debut in March, Cosm’s LED Dome will immerse audiences in the ongoing battle between the Rebel Alliance and Darth Vader’s Galactic Empire. Viewers will land on Tatooine, join the Rebel Alliance on Yavin 4, step inside the Death Star and fly alongside the Millennium Falcon at lightspeed. Related Stories Charli xcx Joins Martin Scorsese's 'What Happens at Night' With Leonardo DiCaprio and Jennifer Lawrence (EXCLUSIVE)

“Since we’ve launched experiential cinema at Cosm, people have been asking for this film for the last year and a half,” Alexis Scalice, the VP and head of Cosm Studios, tells Variety. “We’ve listened, and trust us, we are just as excited. We can celebrate with the fans, too.”

The film, which will celebrate its 50th anniversary in 2027, will be screened in its original format on the center of the Dome’s LED screen, with immersive visuals surrounding it. When deciding which elements to incorporate into the experience, Scalice says she looks to the fans to understand what they prioritize and respond to, a process she calls “decoding the fan.”

“The film remains the hero, and how can we expand?” she says about the creative process. “We pick those moments that are almost like the greatest hits of an album, like the trash compactor scene and the lightspeed [in ‘A New Hope’]. But then there’s these other elements that may not have been a come-out scene, but in shared reality, it becomes a moment.”

“Star Wars: A New Hope” at Cosm is developed alongside Disney, Lucasfilm, Industrial Light & Magic and MakeMake and will be available at Cosm’s locations in Atlanta, Dallas, Detroit and Los Angeles, with tickets going on sale in 2027.

Disney is the third major studio to partner with Cosm, following Warner Bros. Pictures and Universal Film. The shared reality theater will next feature “Shrek” beginning Dec. 15, marking the first animated film to play at the Cosm.

LGBTQ Representation on TV Dropped in 2025-26, GLAAD Study Finds

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Despite high-profile successes like 'Heated Rivalry,' the overall picture last season was less rosy.

Despite the breakout success of Heated Rivalry and multiple Emmy wins for Pluribus, the 2025-26 season was a down year for LGBTQ representation on television.

GLAAD‘s annual “Where We Are on TV” report on the state of LGBTQ characters in scripted series shows a steep 25 percent drop in representation last season. The report counted 369 LGBTQ characters in shows on streaming, broadcast and cable outlets, down from 489 in the 2024-25 season.

The decline among transgender characters was particularly noticeable, going from 33 in 2024-25 to just seven last season — all of them on shows that are not returning this season. That doesn’t necessarily mean there will be zero trans characters on TV this season, just that the shows those seven characters appeared on in 2025-26 aren’t continuing, either because they were limited series, were canceled or ended ongoing runs. Related Stories TV 'Heated Rivalry' Gets Hands on With 4DX Screenings Movies 'Fruitcakes: Dawn of Drag' Goes Back to 'Happy Gays,' "Reclaiming History" Through a Timely Film (Exclusive Trailer and Chat)

“In the last couple years, trans people have been scapegoated by the media. They have been targeted in ways that are offensive and dangerous. Lives have been lost,” said Raina Deerwater, lead author of the report and associate director of entertainment research and analysis for the GLAAD Media Institute. “Often we see media reflecting that, and because of a lot of the political conversation, it’s not a surprise to see trans representation falling on TV. But it’s very disheartening, especially when there are so many great trans artists that are telling wonderful stories. At the moment, their shows aren’t being greenlit, so they have to tell them outside of traditional venues.”

Nick Adams, vp of the GLAAD Media Institute, said in a statement, “Authentic trans characters on television can show audiences that transgender people are their friends, co-workers, family, and neighbors. And yet, at a time when those stories are needed most, TV executives have turned away.”

The percentage of LGBTQ characters who are people of color also fell off, from 51 percent of all such characters in 2024-25 to 44 percent last season, the lowest mark in eight years. GLAAD advocates for a 50 percent benchmark for nonwhite LGBTQ characters on screen.

GLAAD’s report notes that some of the decline in the total number of LGBTQ characters can be attributed to a lower volume of shows produced last season. Still, the number of LGBTQ characters declined at a higher rate (25 percent) than the total number of series (about 15 percent).

Rainwater told The Hollywood Reporter in an interview that the overall decline in production may have made executives more risk-averse, leaning on familiar or recycled IP rather than original stories that might be more inclusive. Some of the decline also might be the aftereffects of the industry’s reaction to a perceived cultural shift following the 2024 presidential election.

“Because production takes so long, what happens sometimes is things get greenlit in a certain era and then don’t come out until later,” she said. “So it does feel a little bit like we’re behind the times like at this very moment.”

The flip side is that those winds can change, as seen by huge Democratic gains — often in the face of anti-LGBTQ, and specifically anti-transgender messaging — in last week’s midterm elections. “Art does reflect politics, and a lot of the conversations around the midterms are like, ‘We have a lot of work to make up,'” said Rainwater. “Looking at these numbers and the erosion of trans representation and queer characters of color hitting an eight-year low, we also have a lot of work to make up [on screen].”

There are some hopeful notes in the report: The number of characters who identify as nonbinary grew in 2025-26, and bisexual characters were a greater percentage of the total (25 percent) than a year earlier.

Rainwater also pointed to the successes of Heated Rivalry and Pluribus — whose lead character is lesbian — and Wednesday’s renewal of FX’s Adults for a 16-episode third season (several of its core characters are queer) as reasons for optimism.

“We have a lot of research that shows Gen Z specifically wants queer representation. They want new stories,” said Rainwater. “A show like Adults getting renewed is obviously top of mind, but it does show that people want to be reflected on screen. That show is also just good and funny, and putting queer people in a classic hangout sitcom and then giving them more episodes is a sure-fire way to success. It’s great to see that.

“The numbers are down, but this was also a year that a lot of queer-inclusive shows were breakaway hits. Obviously, there’s the Heated Rivalry of it all, so it’s definitely a thing of ‘Hey, take note of one of the biggest hits that managed to be monoculture in today’s divided attention economy.’ That’s really impressive. … We want to make sure that these shows are appreciated and [highlight that] these shows are breaking through in a way that not much TV is.”

The Nine Fast-Rising Stars Everyone Is Fighting to Cast

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Sitcom stars, YA heartthrobs and prestige players rule this year’s crop of the hottest young actors in Hollywood.

By Seija Rankin, Kirsten Chuba, Nicole Fell, Mia Galuppo November 12, 2026

From Broadway stand-outs to YA streaming successes to franchise players, these nine rising screen (cover) stars are catching the attention of both audiences and the entertainment industry.

Some are just breaking out, and others are breaking bigger. After years on the Amazon juggernaut The Summer I Turned Pretty, Lola Tung is gearing up for prestige turns in anticipated features from A24 and Neon, while Whitney Peak is readying for her biggest roles to date in the latest Hunger Games movie and Miami Vice, where she joins Michael B. Jordan. Elsewhere, Tom Francis is building his onscreen bona fides after a Tony-nominated musical run, while Keyla Monterroso Mejia may be the most prolific star working in TV right now. Related Stories TV 'Off Campus' Season 2 Sets April Premiere Date THR Cover Story The Emancipation of Charli D'Amelio: How the TikTok Queen Took Back Her Life

The 2026 class joins Next Gen alumni like Zendaya, Paul Mescal, Florence Pugh and, more recently, Black Panther newcomer David Jonsson. No matter how they got to this current moment, this talent is already in demand. As Heated Rivalry and Yellow Jackets standout Sophie Nélisse puts its, ”Career-wise, this is the busiest I’ve ever been.”

At a time when network sitcoms are nearly extinct and the average number of episodes in a season of the streaming comedies that do exist is in the single digits (and those seasons come with roughly the same frequency as Halley’s comet), Monterroso Mejia has managed to achieve the near impossible: ubiquity. From assisting Seth Rogen on Apple TV’s The Studio (as fictional Continental Studios executive assistant Petra) to sparring with Larry David on HBO’s Curb Your Enthusiasm (cast in his autobiographical series Young Larry) to drive-bys on everything from ABC’s Abbott Elementary to Netflix’s Nobody Wants This, she has built an enviable career out of going head-to-head with comedy greats.

It wasn’t always so easy. Having attended a performing arts high school in Pomona, California, the Baldwin Park-raised actress decided to jump right into auditioning. She says she had trouble even getting into rooms at the beginning — in fact, she mostly landed student work and short films (in one, she stars with Obsession breakout Inde Navarrette). “I tried for years and years — and then: King COVID,” she continues. Suddenly, self-tapes became the auditioning norm and the decision-makers had more time to dedicate to unknowns. Slowly, and then all at once, the industry began paying attention.

Looking back, Monterroso Mejia’s climb started in earnest with Curb. She was first asked to audition for a small part with only five lines of dialogue. A week later, she got a callback where she hopped on a Zoom with the show’s producers — and David himself. “I was like, ‘OK, dang, that’s why the show is so successful,’ ” she says. “They really care about even the smallest parts on the show.” The role morphed into a guest-starring arc that carried over Curb‘s final two seasons. Later, it was Abbott creator-star Quinta Brunson who told Rogen to check out Monterroso Mejia as he was casting his Emmy-winning Hollywood satire. While she initially auditioned for Quinn, the creative executive role that went to Chase Sui Wonders, Monterroso Mejia ended up being offered the role of Petra after that first audition. In film, she’s already shared screen time with everyone from Keke Palmer (2025’s One of Them Days) to Will Ferrell (the Prime Video comedy You’re Cordially Invited), and there are more projects on the way.

“I wrestled a lot with how much of it was luck, how much of it was talent and how much of it was work ethic — or, maybe people are just kind,” she says of her successes. But at 28, she’s come to a new realization: “Maybe I’m the common denominator.” — M.G.

WHAT HAS AND HASN’T CHANGED THE MOST IN MY LIFE ”What hasn’t changed at all has been my brother. Still very much the same, very annoying, very nice, love him. And what has changed is everything else. I’ve been so lucky to have worked consistently in the last couple of years, so what my life looked like is completely different to now.”

MY BIGGEST PINCH ME MOMENT FROM THE PAST YEAR ”Being back for The Studio, season two, is very crazy. After the very well-deserved success that season one had, to be back on set with everybody was insane.”

THE ACTOR I LOOK UP TO MOST RIGHT NOW IS ”Right now, just because I just finished watching Lanterns, is Kyle Chandler. My king can do no wrong. 10 out of 10 across the board. I was watching Kyle Chandler in awe. Just so present and alive.”

MY MOST-PLAYED ARTIST THIS YEAR WILL BE ”This is music-related, but not. I have been addicted to Crime Junkies and everything under their banner. I went from Crime Junkies to Park Predators. But since I have picked up listening to that specific genre of podcast, I have not listened to music as much. I probably listen to music maybe 5 percent of my time, and the rest is Crime Junkies. It’s taken over my life.”

Keyla Monterroso Mejia was photographed Oct. 7 at Viceroy Santa Monica. Viviano trench; Yvonne Leon earrings; L’ Dezen earrings; Viltier earrings.

Hair: Mel Dominguez. Makeup: Bryan Mendez.

Peak’s acting career didn’t have the smoothest start. The star, who was born and raised in Uganda until her family moved to Canada when she was 10, signed to a fake agency just as she was starting out as a child actor. She was sent on auditions that were later revealed to be a scam. The early setback didn’t stop her, however.

In fact, at 23, Peak has a résumé that would be impressive for someone twice her age, having led HBO Max’s Gossip Girl reboot for two seasons — “All the fashion, all the locations, all the access; it was a movie, honestly!” — and starred in both Netflix’s Chilling Adventures of Sabrina series and Disney’s Hocus Pocus 2. She credits acting with helping her break out of her shell, acknowledging that auditioning didn’t always come easy. “I would just absolutely be shaking uncontrollably, my whole body, and I was just really shy,” says Peak. “I would choke up and start crying; it was actually a mess.”

She’s about to enter her biggest arena yet as one of the stars of The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping, a role that she almost didn’t put herself up for. Peak’s character (Lenore Dove Baird) called for some singing, which nearly scared her off — she assumed a voice like Rachel Zegler’s (who starred in 2023’s The Hunger Games: The Ballad of Songbirds & Snakes) would be expected. To her great relief, Broadway-caliber belting was not required.

Once Peak is done with the world tour she’s currently on to promote Hunger Games, she has another blockbuster waiting for her back home. Yes, she’ll be joining Michael B. Jordan and Austin Butler in Miami Vice ’85, which has already started shooting. The buzzy project will also reunite Peak with director Joseph Kosinski, with whom she worked on a short film for Chanel’s Coco Mademoiselle fragrance in 2023. (As a brand ambassador for the fashion house, she admits she’s become a “nerd” about “different collections and different designers” as well.)

It’s a lot of big, commercial projects for a self-described “indie girl,” but Peak isn’t complaining. “I’ve read some really great scripts, but nothing hits quite like being on a huge-scale movie and getting to work with every actor you’ve ever admired ever,” she says, referring to a Hunger Games cast that includes Glenn Close, Elle Fanning and Jennifer Lawrence. “And I’m just excited to work, so whatever that looks like is good for me.”

Looking ahead, she has a list of directors with whom she dreams of working, including Joachim Trier, Janicza Bravo, Savanah Leaf, Eva Victor and Raven Jackson. More recently, she’s added Primetime and You Can See Everything‘s Lance Oppenheim to said list. But Peak is working on herself, too. “Just kind of building my arsenal,” she says, “and making sure that I’m equipped to do whatever I do want to do next.” — K.C.MY FAVORITE MUSICAL ARTIST IS ”Nina Simone. I didn’t know who she was until I was maybe 14. The first time I heard a Nina Simone song, I was in a hair and makeup trailer on one of the first jobs I ever did. Tati Gabrielle, who was on Chilling Adventures of Sabrina, was in the makeup trailer, and I don’t even know if she remembers this, but she was playing music and she played Nina Simone, and I was like, ’Who is this?’ Then I went down like a deep rabbit hole.”MY MOST IMPACTFUL PROFESSIONAL EXPERIENCE HAS BEEN ”I feel like it’s always the last one, because you’re just always learning on every single job. You take the tools that you got from the last one and then bring it to the next one. So, probably The Hunger Games. That was incredible, no complaints.”THE ACTOR I LOOK UP TO MOST RIGHT NOW IS ”I love Viola Davis, always. Everything she does is incredible, and I want her on my screen all the time.”AN ACTOR MY AGE I ADMIRE IS “Florence Pugh. She’s done so much. God, I’ve gotta get to work.“

Whitney Peak was photographed Oct. 16 at Viceroy Santa Monica. Full look: Chanel.

Hair: Ursula Stephen. Makeup: Tasha Reiko Brown.

For a brief moment in between the first and second seasons of Adults, Thiele found himself getting a little maudlin. The actor was deeply attached to the FX sitcom about 20-something friends living together in Queens. It premiered in 2025 to positive reviews but was, by all accounts, rather niche. “It felt like we had this special little show that we all loved but that maybe didn’t resonate with the masses, and that was really scary,” he says. “For the first time ever, I told myself, ‘That’s life. You love something and you have to let it go.’ “

Then the renewal announcement came down from on high, and Thiele returned to himself. “I was like, never mind, screw that message, screw that moral,” he adds with a laugh. Adults‘ second season, which aired this fall, proved to be a boon not just for the hangout show genre — its viewership grew 40 percent — but also for Thiele’s career. Suddenly, he is everywhere. In addition to being the biggest breakout star of the FX series, on magazine covers and at A-list parties, he’ll be in next year’s A24 comedy Peaked and the 13 Going on 30 reboot. (And he conducted this interview from the New Zealand set of a yet-to-be-announced horror film.)

“People think I popped out of nowhere, but I’ve been working at this for 10 years,” says the 30-year-old. He grew up in L.A. and attended the prestigious Crossroads School, where he honed his creativity and knack for building relationships; most of his early work was alongside childhood friends like Cazzie David and Molly Gordon. But he was pushing his Hollywood career forward the old-fashioned way, too. “I’ve done many a self-tape that should be burned. I’ve done all sorts of bit parts. I was waiting for a role that I could sink my teeth into,” he says, adding that because of his extended entrée into stardom, he doesn’t feel the pressure to capitalize on the success of Adults the way one might expect. “I know there are ebbs and flows in a career, and there are very few things being made, and very few of them are needing roles for a Black gay man,” he continues. But he feels optimistic about the new crop of young writers who are intent on making diverse and inclusive projects, and also about his future on FX: “Hopefully I get to keep doing Adults for the rest of my life — or at least until we’re in our late 30s.” — S.R.

WHAT HAS AND HASN’T CHANGED THE MOST IN MY LIFE ”I’m never really home, because I’m in Toronto filming Adults. And I miss my parents. You guys, come on the road! Get to Toronto. Everything else is exactly the same. I’m still doing my boyfriend’s laundry.”MY MOST IMPACTFUL PROFESSIONAL EXPERIENCE HAS BEEN ”Working on Theater Camp with Molly Gordon directing. I grew up with her, we were in plays together. To watch her step into those shoes and telling me what to do was really, really fulfilling.”RANDOM SKILL I PICKED UP ON A PROJECT ”I wrestle a raccoon to the floor in Adults. That’s a skill I didn’t know I had. So if you have raccoons in your backyard, call me.”MY FAVORITE PLACE IN LA ”It’s cheesy, but my parents’ house. I love being on their couch.”

Owen Thiele was photographed Oct. 16 at Viceroy Santa Monica. Calvin Klein jacket, shirt, jeans; Thiele’s earrings.

Hair: Coree Moreno. Makeup: Shannon Pezzetta.

Creed-Miles never wanted to be part of Hollywood. She grew up watching her mother, veteran actress Samantha Morton, put herself through the wringer in service of her vocation, and she says she learned at a very young age not to covet fame. But at 16, her mother’s agent coaxed her into auditioning for the part of a young Ruth Wilson in the 2017 thriller Dark River. “Very quickly, I had this feeling of seeing what I was doing and that I was impressing them and having an emotional impact,” says the actress, who adds: “And I really liked feeling like I was good at something.” She was offered the job that day.

From there, Creed-Miles, now 26, began building the career she never knew she wanted. Before long, she was starring opposite Joel Kinnaman in the 2019 Prime Video thriller series Hanna. “As a kid, I was always with my mom on her jobs, so I understood exactly what it meant to be on a set,” she says. “I felt like such a grown-up on Hanna, even though I wasn’t.” But it was Creed-Miles’ turn as Marianne Dashwood — a more wild, nearly feral version of the lovelorn literary stalwart — in this year’s remake of Sense and Sensibility that has the town talking. Ironically, Creed-Miles almost didn’t audition for the role, having convinced herself that a project as lofty as a Jane Austen adaptation would call for a more extroverted actress. (“I’m not the best people person, so I didn’t think I had a chance in hell,” she explains.) In the end, Creed-Miles was drawn in by the cinematic bona fides of the production: a budding auteur in director Georgia Oakley and a mandate to shoot on 35mm. “It reignited my passion for filmmaking,” she says now.

Already, the film has sent her on her first global press tour and scored her her first major nomination — outstanding supporting performance — at the upcoming Gotham Awards. Still, she insists none of it has changed her outlook on fame: “It’s pretty unfair that women are expected to have this whole persona outside of their professional work, but men whose careers I’ve really obsessed over, like Daniel Day-Lewis or Joaquin Phoenix, are allowed to have very mysterious or even nonexistent public personas,” she says. “I just love the idea of showing up to be a weirdo in a movie and disappearing again.” — S.R.

MY FIRST AUDITION WAS… ”I’ve been doing this since I was six, so I have no clue. I did a Harmony Korine film as a kid and I think I had dinner with him and my mom, so maybe that was an audition of sorts.”THE ACTOR I LOOK UP TO MOST RIGHT NOW IS ”I’ll shout out Kristen Stewart, because I’ve worked with her a few times now and she’s an amazing filmmaker and always has really important things to say.”RANDOM SKILL I PICKED UP ON A PROJECT ”I’m not a horse girl, but I did learn horseback riding for Sense and Sensibility. They’re so gentle and kind, and it’s very liberating to be on a horse.”MY FAVORITE PLACE IN LA ”Honey Hi for brunch. They do this amazing bacon breakfast bowl and some really good gluten-free pancakes.”

Esmé Creed-Miles was photographed Oct. 7 at Viceroy Santa Monica. Tom Ford shirt, tights.

Like many young actors this year, Kalyn plays a hockey player on a buzzy television show. Unlike most actors playing hockey players, Kalyn actually grew up playing the sport. “Everyone is very comfortable in their skates now,” he says of his fellow actors, though it was clear his expertise made him the de facto captain of the Off Campus training sessions season one. Now, he jokes: “They’re all going to be ready to play beer league soon.”

This year has been a game-changer for Kalyn, who’d been building a résumé of recurring roles (on Essex County, Cruel Intentions, Gen V) before he landed the role of Dean Di Laurentis in Prime Video’s red-hot hockey romance. For the show’s upcoming season, set to premiere in April, the 28-year-old’s character (alongside Mika Abdalla’s Allie Hayes) will be at the center of the story, as his co-stars Belmont Cameli’s and Ella Bright’s characters were season one. “To watch them go through it was such a great learning lesson for me,” says the actor, who’s already seen his profile soar, as evidenced by his nearly 4 million new followers on Instagram. Once the first season dropped — reaching 36 million global viewers over its first 12 days — Kalyn’s phone began ringing in ways it never had before. In short order, he booked ad campaigns, brand partnerships and new Hollywood projects, including The Housemaid’s Secret, a follow-up to the 2025 film that has him starring alongside Sydney Sweeney, Kirsten Dunst and Paul Anthony Kelly. Having seen the first installment twice in the theater — once with his fiancée, once with his mom — he was admittedly nervous when he first got to set. But he’s since settled in, he says: “I’ve found my fitting in this story now.” — N.F.

MY FIRST AUDITION WAS… ”One of my first auditions would’ve been for a Pizza Pop commercial. I really thought I nailed it, and turns out I didn’t. I didn’t get a call back. I got a Tim Hortons commercial in Canada. They flew me to Mexico. I remember doing it, and I thought: Once this comes out, I’m going to be huge. I’m going to be the face of Canada. It’s going to be unbelievable. That didn’t happen.”

RANDOM SKILL I PICKED UP ON A PROJECT ”I should have known how to do it, but I had to learn how to properly chop onions. There’s a technique to it. It’s not as simple as you think it is. This is for The Housemaid [2], the director came over and he’s like, ’You know how to cut onions, right?’ And I’m like, ’Yeah. Of course I do. Totally. Just one second.’ I went around the corner, I went on TikTok and I quickly found how to cut onions. When you see it on the big screen, you’re going to think, ‘That guy’s a really good onion cutter.’”

MY MOST IMPACTFUL PROFESSIONAL EXPERIENCE HAS BEEN ”As much as I would love to talk about a lot of them, it would’ve been doing season two of Off Campus. There are so many things and emotions and feelings that I had to discover.”

THE ACTOR I LOOK UP TO MOST RIGHT NOW IS ”I always grew up loving Jim Carey. But then, as time went on, I’d say Leo DiCaprio.”

”Career-wise, this is the busiest I’ve ever been,” says the Yellowjackets star. “It’s the first time I’ve not stopped for 10 months straight.” Nélisse got her start at age 11, with the French-language Canadian film Monsieur Lazhar. Now 26, Nélisse worked steadily through her teenage years and into her 20s, ultimately landing a lead role on the Showtime drama about a high school soccer team stranded in the woods after a plane crash.

But as Yellowjackets prepares to close out its four-season run, Nélisse has found herself in an enviable position with a slew of new projects on the horizon, including a starring role in Netflix’s adaptation of the Carley Fortune novel This Summer Will Be Different. It’s a 180 from Yellowjackets, she says, having quite literally gone from crying with dirt under her nails one day to flying to Prince Edward Island, where she immediately went into fittings, got hair extensions and a fake tan.

But the lion’s share of the heat surrounding Nélisse lately stems from a two-episode arc in last winter’s hockey juggernaut Heated Rivalry. Her character, superstar actress Rose Landry, dates Hudson Williams’ Shane Hollander and, in one of the show’s most memorable scenes, helps him come to terms with the fact that he’s gay. Nélisse is set to return for the show’s highly anticipated second season, which is filming in Toronto. She’s still making sense of the series’ outsize success — and her place within it.

“When articles come out and they say ‘star of Heated Rivalry,’ I’m like, ‘I have one scene!'” she jokes. The irony, she reveals, is that she didn’t prepare particularly hard for said scene, even if she’s immensely proud to be a part of it. It certainly wasn’t the “weeks of just sobbing and the most gut-wrenching scenes” she did on Yellowjackets. Instead, she recalls: “I came in, learned my lines and I was like, ‘All right, let’s do this!’ I didn’t think about it twice.” — N.F.

WHAT‘S CHANGED THE MOST IN MY LIFE IN THE PAST YEAR ”Wrapping up Yellowjackets. It had been this stability for the last almost 10 years of my life. Knowing that I was coming back and knowing there was going to be more than one season and also knowing that I was going to be reunited every year with these actors — that really felt like the end of an era and sort of closing a chapter of my life.”

MY FAVORITE PLACE IN LA ”I’m so embarrassed to actually say my response. Now I need to say it. My favorite place, not to visit, but to go to would be the Erewhon hot bar, which is the most embarrassing answer ever. What can I say? I love it.”

The face that launched a thousand ships? Not exactly, but Tung was at the center of the internet’s hottest debate last year when social media took sides over who her character, Belly, should end up with on the hit Prime Video series The Summer I Turned Pretty. (Jennifer Lawrence recently admitted that when she publicly declared herself Team Jeremiah, “The level of hate that I got was bigger than any controversy I’ve ever been involved in in my life.”)

Tung, a 24-year-old New York City native and an alum of LaGuardia High School, led the show for three seasons in her professional acting debut. “I couldn’t have had a better first experience,” she says. She’ll return, alongside her co-stars Christopher Briney and Gavin Casalegno, for a feature film follow-up to the series, but she’s also working with Gracie Abrams and Connor Storrie in Halina Reijn’s upcoming A24 film Please and starring opposite Nicole Kidman and Nico Parker in Osgood Perkins’ horror The Young People. “Belly is such a huge part of me, so I was like, as this chapter is coming to an end, let’s dive into something super different,” she says. And although she’s not out to be the next scream queen — as an admitted “scaredy cat” — she emphasizes, “It’s really cool to be like, ‘Oh, this is how they do the blood and the grossness of it all.’ ” And when the blood and guts are washed off, Tung is busy becoming a fashion “It” girl, serving as a global ambassador for Coach and stepping out on the fashion show circuit. She’s also no stranger to the stage, having made her Broadway debut in 2024 with a limited run in Hadestown.

Looking ahead, Tung says she’d like to add to her already impressive list of female filmmaker collaborators, with Chloé Zhao at the top of her wish list, and hopes to write and direct herself one day — in a return to what drew her to the creative world in the first place. “I used to make music videos with my friends in elementary school, and I was always a really bossy director,” Tung recounts with a laugh. “It was always super fun for me — not fun for my friends, who were like, ‘We want a break, please!’ But I love every aspect of it, and it’s been really cool now to work with so many directors who are so collaborative and get a peek into what that world looks like.” — K.C.MY MOST IMPACTFUL PROFESSIONAL EXPERIENCE HAS BEEN ”It’s funny because you learn something from every single experience that you have, obviously. When Francis was starring in Sunset Boulevard on the West End and on Broadway, he says he approached the experience like a racehorse. “It was like I had those blinders on to block everything out,” he explains. “That was the only way that I could get through it and do the job that I wanted to do.” The strategy worked, of course. He earned an Olivier Award and a Tony nomination, and his performance as Joe Gillis — which included the much-talked-about title-song sequence in which Francis left the theater every night to perform to a Steadicam on 44th Street — caught the attention of big-time directors, including Peter Berg, who was so taken that he offered Francis a role in his new film The Mosquito Bowl. The movie, which centers on a real-life group of star college football players who enlist in World War II, marks Francis’ first major screen role.

The 27-year-old grew up in Suffolk, England, and graduated from drama school during the pandemic, falling quickly into a theater career — which he says actually gave him an advantage on the high-octane, occasionally chaotic production full of combat scenes. “I’m very used to being told, ‘You have to hit your light here every time.’ ” Berg also wrote in a singing scene just for Francis. “It’s factually incorrect because my character wasn’t a singer [in real life], but I figured Broadway fans might watch this movie,” Francis explains. “You’ve got to give the people what they want.” Next, he will be seen in a recurring role on season two of Benito Skinner’s Overcompensating — after that, he will play Richard Gere’s son in the Apple TV series Off Weeks. There was a time when Francis kept lists of his dream jobs, but lately he has simplified. He just wants to work with people who push him. “Benny definitely expected more of me than I thought I could give,” he says of the quippy Prime Video comedy, which he describes as the “most joyous” job he’s ever had.

It’s been a whirlwind year of firsts, but Francis says the most exciting thing to happen to him so far was his recent appearance at the Academy Museum Gala. “It was one of those moments where I looked around the room thinking, it’s filled with the most inspiring people to me,” he says of a crowd that included Taylor Swift, adding: “I’m a Swiftie now.” — S.R.AN ACTOR MY AGE I ADMIRE IS ”Tom Blyth and David Jonsson. I watched Wasteman, and it’s so brilliant. I’d love to try and work with those two at some point in my life.”RANDOM SKILL I PICKED UP ON A PROJECT ”In Rent, there’s a song called ’Light My Candle’ and we had to light the candles while walking at a perfect pace. The candle would go out all the time. I got really good at lighting matches.”MY MOST-PLAYED ARTIST THIS YEAR WILL BE ”Deep sleep piano. I’ve recently gotten into going to sleep with this lo-fi music in the background. So that’s running the whole of the night. But after that, I’d say Matt Corby. I’m really loving his Rainbow Valley album at the moment.”MY FAVORITE PLACE IN LA ”It’s my friend Ray Nicholson’s house. We call it Casa Mysterio because his nickname is Ray Mysterio. His house is very calming.”

Tom Francis was photographed Oct. 16 at Viceroy Santa Monica. Dolce & Gabbana shirt, pants, tee, belt; David Yurman jewelry.

Hall’s collegiate years at Northwestern were primarily dedicated to the basketball team. But when the 6-foot-5 L.A. native realized a future in the NBA wasn’t in the cards, he turned his focus to his other passion — entertainment. It helped that he was already a film major. As was all but inevitable growing up with Julia Louis-Dreyfus and Saturday Night Live‘s Brad Hall as parents, he is a considerable talent and a major comedy fan. One of his first onscreen appearances was in a supporting role in the Amy Poehler-directed 2021 Netflix comedy Moxie, which boosted both his résumé and his confidence. “Amy would laugh occasionally at stuff I was doing,” he says, “and I remember thinking, ‘OK, if someone like Amy thinks I don’t suck, then maybe I don’t suck!’ “

The next few years saw the now 29-year-old jumping from multi-episode arcs and supporting turns in series like Mindy Kaling’s The Sex Lives of College Girls, Ryan Murphy’s Monster franchise and, most recently, Apple TV’s Maximum Pleasure Guaranteed, where he starred opposite Tatiana Maslany. Up next is his biggest project to date: HBO’s The White Lotus, where his castmates will include Laura Dern, Steve Coogan and Ben Kingsley. “It was a really cool moment calling my family and telling them,” he says, laughing as he catches himself: “Even though I think you’re not supposed to do that. Whatever! They can’t fire me now!” Production on the series’ fourth season, which will be set in France, has already stretched well over six months. (“I’ve certainly had enough steak frites to last a lifetime,” he jokes.)

Hall should be hopeful that the Mike White anthology becomes the same kind of springboard for him that it was for his predecessors, be it Sydney Sweeney, Sam Nivola or Patrick Schwarzenegger. Though, in Hall’s case, he’s already lined up several projects, in front of and behind the camera. “For the first couple of years, acting was in the driver’s seat only because, from a career standpoint, I had more traction as an actor than I did as a writer,” he says, and he’d still love to tackle a rom-com or a Hoosiers-esque sports movie, putting those latent basketball skills to good use. But the interest in Hall as a multihyphenate is growing now, too. In fact, he was recently tapped to write a series about a college fraternity drug ring in development at Netflix and a sci-fi high school comedy for Universal and Phil Lord and Chris Miller. — M.G.

WHAT HAS AND HASN’T CHANGED THE MOST IN MY LIFE “I have spent the last five-ish months in France shooting The White Lotus, which has been so much fun. Living in another country where they don’t speak English is quite the culture shock, but it’s been amazing. And something that hasn’t changed at all is that I am still very tall, so I still hit my head on stuff constantly, no matter where I live.“

RANDOM SKILL I PICKED UP ON A PROJECT “The first like three shows I did, all of them required me to roller skate.”

MY FIRST AUDITION WAS… “My first in-person audition was for the show Winning Time on HBO. It’s a basketball show, and I played basketball in college, so I really think they just wanted to see if I could act at all because they wouldn’t have to hire a double for the basketball stuff. I went into the room, and I did the scene, and then they said, “Okay, great. We’ll move on to the next scene.” I had no idea what they were talking about. It turned out that I had printed the script, and my printer, unbeknownst to me, was doing double-sided printing. There was another scene on the back that I had not read.”

FICTIONAL CHARACTER I IDENTIFY WITH ”Coach Taylor, Friday Night Lights.“
