I tried Google’s AI glasses and Meta’s finally got real competition

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The AI experience is better, the privacy protections are more proactive, and they’re still stylish.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

It’s a weird time to launch a new pair of smart glasses.

These last few years, Meta has been the dominant player in pushing a fragile smart glasses resurgence. Unfortunately, its privacy reputation led to the “pervert glasses” moniker. Smart glasses have never been more unpopular.

But in the past week, I’ve been demoing some new smart glasses from Samsung, Warby Parker, and Gentle Monster. Some are retro chic frames. Others have the same Y2K vibe as the Matrix films. All were made by Samsung and have Google’s Gemini built in via Android XR, Google’s platform for smart glasses and mixed-reality headsets.

The AI demos are better than I expected. In one, I’m sitting in an avant-garde art installation as creepy heads float above my head. On the loudspeaker, words blare in French, Spanish, Korean, and German. Pressing two fingers to my temple, Gemini starts translating each language seamlessly in real time. In another demo, a TV chef cooks a “monster pasta” recipe in Korean while people have a conversation behind me. Music in another language is playing overhead. This exact scenario tripped up the Meta Ray-Ban Display’s translation capabilities when I was on vacation. Here it’s no big deal. I don’t even have to specify the languages I need translated or, as with Meta’s glasses, download a specific language pair to translate. I sit at another table with an English speaker, a Thai speaker, and the glasses translate everything they say to me — correctly — into Japanese.

But I was most surprised that the glasses automatically redact faces from any photo or video to protect bystander privacy. I asked Gemini to identify my video director, Owen Grove. Gemini told me it knew I was looking at a person, but it can’t identify him because of privacy protections. We pull up a picture of our editor-in-chief, Nilay Patel. There, too, Gemini said that for privacy reasons, it can’t identify a person from a photo either.

Meta’s glasses also can’t do this. But what’s striking here is that Google is making an active choice to take facial recognition completely off the table.

The Warby Parker and Gentle Monster glasses have the customary privacy LED light that lights up whenever there’s an intent to save visual data. Like Meta’s glasses, there’ll be protections in place to disable the glasses if tampering is detected. However, when I sit down to chat with Juston Payne, Google’s senior director of product management for XR, he says Google intends to go much further than the LED light.

“Somebody might come up with some solution [to tamper with privacy LEDs] that our algorithms can’t detect, so then the question becomes: So what can we do?” says Payne.

“We’re not going to target ads on it. We’re not going to train Gemini on it. Humans aren’t going to be able to use it to improve the models. We’re also redacting faces before they actually go to the server in the first place, which then gets ahead of certain things that can obviously go sideways if you don’t take those steps proactively.”

It’s not just faces that’ll get redacted either. Payne tells me that ambient noise — like bystander conversations — will also get scrubbed. The glasses will only process audio from the glasses-wearer using beam-forming mics. Camera and mic access will also require opt-in permissions.

Alone, these are a bold departure from Meta’s approach to smart glasses. Locally redacting faces and ambient audio, for example, is a conscious choice to give up a valuable source of training data. Google could leave it at that, and Android XR would already have a leg up in the privacy discussion. But Payne says Google plans on using its entire platform to disincentivize glassholes.

For example, YouTube will crack down on any creepy videos featuring harassment or unwanted sexualization, regardless of how they’re captured. Google Shopping and Google Ads won’t allow sales or ads for accessories like lens covers for privacy LED lights. Google Play policies will prohibit third-party apps from enabling social livestreaming, real-time facial identification, or changing anything about how the LED light functions. Payne also says third-party apps requiring camera or mic API access will need further review to prove they’re being used responsibly. This is less of a concern right now, but it’s a future-focused stance for other Android XR devices or even with devices like Xreal’s Aura glasses.

The one area Meta truly nailed with smart glasses has been style. The company, through its partnership with EssilorLuxottica, has over 100 combinations of frames and lenses. Samsung’s partnerships with Warby Parker and Gentle Monster aren’t too shabby to look at. Warby Parker has three designs and three colors: green, brown, and black. Gentle Monster has six frames — including two that are very Y2K — with bolder designs, colors, and tinted lens options. The Warby Parker collection starts at $395, while Gentle Monster starts at $449.

Style-wise, I found both collections were fetching. Warby Parker was a bit more everyday, whereas Gentle Monster felt a bit more editorial and runway-ready. Most people should be able to find a look that fits their needs.

The glasses are fairly lightweight (for smart glasses) at 41g. Battery life is estimated at around nine hours, while the cases support up to seven extra charges. Sound quality is pretty good, and the 12MP ultrawide camera takes decent photos and video with image stabilization. So, head tilters rejoice! Your accidental Dutch-angle photos will be straightened.

Where Android XR excels is that Gemini on the glasses is, so far, more capable than Meta AI on the Ray-Bans. I was able to look at an event flyer for a concert in Mumbai, have Gemini add the details to Google Calendar, and then look up flight details to India. It gave me turn-by-turn walking directions while also telling me the event schedule for the Whitney Museum. Beginning a live translation doesn’t require me to download a specific language pair onto my phone like with the Ray-Bans. I was also surprised to see Gemini occasionally correct itself in real-time when it made a mistake, or ask me to get a different view of an object I was trying to identify so it could deliver a more accurate answer.

Low-vision and blind users aren’t left out either. The glasses feature Google’s Guided Vision, which offers conversational audio descriptions of environments and surroundings. Android XR is also collaborating with Aira, which allows users to stream what they’re seeing using the camera to a real-time human interpreter. At launch, this particular feature will be for Android users only, but otherwise the glasses will work regardless of your phone.

Leaving my hands-on, it’s never been clearer that technology isn’t currently the issue with smart glasses. Whether or not this form factor takes off will boil down to culture, privacy, and usefulness. And on that front, this space has been stagnating a bit because Meta has been the sole big player setting the tone and pushing the conversation. The Android XR approach is notably different, and serious competition for Meta is excellent for consumers. All that’s left is to see whose approach wins.

You can preorder Gentle Monster and Warby Parker smart glasses starting today, and the devices are expected to ship starting December 10th.

Google really needs you to know it cares about smart glasses privacy

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Public trust in smart glasses is very important for selling lots of smart glasses.

The world has changed in the ten years since Snap first launched its camera-equipped Spectacles. Not only has the smart glasses market grown, the number of people sharing videos online has also jumped significantly. Nowadays, thanks to the rise of platforms like TikTok and Reels, anyone can be an influencer. And the possibility of innocent passersby inadvertently being included in a viral clip is far higher than it was.

It is in this climate that Google launches its intelligent eyewear devices today. This year has already seen Meta catch heat for what people called its "pervert glasses" or "creep glasses." In addition to making people feel uncomfortable about being spied on or filmed without their consent, the controversy has also discouraged existing users of those glasses from continuing to wear them, not to mention possibly putting off potential buyers.

Google has been working on this launch for years, and it can be easy to see why it doesn't want its own camera-equipped glasses to suffer the same backlash or be painted by the same brush. Perhaps that's why it's taking pains to make its privacy policies around the intelligent eyewear platform clear today. It's published a blog post outlining the privacy protections it's bringing to the platform, and they don't just revolve around preventing tampering with the LED indicator lights. Google is taking a fairly comprehensive approach that goes beyond device-specific measures, and includes using its wide sphere of influence to prevent surreptitious videos spreading online. In fact, Juston Payne, the lead for XR at Google, told Engadget that there are quite a few ways privacy protection has been considered.

Let's start with what's happening on the glasses. It's basically table stakes at this point, but they all have an LED light on them to indicate when someone is taking a picture or recording a video. The light, which sits on the left side of the frame while the camera is next to the right eye, has sensors to detect if it's covered or tampered with and will prevent the capture of pictures or video.

"Unfortunately, some of the glasses that have launched in the past launched without any kind of detection for those things, and that broke trust." Payne said. "And that's a problem," he added. He also said Google's methods are "pretty effective," though that's something that we'll have to test for ourselves to verify.

And just as there are bad actors who will continue to try to break security protections for any safe in the world or online, there will be people attempting to get around Google's anti-tampering measures here. Payne said "there will always be ways around what we build, and then we'll try to fix those holes," and that he expects there will be an ongoing back-and-forth. "But nonetheless, we're very happy with where we're starting on this one."

One of the main functions of Google's smart glasses is the ability for Gemini AI to use the camera to see what the wearer is looking at and provide more relevant information. During this process, the company says that the camera visuals won't be stored in your Gemini history. When there are faces in view, Google says "your glasses are also designed to automatically redact bystanders' faces before Gemini processes the visuals."

The camera stream also won't be used in AI training or ad targeting, and Google says any data that is sent off the glasses for processing, say in the cloud or on your phone, is encrypted for transit. It's also promising to bring the Private AI Compute processing platform to glasses, enabling these new devices to use the "hardware-secured sealed cloud environment" for greater processing.

While the camera may be the most obvious recording sensor on these devices, they also have four microphones onboard to allow you to speak to Gemini or get translations for people around you. Google uses beamforming to help focus on the individuals you're looking at when deciding whose speech to translate, and the company also said it "designed Gemini to focus on your voice by default, and not other voices around you, using directional microphones on the glasses."

Payne said that the microphones will filter out "all the world audio, as we call it, the bystander audio," It helps prevent accidental recording of people in your vicinity, but also improves Gemini's effectiveness by making sure it doesn't confuse someone else's heated argument as your query.

Of course, to make the glasses more useful, you'll have to grant it access to your apps and personal information. The intelligent eyewear can work with apps like Messages, Keep or YouTube Music, for instance, to send replies or create lists for you. Like on most phones, you'll have to grant permission to specific apps before they can access the camera on your glasses, and even after you do, you can still add or revoke access for every app.

Google also said you can view your past interactions with Gemini, delete individual chats or all of them. You'll also be able to decide if your conversations are used to train AI. And to make sure there's no confusion over whether you left the glasses on or off, and if the microphones or camera might still be tuning in, all the new eyewear have a power switch with a clear red indicator to tell you when they've been shut down.

Most intriguing are the measures Google is implementing across its entire ecosystem. The company said it has protections in place "across Google apps and services to deter misuse of the glasses," and they're pretty far-reaching.

For one thing, Google Shopping and Google Ads will "ban the sale and advertising of stickers or accessories designed to hide recording lights."

The company is also working to cull the demand for videos of a harassing or creepy nature. Payne said that one of the problems Google has seen is a small number of people "who use glasses to take creepy videos."

YouTube will "prohibit content that depicts things like harassment and unwanted sexualization. Any video uploaded to YouTube — whether it's recorded on a phone, a camera, or glasses — has to follow these policies, and videos that don't are removed."

There are also considerations for apps that request permission to use the cameras on these devices. "There's multiple approval layers if an app wants to use the camera," Payne said. "And there's actually some use cases that we're just blanket cutting off."

To that end, Google said that the Play Store and API policies will "prohibit apps from using the camera for social live-streaming or real-time facial identification, and from changing the behavior of the outer indicator light." This live-streaming restriction also applies to YouTube.

Of course, since the eyewear will be compatible with iOS devices as well, it's possible that someone finds an app of that nature in the App Store and uses it. And though Google may ban the advertisement or sale of physical goods that can be used to hide the LED indicator, people could very easily procure one on Amazon or Temu. There's more that needs to be done against this problem, and Google alone cannot cover all bases.

Sure, there's the honor system approach that most modern recording devices already use. Things like requiring an 18+ Google account to activate an intelligent eyewear device and asking users to agree to not recording others without permission or in sensitive places are in place here as well.

But the responsibility of prevention of misuse doesn't just fall to the user or Google. There needs to be greater collaboration across the industry for these measures to be effective. Payne said that Google is sharing these statements now in an attempt to set an example and send a message. "We hope that it resonates with people, and we hope that as a byproduct of that, some others in this space say, 'You know what? We should probably tighten up some of our policies.'"

Payne believes that there is time to regain the public's trust. He said that while there is momentum building in the smart glasses space, it is still early days for the category. It allows the company a chance to "reset this conversation a bit, raise the bar on privacy."

If it's able to get the attention and cooperation of other players in the space, Google stands to benefit. "We think that that will then earn a lot of trust back in the category, then we can focus a lot on how cool and valuable these are."

The pricey new Apple TV 4K is still the best streamer around

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Apple knows how to make a great streaming box to be the center of your home theater, but there are some misses with the new model.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Photography by Hunter McRae for The Verge

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

I’m sitting in front of my TV, puppy nuzzled up next to me, with four HomePods spread around my living room — two flanking my TV and one at either end of my couch — connected to the brand new Apple TV 4K. Young Anakin Skywalker hits the throttle and flies through the room. There hasn’t been an Apple surround sound system until now, and since it was announced, I was excited to try it out. And let me tell you, watching a pod race with an all-Apple audio setup is a whole lot of fun.

Surround sound across four HomePods is one of the flagship features of the fourth-gen Apple TV 4K, which is out tomorrow for $300. It’s an expensive streaming box — double and in some cases quadruple the price of competing options — that tries to earn its place in your living room with premium and convenient new features: a powerful chip, lots of storage, a trackable remote, and lots of AI. If you’re deep in the Apple ecosystem and willing to splurge, the box is a solid choice. But for everyone else, I’m not convinced the added features do enough to justify that steep new price.

Easy to use and fast interfaceSupports both Dolby Vision and HDR10+Four HomePod system sounds great

Expensive for a streamerSiri AI is inconsistent

From the outside, it’s hard to tell what’s new about the fourth-gen Apple TV 4K. Compared to the 2022 model, it’s the same size, in the same finish, with the same number of ports (in the same configuration), showing the same interface, with the same HDR support. Where things are upgraded is largely on the inside: there’s a more powerful chip — the A19, which first showed up in the iPhone 17 — and 128GB of storage by default, which was previously an upgrade. The flip side is that there is no lower-cost 64GB model anymore, which is a shame because I don’t think most people need any more than that.

The included aluminum remote is visually the same as the previous one. It charges via USB-C, has a voice-activation button on its side, and its clickpad is touch-sensitive, which can thankfully be disabled in the menu. There is one small quality of life change, though. The remote now has the Find My feature. It shows up in the Find My app on your phone, which does a great job of pinpointing the remote’s location; it can also make a sound when you can’t spot it. For a busy family like mine, this is great when one of us unintentionally brings the remote into the kitchen or bedroom (or when it’s buried in the cushions or pillows of the couch).

Siri AI is here, too, though it’s also coming to second- and third-gen Apple TV 4Ks with tvOS 27.2. The new Siri can be pretty great, when it works, especially when using personal context to understand your requests. If Apple Intelligence is enabled on your iPhone or iPad and it’s on the same network as the Apple TV 4K, the box can recognize your voice and securely connect to your device to help personalize answers to you. My friend had recently texted me about a show to watch, but I couldn’t remember what it was. When I asked Siri to find the show he had recommended, it searched my messages and opened it up on the TV (it was Pluribus).

But Siri had occasional issues with personalized answers. I asked this request a few times over my days of testing, and it didn’t always give the same answer. While it opened Pluribus on a few occasions, it also once opened The Bondsman which, to be fair, he did recommend to me… just way back in October of 2025. But even when I clarified, “No, the show he messaged about yesterday,” Siri wasn’t able to find it.

The Apple TV 4K also had trouble recognizing my voice from one moment to the next. A few minutes after asking about Pluribus, I put on an episode of Bobby Flay’s The Line. I asked Siri about his competitor Marcus Samuelsson’s restaurant Hav & Mar — where it is, what type of food it serves, if it had any accolades (I found out it was awarded two stars by The New York Times), and when it would be open during an upcoming trip to New York City. But when I changed the topic to a movie my wife and I were texting about, Siri suddenly couldn’t recognize my voice to use personal context, even though it had answered my Pluribus question minutes before. My iPhone was still on the coffee table in front of me and on the Wi-Fi network, so the connection between it and the Apple TV 4K should have been strong enough for personal context to work without issue.

Requests can be misunderstood, too. My days of testing overlapped with the F1 Brazilian Grand Prix, which is broadcast on Apple TV. When live coverage of the race started, I asked Siri, “Play the F1 Brazilian Grand Prix.” Instead of the race, it started the music track “Hymn of the Racers” by the 1995 band F1. “Play the São Paulo F1 Grand Prix” played the song “São Paulo,” a collaboration between The Weeknd and Anitta. I spent more time trying to get Siri to understand what I wanted than it would take to use the remote to start it.

When it hits, Siri AI is great. “Play Empire Strikes Back” started the Star Wars film on Disney Plus in seconds without having to mention the streaming service. Getting Pluribus to open from a question relatively scarce on details was awesome (when it happened). But as it stands right now, Siri AI is still very much a beta project and needs some work and refinement to be consistently useful. That’s not to say it’s much worse than alternatives like Alexa and Gemini, but right now it’s not doing enough to stand apart.

You can talk to Siri using the Apple TV remote, but the new model also has far-field microphones built into the box, so you can talk aloud from anywhere. One of the concerns I had when it was announced that the Apple TV 4K was getting far-field mics was whether it would even be able to hear me while in my home theater stand, but that wasn’t an issue for me. Whenever I said “Hey Siri,” I could see it activated on my TV screen. Now, my credenza has an open back for ventilation, which might have helped, but having a HomePod as part of the system also solves the issue. When one (or more) is part of the TV 4K system, the HomePod mics are listening for the “Hey Siri” requests, communicating through Bluetooth, and determining which device should respond.

Then there’s the new feature I had the most fun with: home theater surround. Apple added the ability to use two second-generation HomePods as a stereo pair (with Atmos) a couple of years ago. But as the hub for a home theater, the Apple TV 4K can now connect with four HomePods for a more immersive experience than what you can get with just your (almost certainly terrible) TV speakers.

This could be four second-gen HomePods, four HomePod mini 2 speakers, or a pair of each, as long as the two up front are the same and the two surrounds are the same. This is the setup I tested, with a pair of second-gen HomePods in front and a pair of HomePod Mini 2s in back.

Calibrating the system is easy. With all of the speakers connected to your Apple Home, each speaker emits a gentle pinging sound and a wash of pink noise that sounds like ocean waves, while a ripple of color moves across the TV screen corresponding to the color on the top of each HomePod to make sure positioning is correct. The process takes only a couple of minutes.

The speakers and the Apple TV 4K need to be a part of the same Apple Home and set up by the same user. My wife’s Apple account is the primary on our Apple Home, and even though we’re all a part of the same Apple family, when I set up the 4K TV with my account, it couldn’t see any of the speakers. This is not a major issue, but it can make setup more difficult.

As a unit, the two second-gen HomePods and two HomePod mini 2 speakers worked together really well. Any of the speakers can be tapped to pause the screen, and the volume controls affect the entire system. The big HomePods have far more bass response than expected for their size. I felt the massive Dreadnought’s cannon fire on the Rebel base in The Last Jedi through the floor. And the car explosions as The Penguin attempts to escape pursuit in The Batman felt deep and impactful. It’s not the same heart-rattling rumble a dedicated subwoofer can provide, but for a smaller space (and one with neighbors) it’s a good amount of bass. (I do hope Apple decides to make a standalone wireless subwoofer for the setup, though.)

The front HomePods create a very convincing phantom center channel, making dialog sound like it’s coming right from the screen. The entire spatial sound placement at the listening plane from the four-speaker system is excellent. The calibration sets the surrounds’ volume at a good level. There’s an option to boost or cut the volume of the surround speaker pair from 8dB to -8dB (in 0.5dB increments), but I didn’t think tweaking was necessary.

The different iconic cars in the opening race of Ready Player One zoomed around the room — as did King Kong as he chased after Parzival and Art3mis. Podracers in The Phantom Menace bumped one another and skittered off in pieces into the desert of Tatooine. The speakers deliver a great surround experience.

Atmos isn’t as convincing. There’s still some height to the sound, thanks to the larger HomePods, but it doesn’t form a full enveloping canopy. As the sandstorm envelops Paul Atreides during the rescue of the spice harvester crew in Dune, there’s an audible shimmer as the spice takes hold and whispers encircle him. As with Ready Player One and The Phantom Menace, the sand swirls around the listener, but doesn’t extend above.

I found the same with Dolby Atmos music, such as “bury a friend” by Billie Eilish. The backup vocals in the surrounds sounds nice and supportive, but there are sonic elements (like a rhythmic drill) that are supposed to fly overhead and instead sound like they move by in front of me. The music mix still sounds great, but it isn’t quite what is intended.

The addition of engaging home theater surround performance and (hopefully) consistent Siri AI integration help to differentiate the new Apple TV 4K from the old. But unfortunately so does its price. At $300, the fourth-gen box is multiple times more expensive than its competitors, many of which are capable streamers, if a bit slower. One of the reasons to pay a premium for the Apple TV 4K in the past is that Apple has one of the best privacy policies on the market. Another is that the Apple TV 4K doesn’t serve up the same level of ads the other streaming devices or built-in TV OSes do (although it still tells me to watch Ted Lasso and Widow’s Bay regularly). Those are compelling reasons to pay more for a streaming box if you can swing it, but this is a major increase in price that doesn’t add features that home theater enthusiasts have been asking for. Adding things like audio passthrough and 120Hz support would set the Apple TV 4K apart from everyone else in capabilities and really validate the bump in cost.

And the expense extends to the home theater system. The entire price of the system I used — two second-gen HomePods, two HomePod mini 2s, and the new Apple TV 4K — costs $1,300. If you already have a couple HomePods in your home and want to repurpose them for your home theater (or you’re already using a stereo pair as your TV speakers), adding in the mini 2 speakers and the new 4K TV is a viable prospect. But if you’re looking to start fresh with a surround system and don’t mind the aesthetics of a soundbar, the Sonos Beam Ultra paired with two Era 100 speakers is an excellent alternative, both for its flexibility of adding more speakers and its superior handling of Atmos content. Plus, you can add a subwoofer later and use a cheaper third-gen Apple TV 4K instead of the new one.

There’s no doubt that Apple still makes one of the best streaming devices available. It’s fast, has a great user experience, and the additions of Find My on the remote, far-field mics, and the home theater surround capability add good value (especially if you already have some newer HomePods).

But the new Apple TV 4K is $300. And Siri AI isn’t there yet to consistently make it worth using. Right now, the Apple TV 4K is an excellent, premium-priced streaming device that doesn’t set itself apart enough from its previous model. And with every other streaming device a fraction of the Apple TV 4K’s price, most people would be well served by a cheaper Google Streamer, Fire TV stick, or Roku stick. Yes, the Apple TV 4K is better than all of them. But as it currently stands, especially with Siri AI still in beta, I don’t think it’s twice as better.

NHS England deletes 137-trust Palantir benefits claim, Guardian reports

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

The list vanished after 29 of the trusts on it said they barely use the system, just as ministers weigh extending the £330M deal.

NHS England has quietly deleted the webpage listing 137 hospital trusts as “live” with and “realising benefits” from Palantir’s federated data platform, the Guardian reported.

The deletion follows the Guardian’s investigation last week, built on freedom of information requests the campaign group Medact sent to every English hospital trust, which found 29 trusts on the list were not using all or most of the system.

Some said it was not live at all; one said it never would be. In the list’s place now sits a vaguer page saying early users see “real results”.

Ministers are weighing whether to extend Palantir’s £330M contract to 2029, and the Office for Statistics Regulation is examining NHS England’s claims about how widely the platform is used. The statistics watchdog has already warned there was no evidence for the claim that the technology helped deliver 117,000 extra operations.

“When NHS England makes claims like this without checking them, it destroys trust,” Labour MP Ben Coleman, a member of the Commons health committee, told the Guardian.

Also, he warned the overclaim could put more hospitals off the platform and threaten its viability. He wants the contract scrapped in favour of an open platform British companies can build on.

The pressure is converging from several directions. Doctors Association UK, Medact, Amnesty UK, and the patients’ group Just Treatment wrote to prime minister Andy Burnham and health secretary Yvette Cooper this week opposing the reported two-year extension, arguing the deal could be safely wound down in 2027 using the contract’s termination assistance period.

The health union Unison wants it ended outright: its general secretary, Andrea Egan, told the Guardian that Palantir is a “sinister American surveillance firm”.

Palantir, which says ending the contract would be “putting politics above patients”, is lobbying hard for the extension. The company hired Labour peer Tom Watson as a full-time lobbyist in September, and he recently wrote to Cooper pitching Palantir as a builder of Britain’s sovereign technological capability, an interesting framing for a company whose presence has already drawn 44,000 objections from patients under UK GDPR.

NHS England did not respond to the Guardian’s requests for comment.

The disappearing list may say enough on its own: the strongest public evidence for the platform’s success was a page its own subjects would not vouch for.

JULES BREACH: Brighton's brilliant start has fans believing of silverware and challenging for the Champions League

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The TNT Sports presenter and Brighton fan on why the Seagulls should dare to dream

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

This season, I think I’ve lost count of the amount of times someone has come up to me and said, “Brighton are in the title race, aren’t they?” Whether they’ve been joking or not, the fact they’ve included the club in the conversation is bonkers. We’ve enjoyed the adulation – it was deserved after an impressive run of results to start the season.

Brighton have begun the campaign as entertainers. Fabian Hurzeler has them free-scoring, upsetting the Premier League champions and playing an attacking brand of football that’s fun to watch.

Most Albion fans have been realistic that mounting a title challenge is one step too far. What does feel possible though, and it still feels crazy that I’m even writing this, is that there’s probably never been a better opportunity to challenge for a Champions League place – madness for a club that only experienced European football for the first time in its history in 2023.

On top of the Seagulls’ great start, some of the usual contenders for the Champions League places didn’t really get going early on. Manchester United and Spurs both struggled, Liverpool and Chelsea were still settling under new managers, and Manchester City have been at risk of being hit with sanctions this term. If ever there was a season where a disruptor could break into those qualification spots, it’s this one. Brighton will be one of the favourites to do it.

I’ll be honest and admit that before a ball was even kicked, I was nervous about this season. As fans, we’re used to losing key players and coping pretty well, but to lose the spine of Jan Paul van Hecke, Carlos Baleba and Danny Welbeck all in the same window was a big blow. Joao Pedro was sold the summer before, and losing both the Brazilian and Welbeck in successive summers without an experienced replacement felt risky.

Scoring goals was a huge concern, but I’ve been proven completely wrong. Again. The club have shown once more that they’re not just one step ahead of everyone else, but two. They had their best ever start to a top-flight season, and over the first seven league games of the campaign, only Barcelona and Bayern Munich had scored more among Europe’s top five leagues.

Brighton consistently find hidden gems. It’s a testament to their recruitment policy and they appear to have done it again with a number of players. The club don’t mind marketing themselves as one that helps a player progress and allows them to leave if a big offer arises, and their culture provides young talent a platform to thrive without too much pressure.

The best features, fun and footballing quizzes, straight to your inbox every week.

Greek teenage striker Charalampos Kostoulas looks like one of the best young players in the league and is key to Hurzeler’s aggressive press. Also just 19 years old, no-one expected Luka Vuskovic to replace Van Hecke so well. They are two young players who are flourishing in a team that also has plenty of experience in captain Lewis Dunk and Pascal Gross to give it the right balance.

Brighton’s stand-out performer has been 35-year-old Gross, who returned to the club in a genius January move after a season-and-a-half at Borussia Dortmund. His workrate, and goal and assist output had already given him legendary status at the Amex, but he’s been on another level and is clearly enjoying his football more than ever.

There have already been a handful of memorable results, including the comeback win at Old Trafford, a 3-0 victory at home to Arsenal and a 5-0 thrashing of rivals Crystal Palace, which included that halfway line goal from Diego Gomez. It will go down as one of the all-time great Premier League strikes and summed up the confidence Brighton are playing with.

The challenge now is to compete consistently and stay in this crazy conversation as long as possible. As fans, we always expect a bad run just around the corner, but this season could be special. The club are Conference League favourites, and supporters are desperate to see a first major trophy.

Football is there to make us believe and Brighton fans will continue to dream after the start we’ve seen. There may never be a better opportunity to break into the Champions League than now, but lifting that first piece of silverware surely has to be a priority.

Jules Breach is a broadcast journalist who has lead presenting roles for TNT Sport, Channel 4, ITV, and Premier League Productions. Jules hosted BT Sport’s live flagship football show Score and also presents live Premier League, Europa League and Champions League matches. Jules is the lead presenter for Channel 4’s coverage of the England games and also hosts ITV’s EFL Highlights show. Jules presents worldwide coverage for Premier League Productions and away from work is a passionate Brighton fan.

Jensen Huang resigns as Nvidia CEO, exits board; shares plunge 13%

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Colette Kress will remain Nvidia’s chief financial officer while serving as interim CEO as the board searches for a permanent successor.

Nvidia co-founder Jensen Huang resigned as president and CEO and stepped down from the board on Thursday, effective immediately. The board named Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress interim CEO.

Kress will retain her role as CFO while Nvidia’s board considers internal and external candidates for a permanent CEO. The board also appointed Harvey C. Jones its first chair and said it expects Nvidia’s operations to proceed without interruption.

Nvidia shares fell more than 13% at the US market open on Thursday, trading at $151.00 as of 13:31 UTC, according to Yahoo Finance.

Kress has served as Nvidia’s CFO since September 2013 and previously held finance positions at Cisco and Microsoft. In Thursday’s announcement, newly appointed board chair Jones said the company expects its operations and product roadmap to continue without interruption.

Nvidia is scheduled to report its fiscal 2027 third-quarter financial results on Nov. 17.

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resigns in the wake of multiple affairs, Chinese spy allegations

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Nvidia’s board of directors has confirmed today that Jensen Huang has resigned as the company's CEO. The news follows revelations that Huang had been involved in not one but two extramarital affairs, one with a suspected Chinese intelligence asset, and another with his direct report, Nvidia’s VP of health care, Kimberly Powell.

"NVIDIA (NASDAQ: NVDA) Board of Directors announced today that Jensen Huang has resigned as President, Chief Executive Officer, and a member of the Board, effective immediately," the board stated. "Colette Kress, current Executive Vice President and Chief Financial Officer of NVIDIA, has agreed to serve as Interim Chief Executive Officer. Kress will continue to serve as Executive Vice President and Chief Financial Officer while serving as Interim Chief Executive Officer until a permanent successor has been named. The Board is initiating a search for a permanent Chief Executive Officer and will consider internal and external candidates."

In recent weeks, stories have come to light alleging that Huang was involved in an overly familiar relationship with Megaspeed executive Alice Huang, whom U.S. intelligence agencies suspect may be a Chinese intelligence asset. It was reported that the AI GPU giant’s CEO may have been the target of a spy plot — Megaspeed is under investigation for its involvement in the smuggling of $2 billion worth of export-controlled AI GPUs into China.

Nvidia shares plunged more than 10 percent in early market trading following the news, and are now down more than 23% in the last month.

Over the weekend, Jensen Huang admitted that he had been unfaithful to his wife and issued a company-wide apology. Separately, Nvidia’s Board of Directors launched an independent investigation into reports that Huang’s entanglement may be a matter of national security, given his role in the global AI revolution.

Colette Kress has been Nvidia's Executive Vice President and CFO since September 2013; prior to that, she spent 25 years at Cisco and Microsoft, among others.

Just a couple of days later, a second bombshell report emerged claiming that Jensen Huang was also romantically involved with Powell, one of his direct reports at the company. Reports indicate the affair was known within the company, and that the two were even filmed being affectionate in a San Jose hotel at Nvidia GTC. Security allegedly sequestered a bystander who filmed the interaction on camera and made the person an offer to sign a nondisclosure agreement.

Nvidia also announced that Harvey C. Jones will serve as the company's first-ever Chair of the Board; he has previously served as CEO and Executive Chairman of Synopsys.
